\title{Mortgage Lock-In and the Federal Reserve's Quantitative Tightening Shortfall}
\author{Eugene Ong}
\affil{Carnegie Mellon University}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
\noindent U.S. mortgages are built for a liquid secondary market: fixed rate, repaid at face value, not portable. When rates rise, that combination locks households into old, cheap loans. I measure what it cost the Federal Reserve as it shrank its balance sheet, when repayments on its mortgage bonds ran \$764.7 billion below the monthly ceilings set for June 2022 through November 2025. It cost less than that gap suggests, and most of the gap was never about behavior. Switch the lock-in response off and the model still accounts for 85.7\% of it, because scheduled amortization and baseline involuntary turnover fall short of a \$35 billion monthly ceiling however households react to rates. A loan-by-loan model of when mortgages are repaid, run on Freddie Mac data, accounts for 91.3\%. What lock-in itself adds is a range rather than a number. The design pins it between $+2.8$ and $+8.7$ points under its production floor form (a wild-cluster interval on the floor read's 31 clusters; the narrower percentile read, $+3.0$ to $+8.0$, under-covers), with a form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points, and it identifies levels only: no claim about month-to-month timing survives its own placebo tests. Inside that range, $+5.6$ points, or \$42.6 billion, is the value at the calibration I headline, and every correction I can measure to the involuntary-turnover floor or to the accounting basis moves it down within the range rather than up. An agent-based model of the household moving decision recovers 13.6\% averaged across seeds. A cross-design variant recalibrated on real loan covariates recovers 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds (76.3\% reweighted to the book's composition), and the contrast cannot be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm. The Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection anticipated the large majority of the realized shortfall. Measured against that projection rather than the never-binding cap, lock-in accounts for roughly a quarter to a half of the genuine surprise, depending on a disclosed choice about how to spread the 2022 figures across months. Both readings are upper limits. The cost to households who could not move is real. The institutional cash-flow cost is small, on an accounting that counts repaid principal at face value.
\end{abstract}

\vspace{0.5\baselineskip}
{\footnotesize\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} mortgage prepayment; lock-in effect; quantitative tightening; mortgage-backed securities; agent-based models; hazard models.\\
\textbf{JEL codes:} E52, E58, G21, R31.\par}
\newpage
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

For most of its history, the Federal Reserve operated under a ``Treasuries-only'' framework, using sovereign debt with fixed, predictable maturities. During the 2008 Global Financial Crisis, and again during the 2020 COVID-19 pandemic, the central bank departed from this framework, launching aggressive Quantitative Easing (QE) programs that purchased trillions of dollars in Agency MBS to stimulate the consumer economy directly.

This intervention suppressed 30-year mortgage rates to historic lows of roughly 2.6\%, triggering a large refinance boom. As inflation surged in 2022, the Federal Reserve attempted to reverse this stimulus through Quantitative Tightening (QT), establishing a passive roll-off target intended to drain up to \$35 billion per month from its SOMA portfolio as homeowners relocated naturally, a cap phased in at \$17.5 billion per month and stepped up to \$35 billion three months later.

That the lock-in effect slowed the runoff of the Federal Reserve's mortgage portfolio is not a new claim. The Federal Reserve's own staff and officials have said as much (\citealp{na2024}; \citealp{perli2024}; \citealp{hammack2025}). This paper's contribution is not that mechanism but its decomposition. That decomposition is an accounting exercise within a fully disclosed model rather than a causal identification: switching the lock-in elasticity off partitions the realized shortfall between an elastic channel and a mechanical baseline, and the elasticity's causal content is inherited from the external estimates that calibrate it, not established here (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). I ask how much of the runoff shortfall the lock-in elasticity accounts for, as opposed to the scheduled amortization and baseline involuntary turnover that would have undershot a \$35 billion monthly cap regardless of any household response to rates. Most of the shortfall is mechanical. A loan-level model with the lock-in elasticity switched off still recovers 85.7\% of the \$764.7 billion shortfall-against-cap benchmark constructed in Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} at the calibration this paper headlines, and 88.7\% at its in-sample calibration point, measured on the paper's benchmark-consistent accounting basis (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}). The Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection independently implies the same composition. The New York Fed's May 2022 staff baseline already anticipated the large majority of the realized cap-shortfall: 75.6--88.5\%, depending on a disclosed intra-2022 allocation choice (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}), a range wide enough that its overlap with the mechanical null's recovery carries no precision. The elasticity's identified footprint is therefore a bounded margin, not a pinned magnitude and not a sign. The floor that dominates the level is calibrated in-window on the same locked-in cohorts the margin decomposes, so I re-measure it outside that window, on pre-episode 2017--2019 discount cohorts, and re-run both legs at the result: the margin runs $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across the off-window floor's defensible range, and $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points once that floor's own sampling error is propagated with a wild-cluster small-sample correction, which is the binding layer (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The value at the mid-grid anchor inside that interval is $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion), and it is the range and not that value that the design delivers: every correction I can measure to the floor or to the accounting basis moves the value down within the interval rather than up, so it should be read as an upper-middle member of the range rather than its center (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The in-window production calibration returns $+9.2$ points (sampling 95\% confidence interval $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points from a 200-replicate paired loan-level bootstrap; sampling and seed noise are negligible at reporting precision, so the operative uncertainty is calibration, Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}), reported here as the in-sample calibration point rather than the headline. Neither figure is the entire shortfall (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A pre-committed sweep of the two calibration inputs that dominate the level, the involuntary-turnover floor and the elasticity band, bounds the margin between $+2.1$ and $+13.2$ points, positive at every point of the calibration box and at every off-window anchor tested, though that positivity checks the specification rather than identifying the mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A functional-form test that replaces the floor's hard maximum with a competing-risks combination leaves the margin inside that box and nearly floor-invariant (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). (``Pre-committed,'' here and throughout, means the grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds were committed to the repository before any run executed; I reserve ``pre-registered'' for third-party registries, which none of this is.) I reach this through two structurally distinct estimators, a 10,000-household agent-based model and a loan-level hazard framework, that disagree sharply about how much household choice explains. The decomposition the hazard framework supports is the paper's central empirical result; the disagreement between the two estimators I read as a supporting diagnostic rather than a clean paradigm contrast, since a real-covariate variant of the ABM recovers far more (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}).

This result sits inside a two-sided exchange: the pool homogeneity that generates To-Be-Announced (TBA) market liquidity is purchased at the price of household mobility, because the standardized contracts that TBA delivery presupposes carry the ``par-payoff'' obligation, under which a homeowner must extinguish an existing mortgage at full face value to relocate. The obligation itself is a property of the U.S. fixed-rate note enforced by the due-on-sale clause (made broadly enforceable by the Garn--St.~Germain Act of 1982) rather than of TBA eligibility, and it binds on portfolio and non-agency loans alike; the FHA/VA loans backing Ginnie Mae pools (20.4\% of the SOMA book) are assumable by statute, a carve-out from the book-wide mobility cost that Section~\ref{sec:pathb} scores by observed speeds. A third design objective, extension-protected institutional cash flow, is often framed as completing a trilemma; Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} presents my evidence that this third leg does not trade off sharply against mobility, so the operative exchange is the two-sided one. The decomposition above disciplines this frame rather than dissolving it: household mobility is constrained, as the household-level lock-in literature documents, while the cash-flow shortfall is governed mostly by loan-level and institutional mechanics, so the trade-off's binding cost is denominated in mobility rather than in institutional cash flow (Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}). Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s Danish counterfactual initially implied a \$925.5 billion institutional gap. Recalibrating against separately estimated Danish elasticities (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) reduces this by an order of magnitude, to $+\$61.2$ billion (8\% of the benchmark) under the shared-accounting hybrid pipeline when the transplant changes only the payoff rule (the Danish moving channel's estimated flatness anchored at the U.S. zero-gap intercept, involuntary turnover floor respected). What that construction can and cannot demonstrate is set by the anchor: a Danish leg pinned at the zero-gap U.S. hazard must prepay faster than a U.S. book that carries a negative rate gap almost everywhere, so a positive gap---and its survival of the refinance-in-place sweep---is forced rather than found, and the informative content is the magnitude. The bracketing anchor that also imports Denmark's baseline mobility flips the sign, to $-\$99.9$ billion---but that leg runs its Danish households at 3.39\% annual prepayment, below the 4\% involuntary-turnover floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side, which is why I bracket with it rather than headline it. I treat the corrected magnitude as informative about where the trade-off actually binds, and the sign as anchor-determined---and, on the cash-flow side, incidence-determined as well: pricing the market-value buyback credit under a cash-haircut reading reverses the gap to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion at the committed proxy discounts, so the institutional relief is established under face accounting only (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

Unlike standard U.S. Treasuries, U.S. mortgages carry asymmetric, state-dependent payoff profiles. As the Federal Reserve's policy tightening pushed the 30-year fixed mortgage rate past 7.0\% (a rise decomposable into the 10-year Treasury yield's climb and a widening of the primary-mortgage-to-10-year spread from roughly 170 to over 300 bp at its 2022--2023 peak, FRED series MORTGAGE30US less DGS10; the decomposition of that spread into MBS supply, prepayment-risk repricing, and convexity-hedging components follows \citet{boyarchenko2019}, whose framework predates and does not document the 2022--2023 magnitudes), the par-payoff rule imposed a substantial financial penalty on relocation, producing a ``lock-in effect'' that suppressed prepayment activity across the U.S. housing market. Because mortgages did not prepay, principal cash flows from the Federal Reserve's MBS portfolio ran far below the redemption caps, extending the portfolio's effective maturity. Cumulative runoff fell \$764.7 billion short of the phased caps over the tightening cycle (methodology and interpretation in Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). How much of that shortfall the lock-in channel itself accounts for is the question Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} decompose.

Standard macroeconomic models often underestimate this extension risk because they assume frictionless, rational household behavior. In practice, the lock-in effect was compounded by historic housing inventory shortages and depressed consumer sentiment.

This paper quantifies the mechanics of this shortfall using two structurally distinct estimators. The first is a 10,000-household Agent-Based Model parameterized with dynamic macroeconomic frictions, used to estimate stranded cash flows and to decompose that estimate against the empirical benchmark. The second, introduced after the ABM's residual proved persistent and unresponsive to additional behavioral realism, is a reduced-form hazard framework estimated on loan-level survival data that abandons the household utility function altogether. As Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} show, synthetic-population household choice explains only a small share of the shortfall (13.6\% on the fifty-seed mean, 10--17\% across seed noise, 11.9\% on the frozen production draw), while the loan-level hazard framework recovers the large majority of it at the aggregate level: 91.3\% in its literature-calibrated microsimulation form on the benchmark-consistent shared accounting basis at the off-window floor this paper headlines, 88.2--93.7\% across that floor's defensible range, and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point (92.2\%--99.2\% across covariate calibrations at that in-sample point), and 100.0\% (approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% across those calibrations) once the sample's coupon mix is reweighted to the SOMA book under the ex-ante priors. Its estimated cohort-hazard form (Path A) lands at 112.4\% but is excluded from headline ranges and does not corroborate the result: its forward simulation carries no useful propagated precision, and no inferential object has been recomputed under its production specification (Section~\ref{sec:patha}). Two qualifiers travel with the two-estimator contrast wherever I state it: the null that switches off the lock-in elasticity (the $\beta_1 = 0$ null) already recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark at the off-window floor and 88.7\% at the in-window production calibration, so the hazard framework's margin over a mechanical model is $+5.6$ points at the off-window floor ($+9.2$ at the in-window production calibration), not the level (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). A cross-design variant of the ABM recalibrated on real loan covariates recovers 59.3\% on the frozen seed, 60.2\% across fifty seeds with a 95\% band of 55.1--66.0\%, and 76.3\% at the book's composition (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), so the contrast is not clean evidence about modeling paradigm alone. The hazard framework is the instrument; the decomposition it yields is the paper's central empirical result. (Section~\ref{sec:robustness} shows, on the production specification, that population size does not drive this gap.) A counterfactual simulation based on the Danish market-value repurchase regime further shows that the institutional cash-flow component of systemic duration risk is a consequence of U.S. institutional design: under the rule-only transplant it matches the lock-in marginal in origin, and in size at the in-sample calibration point on which both were run.

\paragraph{Definitions used throughout.} Four accounting bases attach to every recovery percentage (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}): the \emph{standalone-scorer} basis (no adjustment), the \emph{benchmark-consistent shared} basis (nets a common \$69.6 billion curtailment flow identically from every U.S. leg; the main-text convention), the \emph{full-book} basis (reweights to the SOMA book's coupon mix), and the \emph{composed} basis (both). Two floor calibrations attach to every marginal and level: the \emph{in-sample calibration point} (the 4\% involuntary-turnover floor measured in-window on the locked-in 2023--24 cohorts; returns $+9.2$ points) and the \emph{off-window calibration} (the 4.70--5.33\% floor measured on pre-episode 2018 discount cohorts; returns $+5.6$ points and is the headline). ``Trapped liquidity'' and ``shortfall'' denote the net shortfall of realized roll-off against the phased redemption caps (\$764.7 billion over June 2022--November 2025), and the \emph{lock-in marginal} is the difference between the central simulation and its $\beta_1 = 0$ mechanical null, the paper's identified object, whose operative uncertainty is the calibration-and-form envelope of Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} rather than any sampling interval. That envelope is a grid, not a confidence region, and the distinction is load-bearing: the elasticity band it sweeps is \citeauthor{liebersohn2024}'s \emph{specification} range with my adopted midpoint at its centre, not a published point estimate with a published standard error, and no sampling error of theirs is propagated anywhere in this paper. So the envelope has no coverage property. Where a genuine sampling interval exists---the floor reads' cluster bootstrap, and the loan- and stratum-level bootstraps of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}---I quote it as such and rank it against the envelope explicitly rather than merging the two. One global property of the design should be stated here as well as in the limitations, because every number below inherits it: \emph{no outcome-holdout months exist anywhere in this paper}, without exception. The involuntary floor that sets Path~B's level is calibrated on in-window turnover, Path~A trains on nineteen of the forty-two QT months, and Path~B's level is evaluated on the window that sets it. Each of these is disclosed again where it arises, and the nineteen-month floor variant reported later is an input-stability check rather than an outcome holdout. Every recovery percentage in this paper is accordingly an in-sample or calibrated quantity in the time dimension, and should be read as a description of fit rather than as a prediction (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).

\section{Literature Review}\label{sec:lit}

The theoretical foundation of mortgage prepayment is the assumption of ``ruthless exercise,'' wherein borrowers refinance instantaneously as market conditions dictate \citep{dunn1981}. Subsequent literature recognized behavioral frictions: \citet{schwartz1989} estimate an empirical prepayment function from aggregate GNMA experience with maximum-likelihood hazard techniques and embed it in a valuation framework (the econometric ancestor of the loan-level survival approach I adopt in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}), while \citet{richard1989} formalize the industry practice of modeling prepayment as an empirical function of the refinancing incentive, seasoning, seasonality, and a pool's own prepayment history. \citet{stanton1995} introduced heterogeneous transaction costs, smoothing the instantaneous refinancing cliff into a predictable S-curve, and \citet{agarwal2013} give the rational benchmark its sharpest form, a closed-form optimal refinancing threshold against which observed behavior can be scored. \citet{chernov2018} recover the prepayment function implied by MBS market prices and find it driven by a macroeconomic turnover component alongside the rate response---the same turnover-versus-rate-response split this paper's mechanical null operationalizes on the cash-flow side. \citet{quigley1987,quigley2002} established the lock-in effect as a persistent friction limiting mobility during rising-rate environments. \citet{ferreira2010} provided early large-sample evidence for it, finding that rising mortgage rates and negative home equity each depress owner mobility---the latter an equity-lock-in channel distinct from, and complementary to, the rate channel this paper models (the equity dimension is left unmodeled here, a boundary disclosed in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The 2022--2025 rate shock generated substantial empirical literature documenting the breakdown of standard prepayment assumptions. \citet{fonseca2024} documented the steep decline of U.S. mobility and refinancing activity, while \citet{batzer2024} estimated that U.S. households would have faced roughly \$2.4 trillion in foregone capital gains had they chosen to relocate. \citet{liebersohn2024} quantify the same lock-in mechanism in hazard form, estimating that each percentage point of prevailing rate above a borrower's locked-in coupon reduces quarterly mobility probability by 5.5\% to 7.7\% depending on specification: the elasticity band my Path B microsimulation adopts as its calibration; \citet{graybill2026} independently estimate the same margin from transaction records in a housing-market-equilibrium setting, with deep rate gaps cutting the sale hazard by roughly 7--11\% per percentage point, a corroboration of that band from data that never touch credit records (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

A related strand of literature decomposes how much of the observed mobility decline a rational, rate-gap-driven framework can explain, leaving the remainder as an unexplained residual. \citet{aladangady2024} construct a counterfactual rate-gap distribution holding the 2021 rate environment fixed and find that rate-gap-driven lock-in explains only 44\% of the actual decline in mortgage borrower mobility between 2021 and 2022, after correcting for selection bias from forgone refinancing opportunities. This decomposition approach is methodologically analogous to the one I take in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}, though I apply it to dynamically modeled CPR and cash-flow shortfalls rather than to mobility rates directly, and use two structurally distinct estimators rather than one. \citet{gerardi2024} similarly compare a structural search-and-matching model of lock-in against empirical lifecycle migration patterns, finding that their model accounts for most, but not all, of the observed disruption to housing market liquidity.

A separate behavioral finance literature provides theoretical grounding for what such residuals likely represent. \citet{keys2016} document that more than 20\% of U.S. borrowers fail to refinance even when doing so would be unambiguously in their financial interest, attributing this to procrastination and variation in financial sophistication. \citet{defusco2020} document a structural counterpart to these behavioral frictions, showing that employment-documentation requirements and out-of-pocket closing costs bind most severely in recessions and inhibit refinancing pass-through. \citet{andersen2020} reach a similar conclusion using Danish administrative data, finding that inattention and inertia, rather than transaction costs, account for a substantial share of household failure to act on refinancing opportunities. \citet{andersen2022} further show, using Danish housing data, that households exhibit reference dependence and loss aversion relative to their original purchase price, weighting losses roughly 2.5 times more heavily than equivalent gains. \citet{johnson2019} examine sluggish refinancing using administrative, survey, and field-experimental data, finding that among the behavioral factors they test, suspicion of lenders' motives (rather than standard rational frictions such as time preference or risk aversion) is most consistently related to households' failure to accept profitable refinancing offers, further supporting a behavioral interpretation of the residual. \citet{bissiri2014} formalize this distinction directly, defining ``behavioral risk'' as the share of prepayment decisions not driven by a fully rational financial criterion, an operationalization more recently applied by \citet{perotti2024} to model behavioral uncertainty as a stochastic overlay on an otherwise rational prepayment framework and to derive robust hedging strategies that bound, rather than eliminate, the exposure such uncertainty creates.

A further strand treats prepayment as a loan-level survival process shaped by pool composition and servicer behavior rather than by borrower optimization. Prepayment ``burnout'' (the tendency for a pool's aggregate refinancing sensitivity to decline over time as its most rate-sensitive borrowers prepay earlier and self-select out of the remaining pool) is a standard feature of industry MBS valuation models (one of the four factors of \citepos{richard1989} canonical prepayment function, alongside the Public Securities Association seasoning convention), but is difficult to reconcile with a fixed, representative-agent population. Recent theory formalizes why: in a heterogeneous pool the observed pool-level hazard is the survival-weighted mean of the individual hazards, and its drift carries a negative selection term proportional to the cross-sectional dispersion of those hazards \citep{lesniewski2026}, so pool speeds decline with seasoning even when no individual hazard changes. My hazard framework (Section~\ref{sec:hazard}) is designed to accommodate this loan-level survival structure.

Fixed-income literature separately highlights the systemic risks generated by the lock-in effect. \citet{jiang2023} documented that duration extension in bank asset portfolios, prominently including low-coupon MBS, generated roughly \$2 trillion in unrecognized mark-to-market losses across the U.S. banking sector, contributing to self-fulfilling solvency runs including the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank. \citet{smith2023} demonstrated that QT relies heavily on predictable MBS runoff schedules, which are structurally broken by these duration extensions.

Closest to this paper, the Federal Reserve's own staff and officials have already described the mechanism and measured its first-order footprint. \citet{na2024} report that ``a vast majority of the agency MBS held in the SOMA are far out-of-the-money to refinance and may be subject to the `lock-in' effect,'' and that because ``monthly principal payments have been below the \$35 billion-per-month redemption cap \ldots the size of the SOMA agency MBS portfolio has decreased at a much slower pace than in the case of a binding cap''; they put June 2022 to June 2024 runoff at roughly \$450 billion against the \$820 billion a binding cap would have retired, an implied shortfall near \$370 billion. The same note reports the composition of those below-cap payments: scheduled principal accounts for roughly half, with prepayments mostly due to turnover making up the remainder, an external, staff-measured anchor for the scheduled-plus-turnover decomposition my $\beta_1 = 0$ null quantifies. That anchor and my null have different denominators: Na et al.'s composition describes realized principal \emph{payments} (roughly half scheduled, half turnover-driven prepayment, and by construction the null contains exactly these two components plus amortization mechanics), while my 88.7\% (in-sample; 85.7\% at the off-window floor) describes the null's share of the shortfall \emph{against the caps}; a payments mix of scheduled-plus-turnover running below \$35 billion in essentially every month is the state in which the shortfall accrues mechanically, so their composition and my null recovery are the same fact expressed against the two denominators. \citet{perli2024}, then Manager of the System Open Market Account, stated that the cap ``has not been binding \ldots resulting in very slow prepayments,'' and \citet{hammack2025} observed that some of the low-coupon holdings ``could be very long lived.'' New York Fed staff had projected the below-cap regime from its first month: a September 2022 staff post put monthly agency MBS principal payments at \$20 to \$30 billion over the following months, below the \$35 billion cap that took effect that month \citep{nyfed2022}. I treat the qualitative mechanism as established by these sources rather than as my own finding. Two features separate my exercise from theirs. First, this characterization attributes the below-cap runoff to lock-in without isolating the elasticity's contribution from the scheduled amortization and baseline involuntary turnover that would have undershot the cap under any prepayment behavior; Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} supply that decomposition and find the identified lock-in margin is bounded between $+3.0$ and $+8.0$ points of the benchmark at an involuntary floor measured off the locked-in population, $+5.6$ percentage points at the mid-grid anchor inside that interval ($+9.2$ at the in-window production calibration), and qualified in Section~\ref{sec:identification}. Second, I extend the same shortfall-against-cap construction from the \$450 billion-through-June-2024 quantity to the full June 2022 to November 2025 window, where it reaches \$764.7 billion (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). A more recent \citet{eyal2026} FEDS Note also decomposes the Federal Reserve's balance-sheet reduction, but into macroeconomic drivers (inflation, real GDP growth, and active SOMA reductions relative to nominal GDP) rather than into the loan-level sources of the MBS runoff shortfall itself, so it does not speak to the lock-in-versus-mechanical question I take up here.

The Danish mortgage securitization system offers a tested structural alternative to negative convexity. \citet{berg2018} outlined the Danish ``Balance Principle,'' which requires strict one-to-one match funding. \citet[\S3.2.3]{berger2026} estimate that under these rules household mobility is close to flat in the coupon gap, because borrowers hold the right to repurchase their outstanding mortgages at market value, neutralizing extension risk for the bondholder. That flatness is a slope result on Danish data, and Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} is careful about what it licenses: this paper imports the slope and anchors the level at the U.S. hazard, since Berger et al.\ estimate no moving level for U.S. households under a Danish payoff rule.

A final strand situates the lock-in effect within the broader theory of monetary policy transmission. \citet{berger2021} demonstrate that the Federal Reserve's ability to stimulate the economy through rate cuts is path-dependent, because prepayment behavior depends on the outstanding distribution of in-the-money refinancing incentives. This paper extends that logic to the tightening direction: the same path dependence that weakens easing after a low-rate period also weakens tightening after a low-rate period, since the resulting stock of deeply in-the-money, rate-locked mortgages cannot prepay regardless of how aggressively the central bank subsequently raises rates. \citet{eichenbaum2022} formalize this state dependence for the easing direction (the potency of monetary policy depends on the distribution of outstanding rate gaps). \citet{beraja2019} provide complementary regional evidence that the strength of the refinancing channel varies systematically with the local distribution of in-the-money mortgages, and \citet{dimaggio2020} supply direct evidence for the easing-direction channel this paper mirrors, showing that the Federal Reserve's QE1 agency-MBS purchases transmitted to the real economy specifically by inducing refinancing. This QT setting also connects the paper to the broader literature on how central-bank balance-sheet operations transmit through the supply of long-duration assets: \citet{krishnamurthy2011} document that quantitative easing operates through several distinct channels, including one specific to agency MBS that runs through prepayment, and \citet{vayanos2021} formalize, under preferred-habitat demand, why shifts in the outstanding maturity and supply of held bonds move the term structure at all. Lock-in is thus an input to the balance-sheet-supply channel these papers describe, not a phenomenon separate from it. \citet{du2024} assemble the cross-country QT record---seven central banks, active and passive runoff---and find announcement effects concentrated in yields; the MBS runoff shortfall this paper decomposes is the passive-runoff margin of that record, measured at the portfolio cash-flow level. \citet{vickery2013} document that the liquidity premium of the TBA market derives specifically from the homogeneity of deliverable pools, finding that TBA-eligible securities trade at significantly lower cost than otherwise similar specified pools; this homogeneity requirement is the institutional constraint that, in Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}, I argue makes the Danish market-value repurchase structure difficult to reconcile with the current U.S. secondary market absent a broader redesign of agency securitization rules.

My two estimators sit at the transparent end of two active methodological literatures. On the survival side, a growing body of work fits prepayment and default hazards with nonparametric and machine-learning methods, including random survival forests \citep{ishwaran2008}, competing-risks Bayesian additive regression trees \citep{sparapani2020}, and deep neural-network mortgage hazard models \citep{sadhwani2021}. The last of these is the benchmark for what flexibility buys at scale: fitted to origination and monthly performance records for over 120 million U.S. mortgages, the deep network of \citet{sadhwani2021} learns highly nonlinear, interaction-rich prepayment and default responses, including the burnout-type history dependence that Path~A carries as an explicit stratum-level regressor and the ABM probes by pre-committed falsification (Sections~\ref{sec:patha} and~\ref{sec:abm-burnout}), without hand-specified functional form. I instead estimate a fixed-effects Poisson hazard and a literature-calibrated microsimulation whose functional forms and calibration inputs are written out in full, because the object I need is not maximal predictive fit but an auditable decomposition: the null of Section~\ref{sec:identification} that switches the lock-in elasticity off is interpretable only if every term it holds fixed is visible, which a flexible black-box hazard would obscure. Those machine-learning survival methods are a natural robustness avenue for the loan-level fit, and I probe its tabular end directly: Section~\ref{sec:patha} reports a gradient-boosted Poisson comparator and an isotonic-recalibration comparator, each given Path A's information set on the identical pre-committed holdout, and each recalibrating levels without gaining cross-cell predictive power; the deep loan-level variants remain future work on data-availability grounds. A complementary strand attacks nonstationarity rather than nonlinearity: landmarking re-anchors a simple hazard among the survivors at successive prediction times \citep{vanhouwelingen2007,putter2022}, and recent credit-risk work pairs landmark targets with explicit probability recalibration to keep survival predictions stable under covariate and base-rate drift, demonstrated on the same Freddie Mac performance data my hazard paths use \citep{peng2026}. My design meets the same concern with fixed, visible machinery (the stratum fixed effects, calendar-month dummies, and dynamic friction index of Section~\ref{sec:patha}) and prices the residual time-variation it cannot remove through the temporal moving-block bootstrap reported there. On the agent-based side, my calibration is fixed by the pre-committed parameterization of Section~\ref{sec:method-abm} rather than by an automated estimator, but it sits alongside a maturing literature on data-driven agent-based-model calibration and estimation, including simulated-minimum-distance and Bayesian methods \citep{grazzini2017,platt2020} and the empirical-validation taxonomy of \citet{fagiolo2007}, within which the pre-committed falsification design of Section~\ref{sec:abm} is one validation strategy among several.

\section{Empirical Methodology}\label{sec:method}

\subsection{Two Structurally Distinct Estimators}\label{sec:method-estimators}

The first estimator is a 10,000-household Agent-Based Model (ABM), specified in Section~\ref{sec:method-abm}, which draws a synthetic population calibrated to aggregate FRED medians and tests whether a utility-maximizing household decision rule, rational or behaviorally extended, can account for the shortfall; its results are reported in Section~\ref{sec:abm}. The second is a hazard framework, specified in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, which abandons the household utility function entirely and models loan-level survival directly on real Freddie Mac loan-level data: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard model estimated by Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood on the full 2017--2021 origination universe aggregated to stratum-months (Path A), and a loan-level microsimulation with competing prepayment and default risks on a stratified 75,000-loan sample drawn from that universe (Path B).

Both estimators are evaluated against the same \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) and the same June 2022--November 2025 QT window, so their results are directly comparable even though the estimators draw on different data and different behavioral assumptions. The two estimators differ in modeling paradigm (household choice versus loan-level survival), and, on the hazard side, in the use of real rather than synthetic loan-level data; Section~\ref{sec:robustness} finds that the difference in population size between them (10,000 households versus Path B's 75,000 simulated loans, with Path A estimated on the full origination universe) does not drive the gap in their explanatory power, though the data-source difference remains an unresolved confound that qualifies the paradigm interpretation throughout. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} maps the pipeline.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig7_architecture}
\caption{Pipeline architecture. Data sources (FRED macro series, NY Fed SOMA holdings, Freddie Mac loan-level performance) feed three estimators (the synthetic-population ABM and hazard Paths A and B), whose simulated roll-off paths are scored through one shared macro-accounting layer against the \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark of Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}. The two robustness cross-links are the cross-design test (real Freddie covariates into the ABM, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}) and the symmetric companion test (synthetic population into the hazard framework, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis}).}
\label{fig:architecture}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Agent-Based Model: Specification}\label{sec:method-abm}

I adopt an agent-based approach rather than a representative-agent framework because the phenomenon under study is heterogeneous: households differ in coupon, loan age, and exposure to search and sentiment frictions, and these differences interact non-linearly with the level of interest rates. \citet{geanakoplos2012} argue for, and demonstrate the feasibility of, agent-based models of mortgage prepayment in environments where behavioral and search frictions vary across the population and over time, a property directly relevant here since the frictions I model (housing search costs and consumer sentiment) are themselves time-varying. The broader methodological case for agent-based computational economics in settings with meaningful heterogeneity is set out in \citet{tesfatsion2006}.

The ABM simulates a population of N = 10,000 heterogeneous households. Initial agent endowments (income, home value) are drawn from log-normal distributions calibrated to Federal Reserve Economic Data (FRED) medians. In the production multi-vintage specification of Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}, each household's mortgage coupon and origination date are drawn from the Federal Reserve's actual holdings composition: the live CUSIP-level SOMA parse of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}, across seven coupon cohorts. A single-cohort variant, used for exposition and mechanism analysis, instead endows every agent with a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage at a 3.0\% coupon aged 60 months at the simulation start; where the text below discusses a single representative coupon, it refers to this pedagogical case, not to the production population.

Section~\ref{sec:robustness} reports a scale-sensitivity replication showing that the gap in explanatory power is not an artifact of sample size; whether it reflects modeling paradigm or data source is taken up in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}.

The decision to relocate turns on a comparison of the utility of moving against the aggregate financial penalty of doing so. The monthly payment is calculated via the standard annuity formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:annuity}
M = P\,\frac{r\,(1+r)^{n}}{(1+r)^{n}-1},
\end{equation}
where $P$ is the outstanding principal, $r$ is the monthly interest rate, and $n$ is the remaining term in months. The mobility penalty evaluates \eqref{eq:annuity} twice: once at the borrower's locked-in note rate on the original principal and term, which gives the current payment (by the amortization identity, equal to the payment on the outstanding balance at the note rate over the remaining term), and once at the prevailing market rate on the outstanding balance over a fresh full original term---the replacement loan re-amortizes, a term-preference persistence the code implements deliberately---which gives the payment the borrower would take on by refinancing, so the penalty scales with the difference between the two; a native short-term variant of the penalty, active only for terms at or below 240 months, floors payment decreases at zero and is specified with the full decision rule in Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}. Here $r$ is expressed in decimal monthly terms (the annual note rate divided by twelve; a 3.0\% coupon gives $r = 0.0025$), a rate \emph{level} distinct in both role and units from the annualized rate \emph{gaps} $g_{i,t}$ (in 100 bp units) and $\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ (decimal) that drive the hazard framework of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; no rate symbol is reused across the two. Across a three-dimensional grid of market rate, dynamic friction, and rate velocity (and, in the multi-vintage specification, across seven coupon cohorts), the population's move/stay decisions are evaluated household-by-household at each grid point and aggregated to a pool-level annualized Conditional Prepayment Rate (CPR) surface (CPR is a pool-level convention; the household-level object is an annualized move intensity, consumed by the accounting layer at one-twelfth per month, Appendix~\ref{app:abmspec}), which is precomputed once and interpolated month-by-month against the live population of simulated market conditions, rather than re-solved via per-month household loops. This reduces the cost of the full grid by roughly two orders of magnitude and makes fifty-seed Monte Carlo estimation feasible. The surface stores each node's direct household-by-household evaluation, so interpolation reproduces the micro-simulation exactly at grid points; the operative approximation is the trilinear segment between 50 bp-spaced nodes, and a spot-check validates it directly: after an on-grid parity gate reproduced the committed surface values, direct micro-simulation and interpolation at worst-case off-grid midpoints differ by at most 0.23 points of CPR away from the freeze threshold, with the Danish column exact. The one systematic difference is the wait-and-see freeze itself: the engine applies it as a step at the 150 bp trigger while the surface renders it as a ramp across the adjacent velocity nodes, which binds in four realized months (August--November 2022; SOMA-weighted mean absolute difference 0.22 points of CPR over the window): surface semantics that the production figures share, and whose trapped-liquidity consequence the binary-step leg of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}'s freeze sweep prices directly. Figure~\ref{fig:cprsurface} renders one slice of that surface---the 3.0\% cohort of the expository variant---with the realized QT window located on it.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig8_cpr_surface}
\caption{The precomputed CPR surface: the production surface's slice at the 3.0\% thirty-year SOMA cohort, at its parsed seasoning. Left: the U.S. par-payoff surface over the market-rate $\times$ dynamic-friction grid (no-freeze regime), with the 42 QT-window months overlaid at their realized (rate, friction) coordinates; the window sits entirely in the deep out-of-the-money corner, near the involuntary-turnover floor. Right: rate slices at the friction grid point nearest the window mean. Velocity enters the grid only through the wait-and-see freeze of Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions} (20\% of otherwise-mobile households held in place above a 150 bp trailing six-month rise), so the two regimes shown are its entire velocity structure; the Berger-recalibrated Danish counterfactual (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) is near-flat, moving roughly a quarter point across the grid. The U.S. slice is identical across the frozen production surface and the current code head; the Danish curve is the Berger production specification.}
\label{fig:cprsurface}
\end{figure}

The simulation contrasts two institutional payoff rules. Under U.S. par-payoff, the cost to retire the mortgage is strictly equal to the outstanding principal, the negatively convex case, in which the investor's upside is capped while extension risk is bounded only by the scheduled-amortization profile: a 14.7-year weighted-average life at zero CPR against the no-shock 3.4-year life (Table~\ref{tab:wal}), an order-of-magnitude extension ceiling rather than an unbounded one. Under Danish market-value repurchase, the payoff cost is the present value of the remaining scheduled payments discounted at the current 30-year primary mortgage rate (FRED MORTGAGE30US), capped at par. Two qualifications attach to this proxy. First, it is an option-free present value discounted at the primary rate: it ignores the embedded par call and any option-adjusted spread, and the primary rate embeds the primary--secondary spread (guarantee fee, servicing, originator margin), whereas the bond-market discounting a true buyback would use sits below it; both choices overstate the borrower's buyback discount as rates rise. These biases inflate the mechanism-substitution Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} (the since-superseded 47.1\%) but are exactly immaterial to the Berger-recalibrated production finding: under neither transplant anchor does that leg's prepayment hazard consume this pricing proxy (the Danish-level anchor imports the near-flat moving curve; the production U.S.-intercept anchor evaluates the U.S. hazard at zero rate gap), and a replication-package diagnostic that re-runs the Danish leg with the discount rate shifted down by 25--150 bp moves trapped liquidity by \$0.00 billion, while flipping 2,470--92,495 of the 1,683,082 loan-month present-value classifications\footnote{This Danish-leg classification universe of 1{,}683{,}082 loan-months is 42 fewer than the 1{,}683{,}124 evaluated in the U.S.\ production hazard panel (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}): the two figures count active loan-months in the two counterfactual legs, whose prepayment paths differ marginally even under the shared imported elasticity,} that no downstream quantity in that leg consumes. Second, what the Danish rule neutralizes is extension risk for the bondholder \citep{berger2026}: the borrower retains the par call, so the rate-decline side keeps its price cap, and I use ``convexity-neutral'' below strictly in this rate-rise sense. Under the Danish rule, as market rates rise above 3.0\%, the bond's present value falls, allowing the borrower to extinguish the debt at a discount and guaranteeing continuous cash flow generation. The Danish counterfactual balance is simulated forward on a dynamic, declining-balance basis, so that each month's elevated Danish roll-off is applied to the already-shrunk balance carried over from the prior month and the simulated payoff path compounds correctly over the multi-year QT window.

\subsection{Macroeconomic Friction and Structural Extensions}\label{sec:method-frictions}

To quantify the behavioral friction gap in standard models, I scale base transaction friction using two FRED series: (1) search friction, measured as the shortfall in Active Listing Count (ACTLISCOUUS) against a 2017--2019 baseline, the last stable pre-pandemic period before the 2020/21 rate-driven frenzy and the 2022 inventory collapse; and (2) psychological friction, indexed from University of Michigan Consumer Sentiment (UMCSENT) against a baseline of 85.0, with readings below that line scored as incremental friction at +5 bp per point short, capped at +150 bp during acute sentiment stress. This produces a dynamic friction parameter (a one-time moving transaction cost expressed as a share of the household's current home value) that scales between 8.23\% and 9.96\% of home value over the 42 active QT months.

Three behavioral extensions were added to the household decision function, none with any parameter tuned to match the empirical benchmark, though their grounding is uneven (the first two are parameterized from external sources, the third an author calibration): a debt-to-income (DTI) constraint that vetoes any move in which the new housing payment would exceed 43\% of gross monthly income, a front-end heuristic motivated by the pre-2021 General QM debt-to-income limit (12 C.F.R. \S{} 1026.43(e)(2)(vi)); the regulatory threshold was a back-end total-debt ratio and was superseded by the price-based General QM definition in 2021, so I use it as a behavioral anchor rather than a live regulatory constraint, and the model carries no total-debt data against which to test the threshold (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}); asymmetric loss aversion that scales perceived payment increases by 2.25$\times$ while leaving decreases unscaled (loss aversion: \citealp{kahneman1979}; the 2.25 coefficient: \citealp{tversky1992}; \citepos{andersen2022} housing-market estimate of roughly 2.5 is close, and because any coefficient in this range triggers the same turnover-floor recalibration described below, the artifact interpretation of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} does not hinge on the choice); and a wait-and-see freeze that holds 20\% of otherwise-mobile households in place whenever the trailing six-month change in mortgage rates exceeds 150 bp, modeled as a fixed per-agent trait so the CPR surface remains deterministic; the 20\% share and 150 bp trigger are author calibrations with no external source, and the same is true of the sentiment-to-friction mapping and the base transaction-friction level of the preceding paragraph (the UMCSENT and ACTLISCOUUS inputs are data; the $+5$ bp-per-point mapping, its $+150$ bp cap, and the base friction level are mine). An externally parameterized variant of the mobility-desire scale and the freeze share, drawn from the same source as Path B's elasticity, is tested in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}.

Because loss aversion steepens the penalty function facing every household, and the model's mobility-desire parameter is independently calibrated to reproduce a real-world involuntary-turnover floor of 4--5\% CPR at 8\% market rates (the turnover speed observed on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, when refinancing incentives were absent and remaining prepayment reflected death, divorce, and forced relocation), I hold that empirical floor fixed and re-run the calibration under the steeper penalty function, which requires nearly tripling the mobility-desire scale parameter. This mechanical recalibration is the principal driver of the results in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}.

Whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household, the mobility-desire scale, and only that parameter, is re-derived so that the simulation continues to reproduce the empirically observed 4--5\% involuntary-turnover floor. The same rule generates the recalibrated variant of the cross-design test (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}), whose frozen variant is defined by suspending it, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} reports how much work this single choice does. Because the 4--5\% floor is imposed by construction, the ABM's agreement with observed discount-cohort turnover at deep rate gaps is an input, not a finding. The model earns no evidential credit on that dimension; its evidential exposure lies entirely on the dimensions the anchor leaves free, the dollar recovery and the timing of the CPR path. The anchor is also insulated from the behavioral gates by construction: the calibration point is evaluated at zero rate velocity, where the wait-and-see freeze cannot bind, so no freeze parameterization can move the floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} verifies the calibrated scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every freeze share swept). What the anchor purchases, and what dropping it costs, is measured directly in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}: the externally parameterized variant that removes the floor overshoots the benchmark by half while predicting essentially zero turnover at QT-window rate gaps.

An income-scaled curtailment channel was added as an extension specified ex ante: a diagnostic correlation between the ABM's monthly CPR over-prediction and year-over-year growth in real disposable income (FRED series DSPIC96) of $r = -0.487$, concentrated in the worst over-prediction months (June--September 2022), led me to predict ex ante that curtailment, a strictly additive channel, would worsen the aggregate match. Curtailment was implemented as an income-scaled addition to simulated roll-off, mapping real income growth linearly from zero curtailment at $-2$\% growth to a full-scale annualized rate of 1.2\% CPR at +4\% growth, consistent with GSE daily prepayment report estimates.

Finally, the single representative 3.0\% coupon assumed elsewhere was replaced, as a diagnostic, with a multi-vintage composition: SOMA holdings bucketed into seven coupon cohorts yield a weighted-average coupon of approximately 2.55\% on the 30-year book, with the 2.0\% and 2.5\% buckets accounting for roughly 69\% of 30-year face value. Because a lower coupon implies a larger gap to prevailing market rates, my ex ante hypothesis was that this composition would increase predicted trapped liquidity toward the empirical benchmark. Fifteen-year MBS (approximately 9\% of SOMA face value) are excluded from this pass, and cohort origination dates are approximated from parsed maturity strings under a 360-month term assumption. Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} describes the production replacement of this diagnostic with a live, CUSIP-level pull of SOMA holdings.

\subsection{Empirical Validation Benchmark}\label{sec:method-benchmark}

I construct the empirical shortfall benchmark from the New York Fed's SOMA current face value series, netting realized roll-off against the actual phased redemption cap schedule (\$17.5 billion per month from June through August 2022, stepping up to \$35 billion per month from September 2022 onward) strictly within the active QT window of June 2022 through November 2025. Net accumulation, rather than one-sided accumulation of shortfall months only, is essential for comparability with the Danish counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, where simulated roll-off routinely exceeds the cap. Figure~\ref{fig:benchmark} traces the construction: monthly roll-off against the phased cap, and the cumulative paths whose terminal gaps are the cap-relative benchmark and the expectations-based complement introduced below.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig9_benchmark_construction}
\caption{The empirical benchmark. Left: realized monthly SOMA agency MBS roll-off against the phased redemption cap; the shaded area is the monthly shortfall, which accrues on a net basis (the two circled opening months are net settlement inflows). Right: the same construction cumulatively. The terminal gaps are the \$764.7 billion cap-relative benchmark and, against the New York Fed's May 2022 ex-ante projection, the \$87.8 billion expectations-based complement. Monthly series from the frozen production run's metrics; the projection's monthly path is derived from the published year-end levels under the pre-committed uniform-spread rule and asserted, in the generator, to reproduce the frozen expectations-run totals.}
\label{fig:benchmark}
\end{figure}

This methodology yields an empirical shortfall of approximately \$764.7 billion (unrounded: \$764.748 billion; every percentage-of-benchmark figure in this paper divides by this value), which I adopt as the benchmark for the ABM's and the hazard framework's simulated trapped liquidity throughout Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}. The SOMA face underlying this benchmark spans the Federal Reserve's full agency MBS book (including Ginnie Mae collateral, 20.4\% of face), and 66.2\% of that face lies in the 2017--2021 vintages covered by the Freddie Mac loan-level sample of Section~\ref{sec:hazard}; the hazard framework's loan-level foundation therefore directly samples neither Ginnie Mae collateral nor roughly one-third of the book by vintage (the Ginnie dimension is externally bounded and scored by a published-speed overlay, and the vintage dimension is bounded by observed out-of-sample-vintage speeds from the replication ingest, both in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; what each bound leaves open is stated in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}). The empirical CPR back-out nets cohort-weighted scheduled amortization; an income-scaled curtailment assumption averaging approximately 0.84\% annualized, consistent with GSE daily prepayment report estimates, enters on the simulated side (added to each simulated roll-off and netted as a common dollar item in the shared accounting layer of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}), so the back-out's CPR series retains the curtailment component. Two implementations of this back-out exist in the pipeline (the ABM side nets cohort-weighted, term-aware scheduled amortization, while the hazard side uses a single-pool amortization approximation), and any correlation computed against them differs only at the third decimal; Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s note states which rows use which. 

\paragraph{Accounting bases.} Every recovery percentage in this paper is quoted on one of four accounting bases, which differ only in two orthogonal choices, each a pure accounting layer with no modeling content: whether the common curtailment flow is netted out, and whether the estimation sample is reweighted to the SOMA book's coupon mix. The \emph{standalone-scorer basis} makes neither adjustment. The \emph{benchmark-consistent shared basis} nets the common curtailment flow just described (\$69.56 billion over the window) from every U.S. leg identically, a flat 9.1 percentage points of the benchmark for every U.S. estimator. The \emph{full-book basis} instead reweights the standalone figure from the estimation sample's coupon mix to the SOMA book's weighted-average coupon (WAC), with no curtailment netting. The \emph{composed basis} applies both adjustments: the same 9.1-point netting to the full-book (book-coupon-reweighted) figure. Because the netting is bit-identical across the central and null legs of every comparison, it cancels exactly from the lock-in marginal and from every cross-estimator gap, an invariance that holds at every cell of the calibration box and under a $\pm 25\%$ stress on the assumed curtailment rate (derivation and stress in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}; the four bases are collected for every hazard leg in Table~\ref{tab:bases}). The cancellation is term-by-term monthly, not merely aggregate: the netted flow is the product of a shared monthly rate and the shared actual-holdings path, so any time profile for curtailment (calendar-seasonal, or income elasticities that differ across sub-periods) cancels identically from the marginal and from every cross-estimator gap, moving only the common wedge. The functional form of the income-to-rate mapping is immaterial to the marginal for the same reason: any alternative map (concave, threshold, or piecewise in income) produces some monthly rate series, which is again a shared profile, so it can move only the common wedge. Main-text headline figures are quoted on the shared basis throughout.

The cap-relative basis now carries an expectations-based complement, addressing a tension in the cap-relative construction itself: the caps were widely expected to be non-binding for agency MBS, so a shortfall measured against them mixes an anticipated mechanical component with genuine surprise. Measured instead against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection, the baseline path published in the New York Fed's May 2022 Open Market Operations annual report \citep{omo2021}, whose projections start from the February 2022 balance sheet, incorporate the FOMC's May 2022 Plans, and model runoff from June 2022, with the published year-end figures spread uniformly within each printed year and clipped to the window under a rule fixed before the numbers were seen (the report prints year-end levels only, and no staff projection at monthly granularity is public for any vintage covering this window; the spread rule was pre-committed precisely because the source is coarser than monthly), projected agency MBS runoff over the window is \$740.6 billion against realized runoff of \$652.8 billion: an expectations-based shortfall of \$87.8 billion, 11.5\% of the cap-relative \$764.7 billion. Equivalently, the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection already implied 75.6--88.5\% of the realized cap-shortfall, the range spanning defensible intra-2022 allocations of the projection's printed annual net: \$676.9 billion, or 88.5\%, under the pre-committed uniform-spread central construction. That a source predating every estimate in this paper already anticipated the large majority of the shortfall is the substantive point, and it stands on its own. What does not stand is a sharper reading it invites. Under the uniform-spread construction the 88.5\% sits 0.2 points from the 88.7\% the $\beta_1 = 0$ mechanical null recovers on the benchmark-consistent basis, and I presented that near-equality as convergent evidence. I withdraw the presentation: the 0.2 points require two conditions at once. They require the in-sample 4.0\% involuntary floor, because at the off-window floor the paper headlines the null recovers 85.7\% and falls 2.8 points \emph{below} the projection rather than 0.2 above it; and they require the uniform-spread allocation, because the settlement-aware allocation puts the anticipated share at 75.6\%, which the null clears by 13.2 points in-sample and 10.1 points at the headline floor. A gap that ranges from $-2.8$ to $+13.2$ points across two disclosed choices is not a convergence, and I now report the projection range and the null's recovery as two separately stated quantities. The agreement that survives is coarse and is the one I rely on: both objects put the anticipated mechanical component in the large majority of the shortfall. On the allocation choice itself, the central 88.5\% rides on the uniform-spread rule's treatment of the printed 2022 annual net, which nets first-half settlement inflows against June--December runoff, and the settlement-aware allocation that assigns those inflows to the first half (the realized pattern: holdings rose \$92.3 billion over January--May 2022 in the benchmark's own SOMA series) is what lowers the anticipated share toward roughly three-quarters (75.6\%), leaving the large-majority reading, and every gate and threshold of the run, unchanged. Parametric within-year ramps bound the remaining allocation freedom: linear 2022 ramps move the anticipated share only to 88.0--89.0\%, the December-2025 window clip spans 86.1--90.6\% between its all-front and linear-back extremes, and the extreme bracket that pushes all 2025 runoff to the clipped December raises the share to 115.3\%; the mechanical-majority threshold survives at every allocation. Every percentage-of-benchmark figure in this paper remains a cap-basis quantity; dollar objects and estimator rankings are basis-invariant, the same dollar decomposition rescales by the $8.7\times$ benchmark ratio on the expectations basis, and the identified lock-in marginal amounts to 49\% of the expectations-based shortfall under the central uniform-spread construction at the headline off-window floor ($+\$42.6$ billion), or 80\% at the in-sample calibration point ($+\$70.3$ billion); because that shortfall is itself allocation-dependent, the ratio falls as the anticipated share falls (to roughly 23\% and 38\% respectively at the 75.6\% allocation) and is undefined at the extreme 115.3\% bracket, where the expectations-based shortfall turns negative. Every one of these ratios rests on one condition I cannot test: it reads the expectations-based shortfall as a residual free of lock-in, when the projection's own prepayment model may already have embedded part of the channel. I cannot recover that model from the published path of holdings, so whatever lock-in the projection already anticipated sits inside the \$740.6 billion rather than in the \$87.8 billion residual, and the ratios are upper bounds on lock-in's share of the genuine surprise, exact only if the projection embedded none of the channel.

The benchmark measures shortfall relative to the redemption cap, and the cap is a ceiling on runoff rather than a forecast of it: New York Fed staff projected the below-cap regime from the month the \$35 billion cap took effect \citep{nyfed2022}, so the shortfall relative to the cap is an upper bound on the shortfall relative to any reasonable ex ante expectation, an upper bound the complement above now quantifies: the expectations-based shortfall is \$87.8 billion, 11.5\% of the cap-relative figure. I retain the cap as the headline counterfactual for two reasons. First, the cap is the only policy-committed quantity available: the staff projection underlying the complement is a baseline forecast, not an FOMC-stated target, and adopting any forecast, the Federal Reserve's or a model's, as the headline benchmark would render it circular with respect to models of expected prepayment, the object under study. Second, the cap-relative construction is a policy-relevant quantity: it measures the gap between the maximum balance-sheet reduction the FOMC authorized and the reduction the mortgage structure delivered. Throughout the paper, ``trapped liquidity'' and ``shortfall'' accordingly denote the shortfall relative to the cap.

\section{Agent-Based Model Results: A Synthetic-Population Falsification Test}\label{sec:abm}

This section is a falsification test of the household-choice hypothesis, not the paper's main estimate, and the first thing to report about it is what its own cross-design leg establishes about its scope, because that result governs how everything below should be read. Re-running the same decision rule on real Freddie Mac structural covariates, under the recalibration rule the model already applies elsewhere, recovers 59.3\% of the benchmark on the committed draw and 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds---above the 50\% threshold fixed before that run at which this section's paradigm reading would be undercut, and undercutting on all fifty of them---rising to 76.3\% once the sample is reweighted to the SOMA book's own coupon composition. Three things bound that reading and travel with it wherever it appears: the same reweight moves the frozen-calibration leg the other way, down to 12.6\%; the covariates swapped in are structural only, since the loan records contain no analogue for mobility desire or loss aversion, so no leg here replaces a synthetic behavioral population with a real one; and the verdict the run supports is that the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm rather than to calibration and data source (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). What this section falsifies is therefore a \emph{synthetic-population} household-choice specification: no specification tested here explains the Federal Reserve's trapped liquidity, and why it does not is not cleanly separable from the population it was tested on. A second qualifier travels with the verdict and is developed where it binds: the multi-vintage stage moved against its own predicted direction and the inversion is undiagnosed (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions}).

One consistency point belongs here rather than three sections later, because the same number is put to two uses. Section~\ref{sec:identification} \emph{declines} this cross-design recovery as evidence that the imported lock-in elasticity is too small, on the ground that a recovery level is not what that design identifies and that the same reweight moves the frozen leg down. Read as a scope limit on this section's verdict, which is how it is used here, it says something narrower and survives that objection: it bounds the population over which a household-choice specification was shown to fail, and makes no claim about the size of the elasticity. The two uses are consistent, and neither licenses the other.

The production estimate below is the synthetic-population special case, and it is the case in which the falsification bites. Every specification tested there (rational baseline, behavioral extensions, and a burnout variant) moves further from, or fails to close, the \$764.7 billion benchmark, so I report them briefly and move to the structurally distinct hazard framework of Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, the estimator on which the paper's central decomposition rests and which recovers, at the aggregate level, nearly all of the benchmark the ABM cannot. Figure~\ref{fig:recovery} summarizes the estimator comparison developed in this section and the next, and Figure~\ref{fig:waterfall} traces the ABM's specification path stage by stage.

Stage levels from the frozen run manifests catalogued in the run ledger (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}).}
