\subsection{The Synthetic-Population Estimate: Baseline, Production, and Behavioral Extensions}\label{sec:abm-results}

A purely rational baseline projects a structural cash flow deficit of \$544.0 billion (71.1\% of benchmark). The production ABM builds on this baseline in four ways: scheduled amortization, a live CUSIP-level parse of SOMA holdings, a corrected empirical CPR back-out, and a settlement-lag kernel. The kernel is retained in production, and both frozen run manifests record it as applied; it is a documented timing null rather than a fit improvement: a pre-fold-in ablation left the peak cross-correlation unchanged at $r = +0.192$ at lag $-3$ with the kernel on or off, and the kernel-applied production run peaks at $+0.195$ at the same lag. The production specification is not kernel-free, and every figure reported here is the kernel-applied run's. Together, these additions yield a production estimate whose headline quantity is the seed distribution, not any single draw: a fifty-seed mean of \$103.7 billion in trapped liquidity, or 13.6\% of the benchmark, with the frozen seed-42 replication draw (\$91.0 billion, 11.9\%, near the 34th percentile of the distribution) retained as the reproducibility anchor the run manifests freeze, after incorporating the previously excluded 15-year MBS book into cohort weighting and scheduled amortization (structural terms only; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline} documents the fold-in, why voluntary CPR for 15-year cohorts is drawn from the same-coupon 30-year surface in this frozen headline specification, and the native 15-year gate behind the Berger-run variant). The CPR path fit is equally weak (Figure~\ref{fig:abmpaths}). Simulated mean CPR runs at 11.68\% against an empirical 5.14\%, and the raw path correlation is negative at lag 0 ($r = -0.318$), with no value inside a $\pm$3-month window exceeding the +0.19 at the three-month lead. The path $R^2$ is $-6.984$: throughout, $R^2$ is computed as $1-\mathrm{SSE}/\mathrm{SST}$ against a mean-only null, so a negative value indicates fit worse than a constant at the empirical mean. (That fifty-seed distribution's seed-level standard deviation is \$24.5 billion---a $\pm$1~SD share band of 10.4--16.8\%---and the 95\% CI of the mean, \$96.9--\$110.5 billion, reflects only simulation noise in the mean itself, not seed dispersion; Figure~\ref{fig:mc} shows the distribution against the benchmark. The 34th percentile is itself pinned only to within roughly $\pm$7 percentage points from 50 draws, so the ABM's share is better read as on the order of 10--17\% than as a three-digit point estimate.)

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig10_abm_cpr_paths}
\caption{Production ABM simulated CPR against the empirical SOMA back-out over the QT window (frozen fold-in run, seed 42). The simulated path runs at roughly twice the empirical level and does not track its month-to-month shape; the annotated means, lag-0 $r$, and $R^2$ are the manifest values quoted in the text. This is the level-and-shape mismatch behind the negative path statistics; the cross-correlation structure across lags is in Figure~\ref{fig:ccf}.}
\label{fig:abmpaths}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.82\textwidth]{monte_carlo_trapped_liquidity}
\caption{Monte Carlo population-draw distribution of ABM trapped liquidity (50 seeds, fold-in specification; CPR surface rebuilt per draw). The seed-level dispersion (SD \$24.5 billion) dwarfs the distance between the frozen seed-42 production draw (\$91.0 billion, 34th percentile) and the seed mean (\$103.7 billion); the empirical benchmark of \$764.7 billion lies far outside the distribution's support, which is the visual form of the ABM's 10--17\% recovery band.}
\label{fig:mc}
\end{figure}

Layering in three literature-grounded behavioral extensions---a debt-to-income constraint with asymmetric loss aversion and a rate-shock freeze, income-scaled curtailment, and multi-vintage coupon composition---moves the model to 54.9\%, then 45.1\%, then 33.7\% of the benchmark (Figure~\ref{fig:waterfall}). The first move is a recalibration artifact rather than evidence for loss aversion (holding a real-world turnover floor fixed under a steeper loss-aversion penalty mechanically inflates rate sensitivity), and none of the three touches the CPR path's central failure, since each operates on the level of simulated mobility rather than its timing; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-extensions} reports the mechanism-level detail for all three.

\subsection{Burnout Falsification}\label{sec:abm-burnout}

I tested whether path-dependent prepayment ``burnout,'' implemented as closed-population survivor selection, could explain the ABM's weak CPR fit. It failed: simulated CPR collapsed to approximately zero and trapped liquidity rose to 147\% of the benchmark, because a closed population of 10,000 agents depletes its mobile mass during the low-rate 2020--2021 window before QT even begins, an absorbing state incompatible with a real MBS pool that continuously receives new originations. This is \citepos{lesniewski2026} selection identity in its degenerate limit; a negative finding, not used in production, it motivates the cohort-level rather than per-agent treatment of burnout in Section~\ref{sec:hazard}.

\subsection{Interpretation}\label{sec:abm-interp}

Every ABM specification tested here either widens the gap to the benchmark or leaves it unchanged, and its CPR path stays uncorrelated or negatively correlated with the empirical path throughout. Section~\ref{sec:robustness}'s scale-sensitivity replication finds no size effect on the production specification, so what remains is either that household-level decision-making does not govern the majority of the shortfall, or that this specification is missing a mechanism. The cross-design leg reported at the head of this section bears directly on that either-or and does not settle it: handing the same rule real structural covariates moves the recovery from 13.6\% to 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds, which is what a missing-population problem looks like rather than a missing-mechanism one, but the behavioral layer stays synthetic in both legs, so the alternative is narrowed rather than eliminated. Section~\ref{sec:hazard}'s hazard framework, built on a different data source and modeling paradigm, tests the first branch directly. Whichever explanation holds, the ABM results already narrow the trade-off named in this paper's title: they weigh against household mobility choices, rational or behavioral, as the primary price the Federal Reserve paid for secondary-market liquidity, and point instead toward the loan-level and institutional channels Section~\ref{sec:hazard} takes up. The institutional counterpart---whether the par-payoff rule itself, rather than household behavior, drives the trap---is examined as a securitization-policy alternative in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}.

One asymmetry in that comparison should be stated rather than left for a reader to notice. Section~\ref{sec:identification} insists that this design identifies a differential and not a level, and the ABM is nonetheless compared with Path B on levels, because the ABM admits no counterpart to the $\beta_1 = 0$ null. The reason is structural. In \eqref{eq:pathB} the elasticity is a separable term sitting over a floor that is measured independently and survives the elasticity's removal, so the null is still anchored to observed involuntary turnover and lands at 85.7\% of the benchmark. The ABM has no such term: its 4--5\% involuntary floor is not a component of \eqref{eq:abmmove} but a \emph{product} of it, obtained by searching the mobility-desire scale $\theta$ until the penalty-bearing rule returns 4--5\% CPR at an 8\% market rate. Zeroing the penalty therefore removes the floor along with the channel. With the production $\theta = 43{,}882.8$ held fixed, the resulting leg returns 40.7\% CPR at the calibration point against the production rule's 4.8\%, over-retires the book, and scores $-259.4$\% of the benchmark; re-deriving $\theta$ under the null restores the anchor to 4.7\% but scores $+116.3$\%, because the re-search fits the null to the very turnover level the differential is meant to measure. The two readings imply marginals of $+270.4$ and $-105.3$ points. They do not bracket a magnitude; they disagree in sign, and neither is interpretable. I therefore report no ABM differential, and the estimator contrast of Section~\ref{sec:hazard} remains a contrast of levels, with the qualification that entails (run \texttt{abm\_null\_feasibility}; a feasibility probe, not a pre-committed estimate, and nothing in this paper's ranges depends on it).

\section{The Hazard Framework: A Structurally Distinct Estimator}\label{sec:hazard}

This is the estimator on which the paper's central decomposition rests. The ABM of Section~\ref{sec:abm} does not approach the hazard framework's recovery at the aggregate dollar level (91.3\% in the microsimulation form on the shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid} at the headline off-window floor, 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point; the estimated cohort-hazard form corroborates at 112.4\% but is excluded from headline ranges for the reasons Section~\ref{sec:patha} states): the ABM's production specification recovers 11.9\% on the frozen draw (fifty-seed mean 13.6\%). Even its highest-recovery behavioral extension (the 54.9\% figure I attribute in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} to a mechanical recalibration artifact rather than to loss aversion as a mechanism) falls well short. (The uncorrected rational baseline reaches 71.1\%, but that figure predates the scheduled-amortization, CUSIP-parse, and CPR-back-out corrections described in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results} and is not part of the production estimate family.) The hazard framework's recovery range, by contrast, rests on a different data source and modeling paradigm. Both hazard paths are evaluated against the same aggregate benchmark as the ABM, but only Path B's recovery is accurate on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis, and no estimator's monthly co-movement with the empirical path, Path B's included, survives removal of the window's shared trend (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Path A's monthly path fit is no better than the ABM's, a limitation Sections~\ref{sec:patha} and~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} quantify and assess, because it bears directly on how much evidential weight each recovery figure can carry.

\subsection{Motivation}\label{sec:hazard-motivation}

Section~\ref{sec:abm}'s pattern is difficult to reconcile with the hypothesis that the residual reflects a missing rational or behavioral household mechanism that a sufficiently rich utility function would eventually capture. It is more consistent with the hypothesis that the binding constraints on the empirical prepayment path lie at least partly outside the household decision function: in loan-level heterogeneity, servicer pipeline dynamics, and cohort-level path dependence that a fixed, representative-household population struggles to represent.

The hazard framework introduced in Section~\ref{sec:method-estimators} is built to test this hypothesis. It abandons the household utility function: no mobility-desire parameter, no loss-aversion coefficient, no utility-maximizing decision rule; it instead models loan-level survival directly, using proportional hazards (proportional above an involuntary-turnover floor; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}) estimated on real loan-level data, with the closed-population survivor-selection failure of Section~\ref{sec:abm-burnout} motivating a cohort-level rather than per-loan treatment of burnout. The framework retains the same \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark and the same QT window definition as the ABM for comparability, and comprises two paths: a stratum-month discrete-time hazard model estimated on aggregated loan-level data, and a loan-level microsimulation calibrated to literature elasticities with explicit competing risks.

\subsection{Path B: Literature-Calibrated Microsimulation}\label{sec:pathb}

The first hazard path brings competing risks, literature-calibrated elasticities, and a cleaner isolation of the Danish counterfactual, without reintroducing the utility-maximizing machinery of the ABM. It simulates a stratified sample of 75,000 loans (40,234 of which are still active when the QT window opens; Appendix~\ref{app:params}), each carrying static covariates (FICO, region, original LTV, coupon, and a stratum identifier matching the Path A specification) and dynamic state (loan age, balance, and a regime-specific rate gap). Equity enters only through the static original-LTV covariate: no updated CLTV and no local house-price feed exists anywhere in the pipeline, so equity dynamics over the window are unmodeled---a disclosed design boundary for this turnover-dominated, deeply out-of-the-money window, with the FICO/LTV covariate ablation reported below bracketing what the static block contributes. Each loan's monthly hazard of prepayment is specified as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathB}
h^{\mathrm{prep}}_{i,t} = \max\!\left\{\, \underline{h},\; h_0\!\left(a_{i,t}\right) \exp\!\left( \beta_1\, g_{i,t} + \beta_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \beta_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} + \beta_B\, B_{s(i),t} \right) \right\},
\end{equation}
with a competing hazard of default
\begin{equation}\label{eq:default}
h^{\mathrm{def}}_{i,t} = h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 \exp\!\big( \gamma_r\, \sigma_{i,t} + \gamma_F\, z^{\mathrm{FICO}}_{i} + \gamma_L\, z^{\mathrm{LTV}}_{i} \big), \qquad \sigma_{i,t} = \max\{0,\ -\tilde g_{i,t}\},
\end{equation}
where $\sigma_{i,t}$ is rate stress ($\tilde g_{i,t} = g_{i,t}/100$ is the rate gap of \eqref{eq:pathB} expressed in decimal units, so $\sigma$ is the decimal magnitude of an out-of-the-money gap), and $h^{\mathrm{def}}_0 = 3\times10^{-4}$ monthly. Every Path B parameter is fixed a priori in the configuration and none is estimated on the benchmark; Appendix~\ref{app:params} collects the full parameter vector with provenance, together with the delinquency transition matrix. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} maps every symbol here to its Path A counterpart. Here $g_{i,t}$ is the rate gap (the loan's coupon minus the prevailing market rate, in 100 bp units, negative when the borrower is locked in, so that the positive $\beta_1$ of \eqref{eq:beta1} suppresses prepayment exactly when the par-payoff penalty binds); $B_{s(i),t} \in [0,1]$ is the cohort-level burnout state of loan $i$'s stratum $s(i)$ (the stratum's cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB, capped at one, so $\beta_B = -0.5$ scales the hazard by $e^{-0.5 B} \in [0.61, 1]$); and $\underline{h}$ is the involuntary-turnover floor: the monthly single-month-mortality (SMM) equivalent of 4\% annual CPR, $\underline{h} = 1-(1-0.04)^{1/12} \approx 0.0034$, representing death, divorce, and forced relocation. That label is a modeling reading, not a measured decomposition: deep-discount-cohort turnover at this level includes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings alongside strictly involuntary events, no component decomposition exists in this design, and the strictly-involuntary share is plausibly well under half. Where the floor's semantics do institutional work---selecting the U.S.-intercept Danish anchor (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) and retaining the max floor form (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor})---the operative premise is only that baseline turnover cannot fall below its observed deep-discount level when the payoff rule changes, which the total-turnover reading supports; those choices are conventions consistent with that premise, not consequences of a strictly-involuntary decomposition. The floor's level is anchored to the same empirical observation as the ABM's mobility calibration (Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}): turnover on deeply out-of-the-money discount cohorts during 2023--2024, which lies \emph{inside} the June 2022--November 2025 evaluation window. While no Path B parameter touches the benchmark dollar figure, the specification's dominant level-setter is measured from in-window prepayment behavior. The verification content below is the marginal over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null rather than the aggregate level itself. The pre-committed floor sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} shows that even this marginal is conditional on the floor calibration: it spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range ($+9.2$ at the production 4\% floor), with that interval, not any point inside it, the identified object; its positivity at every floor and band point swept is a consistency check the window's rate configuration cannot let fail (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The floor's functional form matters too: replacing the hard maximum of \eqref{eq:pathB} with a competing-risks combination, under which the elasticity is never censored, moves the production marginal to $+11.25$ points and makes it nearly floor-invariant ($+11.29$ to $+11.21$ across the same 3--5\% range; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The max-form marginal's floor-dependence is censoring-driven rather than evidence about the elasticity. The floor binds in 36\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months in the production U.S. run. A substantial share of the aggregate level rides on the floor rather than the elasticity, consistent with the no-lock-in null of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, which shows the mechanical model recovers 97.8\% of the benchmark under the standalone scorer (88.7\% on the shared accounting basis). Proportionality in \eqref{eq:pathB} accordingly holds above the floor, not through it. The baseline hazard follows the 100~PSA seasoning ramp (CPR rising 0.2 percentage points per month of loan age to 6\% at month 30) applied at monthly frequency via $\mathrm{SMM} = 1-(1-\mathrm{CPR})^{1/12}$. The rate-gap coefficient is calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024}, who estimate that a 100 bp increase in the prevailing rate above the locked-in coupon reduces quarterly mobility probability by 5.5\% to 7.7\% depending on specification; I adopt the 6.5\% midpoint of that band as the ex-ante central scenario, with the band edges as sensitivity bounds\footnote{The central 6.5\% is the midpoint of that band, adopted as the ex-ante central scenario rather than a published point estimate, with 5.5\% and 7.7\% as sensitivity bounds. Quarterly probability shocks are mapped to the proportional-hazard coefficient $\beta_1$ via the survival-function transform in \eqref{eq:beta1}, evaluated at a baseline quarterly mobility probability of 6\%, a turnover-proxy calibration auxiliary that affects only the conversion level, not the elasticity band itself, and only weakly (derivation and range in Appendix~\ref{app:params}). \citet{fonseca2024} report a related but distinct estimand (a roughly 9\% reduction in moving rates per percentage point of mortgage-rate delta), which I cite as independent corroboration of the lock-in magnitude, not as a source of the band.}, converted to a monthly hazard via the survival-function transform
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta1}
\beta_1 = -\ln\!\left[\frac{1-\big(1-(1-\delta)\,P_q\big)^{1/3}}{1-\big(1-P_q\big)^{1/3}}\right],
\end{equation}
where $\delta$ is the proportional quarterly mobility decline per 100 bp of rate gap, $P_q = 0.06$ the baseline quarterly mobility probability, and the inner terms are the monthly single-month mortalities implied by constant-hazard compounding within the quarter, $1 - P_q = (1 - h_m)^3$. Equation~\eqref{eq:beta1} is the transform implemented in the codebase: at the central $\delta = 0.065$ it evaluates to $\beta_1 = 0.0686$ (unrounded $0.068571$), the value carried in the production configuration and rounded to $0.069$ in Table~\ref{tab:params}. The superseded $0.0693$ is a distinct object, the continuous-hazard variant $-\ln\!\big[\ln(1-(1-\delta)P_q)/\ln(1-P_q)\big] = 0.0693$ (unrounded $0.069258$), which the simulation does not use; the two forms differ by $0.0007$ and both round to $0.069$, but only \eqref{eq:beta1} reproduces the reported results. The transform yields the dimensionless log-hazard-ratio implied by a quarterly probability decline (it replaces a divide-by-three approximation, not a hazard itself), and \eqref{eq:pathB} then applies $\beta_1$ linearly per 100 bp of gap. At QT-era gaps of roughly $-380$ bp this compounds the local quarterly estimate to a hazard ratio of about $0.77$ ($\approx e^{3.8 \times -0.069}$), a log-linear extrapolation beyond the range in which the source elasticity was estimated. I flag it as a functional-form assumption and test it directly: a piecewise-concave transform (full marginal effect over the first 200 bp of lock-in, half beyond) moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 105.7\% (\$808.4 billion) and slightly improves the contemporaneous path correlation ($+0.281$ versus $+0.190$, peak lag unchanged at $-3$). The log-linear extrapolation is not load-bearing for the level, and is for the marginal---but the asymmetry is one of proportion, not of magnitude, and the same absolute $\pm 1$-point convention cannot support both readings. A pre-committed rerun of the paired legs under the concave transform (run \texttt{concave\_marginal}) puts the concave marginal at $+7.87$ points against the production $+9.20$ at the in-sample floor, and at $+5.06$ against $+5.57$ at the off-window point floor. At the in-sample floor the concave null is bit-identical to the production null (\$748.185 billion on both legs, because $\beta_1 = 0$ leaves nothing for the transform to act on), so the level move and the marginal move are numerically the \emph{same} 1.32 points. That 1.32 points is 1.2\% of a 107.0\% recovery and 14\% of a $+9.20$-point marginal, and it is that relative reading, not the absolute one, on which the transform is immaterial to the level and material to the marginal; on the absolute $\pm 1$-point materiality threshold the floor-form test of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} applies, both moves clear it, and the transform dimension travels with the marginal's operative uncertainty in Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}. One more assumption travels with the import, and nothing here tests it. The decline \citet{liebersohn2024} estimate is a proportional fall in a quarterly ZIP-code moving probability, while \eqref{eq:pathB} applies it to a loan's total prepayment hazard, which contains refinancing as well as moving and which is floored at a turnover level that, as noted above, itself mixes discretionary life-cycle moves and cash-out refinancings. No split of realized prepayment into moving and refinancing components exists in this design, so treating the two objects as one is a specification assumption rather than a result, and I do not sign the direction of whatever error it carries. Each month, both hazards are evaluated for every active loan. In the production configuration prepayment is fractional: each active loan's balance is drained deterministically by $h^{\mathrm{prep}}$ (a per-loan uniform draw for prepayment is a non-production discrete branch), while a uniform draw governs the default hazard, sending hits into the delinquency pipeline (current loans to D30, already-delinquent loans to default), where they transition through the Markov delinquency-resolution matrix of Appendix~\ref{app:params}. A proportional rescaling guards the case $h^{\mathrm{prep}} + h^{\mathrm{def}} > 1$. It is a safety clamp, not a competing-risks construction. It never binds in production: across all 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months per regime the maximum hazard sum is 0.016, two orders of magnitude below the threshold, at which level the exact exponential cause-allocation construction $1-\exp(-(\lambda_p+\lambda_d))$ with shares $\lambda_p/(\lambda_p+\lambda_d)$ differs from mine only at second order in the hazards. In the taxonomy of standard competing-risks formulations \citep{putter2007}, Path B is a discrete-time multi-state model parameterized entirely through cause-specific hazards: Equations~\eqref{eq:pathB} and \eqref{eq:default} are monthly event probabilities conditional on the loan remaining active (the estimand of discrete-time cause-specific regression, as implemented in open-source packages such as PyDTS \citep{meir2025}), with the delinquency pipeline of Appendix~\ref{app:params} extending the two-cause structure to a multi-state process with cures rather than an immediately absorbing default. No subdistribution (Fine--Gray) hazard \citep{fine1999} appears in the specification, and none is needed: that device places covariate effects directly on cumulative incidence functions, whereas the simulation obtains every cumulative-incidence functional (prepaid UPB, the delinquency stock, trapped liquidity) by forward composition of the cause-specific hazards. One caveat qualifies the mapping: in production, prepayment is a fractional balance-flow intensity rather than a per-loan absorbing transition, so the cause-specific reading is exact for default and holds at the balance-aggregate level for prepayment. Prepaid balances settle to simulated SOMA cash flow in the month they occur; no settlement delay is modeled on this path.

The transform above prices how the elasticity extrapolates in the gap; a second mapping assumption is what the elasticity applies to. \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimand is a moving probability, and \eqref{eq:pathB} applies the implied $\beta_1$ to the entire voluntary hazard, moving and refinancing alike. A pre-committed bracket prices that convention (run \texttt{moving\_share\_bracket}; the gap response enters through a mixture, with $1-s$ of the voluntary hazard held at its no-response level and $s$ of it carrying the full response, the involuntary floor unscaled, and parity at $s = 1$ against the committed central leg plus an algebraic identity probe fixed in the script header before any run): at the production calibration the marginal is $+\$37.7$ billion ($+4.9$ points) at $s = 0.5$ and $+\$19.2$ billion ($+2.5$ points) at $s = 0.25$, against the production $+\$70.3$ billion at $s = 1$, close to proportional in $s$. For this book's rate configuration the convention is therefore signed: applying the imported elasticity to less of the hazard can only shrink the marginal, and by roughly its share. No loan-purpose field exists in the ingested data, so $s$ is a bracketing parameter rather than an estimate, and the production convention $s = 1$ remains the reported form.

The floor enters \eqref{eq:pathB} as a single constant $\underline{h}$ broadcast to every loan-month, carrying no rate-gap, calendar-time, or covariate argument, so the specification treats involuntary turnover as acyclical and assigns every rate-responsive movement in prepayment to the elastic channel. The marginal-over-null design cancels the floor's level, since both the central and the $\beta_1 = 0$ legs carry the identical constant, but it does not cancel a floor that itself co-varies with the rate cycle: to the extent that involuntary moves are themselves suppressed when a household is locked in, that co-movement is absorbed into the estimated elastic marginal. The identified marginal at the in-window calibration is therefore best read as total rate-attributable prepayment suppression, an upper bound on the strictly-voluntary elasticity's contribution rather than a clean voluntary-versus-involuntary decomposition. At the off-window calibration the ordering reverses: the demotion's window component (roughly \$20.9 billion; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) is in-window involuntary turnover that the off-window floor classifies as rate-suppressed and the marginal no longer counts, so the off-window $+\$42.6$ billion bounds total rate-attributable suppression from below (the matched-depth $+\$63.5$ billion restores that component), and the two calibrations bracket two estimands rather than conservative and liberal readings of one. The level sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} already brackets any plausible constant-floor excursion (the 3--5\% range keeps the marginal within $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity), and the within-run cyclical variant is now tested directly: replacing the constant with $\underline{h}_t = 4\% \times (1 + \kappa z_t)$, where $z_t$ is the window-standardized 30-year mortgage rate and $\kappa = 0$ nests (and in the run exactly reproduces) the production specification, moves the marginal between $+7.1$ and $+9.4$ points across $\kappa \in [-0.5, +0.5]$, every cell inside the level sweep's bracket.

That range is mostly not a measure of cyclicality, and I no longer read it as one. A separate pre-committed run (\texttt{floor\_cyclical\_permutation}) reproduces all seven committed $\kappa$ cells bit-exactly and then decomposes them. Scrambling the floor path's time order within each $\kappa$ cell, preserving the multiset of monthly floor values and their mean exactly, leaves 85.0\% of the range span standing: 72 draws, twelve per non-zero $\kappa$, span $+7.20$ to $+9.12$ points against the committed $+7.09$ to $+9.36$. Switching only the combination rule removes it: under the competing-risks (additive) form the identical $\kappa$ grid spans $+11.22$ to $+11.27$ points, a span of $0.045$ points, so changing the rule alone eliminates 98.0\% of the max-form span, and the additive form moves less across the whole $\kappa$ grid than it moves across the 3--5\% constant-floor level sweep. The mechanism is the one-sided Jensen effect that Section~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses at length for the seasonal floor: under a hard maximum, dispersion in $\underline{h}_t$ lifts the effective hazard whenever the high cells bind and is truncated away where they do not, regardless of \emph{when} it occurs, so $\kappa$ is a dispersion dial before it is a cyclicality dial. Decomposing the low endpoint confirms it. At $\kappa = +0.5$, which departs $-\$16.09$ billion from production, level equivalence accounts for $-\$2.14$ billion (13.3\%), order-free dispersion for $-\$11.42$ billion (71.0\%), and genuine time-order for $-\$2.53$ billion (15.7\%): 84.3\% of that endpoint is order-free. The high endpoint is not an endpoint at all, departing from production by $+\$1.23$ billion, $+0.16$ points, at $\kappa = -0.1$.

A cyclicality signal does survive, and it is real but small. The true marginal falls outside the entire twelve-draw permutation range at every one of the six non-zero $\kappa$ (0 of 72 draws bracket it), and the order components are monotone in $|\kappa|$ and sign-consistent with the co-movement story the concession above states: $+\$5.29$, $+\$4.77$, and $+\$2.69$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$, $-0.25$, $-0.1$, and $-\$1.42$, $-\$1.75$, and $-\$2.53$ billion at $+0.1$, $+0.25$, $+0.5$. A floor rising with rates does lower the marginal and one falling with rates does raise it. But scrambling the time order removes most of the grid's asymmetry while leaving its level drop intact: the permuted means differ across the sign of $\kappa$ by only $\$0.22$, $\$1.80$, and $\$6.01$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.1$, $0.25$, and $0.5$, against a true spread of $\$13.82$ billion at $|\kappa| = 0.5$ ($+\$68.08$ billion at $\kappa = -0.5$ against $+\$54.26$ billion at $+0.5$). A two-sided response that survives losing the time order is the signature of dispersion rather than of co-movement: the symmetric part of the grid's movement is large and is dispersion, the asymmetric part is small and is cyclicality. What the grid bounds is therefore the marginal's sensitivity to within-run floor variation of this amplitude, of which at most \$5.29 billion in any cell is attributable to the direction of co-movement. That the concession's residual is bounded by the level sweep already reported holds a fortiori. One correction to the specification as earlier described: the window mean is pinned at the production 4\% only where the floor's non-negativity clip does not bind, and it binds in the two $|\kappa| = 0.5$ stress cells, whose realized window means are 4.0005\% and 4.0828\% rather than exactly 4\% (the artifact flags the clip per cell). Of those two, $\kappa = +0.5$ is the cell that sets the bracket's low end at $+7.09$ points, while $\kappa = -0.5$ sits mid-grid at $+8.90$; the invariant therefore fails in the cell that matters most for the reported range, and the convexity-matched flat floor equivalent to the $\kappa = +0.5$ path is 4.0977\%, not 4\%.

Aggregation from the 75,000-loan sample to the SOMA book applies balance weights: simulated prepaid UPB is scaled by the ratio of the Fed's MBS book to the sample balance, with no reweighting toward Ginnie Mae collateral (20.4\% of book face) or vintages outside 2017--2021; the Freddie-estimated hazards are extrapolated to those segments as-is. This extrapolation is not behaviorally one-to-one: Ginnie Mae pools are FHA/VA collateral with a distinct borrower composition and a servicer buyout channel absent from the conventional book, so Freddie voluntary speeds cannot be mapped onto the Ginnie share as a like-for-like behavioral prediction. The framework therefore does not model Ginnie behavior; it bounds the composition error that treating the Ginnie share as conventional induces. Two bounds constrain this extrapolation. Along the coupon dimension, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}'s full-book reweighting (sample WAC 3.9\% mapped to the book's 2.49\%) moves Path B from 107.0\% to 109.1\%, a composition effect of about two percentage points. Along the agency dimension, an external bound now applies: Ginnie Mae's fixed-rate speeds ran 1.2--2.8 percentage points above Freddie Mac's across the window (5.2\% versus 4.0\% in February 2023; 10.3\% versus 7.5\% in May 2025; Ginnie Mae Global Markets Analysis Report, Recursion data), with the involuntary buyout channel (CDR 2.1\% versus 0.4\% in May 2025) supplying most of the structural difference. Treating the 20.4\% Ginnie face share as conventional therefore mis-states book-wide CPR by at most $0.204 \times (1.2\text{--}2.8) \approx 0.24$--$0.57$ percentage points. At the null-to-central sensitivity of roughly \$83 billion per CPR point, the composition error is bounded at \$20--\$47 billion (2.6--6.2\% of benchmark), signed toward overstating trapped liquidity. The bound is one-sided because the observed differential is: the published speeds run Ginnie above Freddie across the window, so the symmetric lower branch, a Ginnie-slower error signed toward understating trapped liquidity, does not arise in this data. The vintage dimension carries no bound of either kind: the 2022 vintage alone is 23.1\% of book face (and pre-2017 vintages another 10.6\%; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}), lies outside the 2017--2021 Freddie sample, was originated near or above prevailing market rates (so its lock-in state differs qualitatively from the sampled vintages'), and neither the coupon reweighting nor the Ginnie speed comparison constrains the extrapolation. An observed-speed bound in the same template does: the per-quarter cohort cells retained from the Fannie Mae replication ingest carry observed QT-window prepayment for the out-of-sample vintage groups. Over the window the 2022 vintage prepaid at 4.48\% against the sampled universe's 4.30\% (an unrounded differential of $+0.17$ points) and the pre-2017 vintages at 5.26\% ($+0.96$ points; the 2015--2016 cohorts alone are 99.99\% of that tail's exposure and reproduce its speed unchanged), a share-weighted book-CPR error of $+0.14$ points. At the same \$83-billion-per-CPR-point sensitivity, a bound of \$11.7 billion, 1.5\% of benchmark, below the static Ginnie bound's own lower edge and more than a quarter of the headline lock-in marginal (27.6\% of the off-window \$42.6 billion; a sixth, 16.7\%, of the in-sample \$70.3 billion), signed the same conservative direction: out-of-sample vintages prepaid \emph{faster}, so trapped liquidity is if anything overstated. Three caveats travel with the bound: Fannie observed speeds proxy the book's, the pre-2017 leg is a seasoned acquisition tail rather than a random draw of old originations, and the 2022 leg is a full-year speed applied to an H1-tilted face. Burnout is deliberately cohort-level rather than per-loan: a fractional burnout measure is not well-defined for a one-time, absorbing transition (Section~\ref{sec:abm-burnout}). Instead, each stratum tracks its cumulative prepaid UPB as a share of its original UPB (the $B \in [0,1]$ state defined under \eqref{eq:pathB}) after all prepayment transitions in a given month, broadcast to all of that stratum's remaining active loans before the next month's hazard is evaluated, and entering \eqref{eq:pathB} with the calibrated prior coefficient $\beta_B = -0.5$ (Appendix~\ref{app:params}); Path A's separately estimated burnout coefficient, statistically indistinguishable from zero (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), plays no role here. Because the simulated burnout state is endogenous, I test whether the channel is load-bearing with a $\beta_B = 0$ rerun on the production seed (run \texttt{burnout\_ablation}): trapped liquidity moves by \$6.58 billion ($-0.86$pp of benchmark, to 106.17\%), with the peak lag unchanged at $-3$. Two qualifications travel with that figure. It is measured at the in-window 4.0\% floor; at the 4.991\% off-window floor the paper headlines the same ablation is worth only \$3.48 billion ($-0.45$pp), because the floor's bind share rises from 36.3\% to 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months and the hard maximum makes burnout inert wherever it binds. And because the burnout state is endogenous to realized prepayment, the ablation moves the $\beta_1 = 0$ null as well as the central leg, so the identified marginal is not burnout-invariant: switching the coefficient off raises it from $+9.20$ to $+9.94$ points at the production floor and from $+5.57$ to $+6.51$ at the off-window floor. Both moves sit inside the $\pm 1$-point materiality convention, the second only barely, so I report them rather than resting anything on them. The FICO and LTV covariates receive the same treatment: a $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery to 108.3\% ($+1.3$pp), and a rerun under coefficients estimated Path-A-style from the panel moves it to 101.3\% ($-5.7$pp); Appendix~\ref{app:params} reports the estimation, whose imprecise point estimates are consistent with the priors' turnover-regime signs without rejecting alternatives. Production retains the ex-ante priors (the estimates are too imprecise to license replacing a pre-specified calibration mid-stream), and I flag the covariate block as the specification's largest single sensitivity: the two endpoints correspond to 99.2\% and 92.2\% on the shared accounting basis (the common netting makes the conversion exact), a swing comparable in size to the lock-in marginal itself.

The static Ginnie bound is now extended to a time-varying overlay built from the published series the bound's two snapshots came from. Extracting the full monthly agency CPR series (June 2017 through November 2025) from the December 2025 Global Markets Analysis Report (whose aggregation was revised in that issue to weighted-average unpaid principal balance, one consistent dollar-weighted methodology across the whole window) and scoring the 20.4\% Ginnie face share by the observed Ginnie speed in \emph{both} Path B legs moves the shared-basis central from 97.9\% to 89.3\% and the null from 88.7\% to 81.9\% in the raw construction, both legs at the in-sample calibration point, and confines the identified marginal to the conventional share: $+7.3$ points, positive under every variant tested (total-CPR, voluntary-only, and placebo), scaling with the conventional face share almost exactly ($0.797$ against $0.796$). Composed with the off-window headline calibration (run \texttt{ginnie\_overlay\_offwindow}), the overlay pair is 84.0\%/79.5\% central/null on the shared basis with a $+4.4$-point marginal, and the marginal correction is pure conventional-share scaling ($0.797\times$), identical across the primary, voluntary-only, and placebo series; the headline marginal therefore carries the conventional-extrapolation convention wherever quoted, with $+4.4$ points its overlay-scored member. The raw level shift overstates the Ginnie-specific effect, though: a placebo that scores the same 20.4\% share by the observed GSE-mean series moves the level by $+\$27.8$ billion on its own (the model's level calibration plus the full-universe-versus-SOMA composition difference, common to any observed-series overlay), so the Ginnie-specific differential contribution is $+\$38.5$ billion, 5.0\% of benchmark (the placebo and Ginnie-specific components, $\$27.8 + \$38.5 = \$66.3$ billion, reconcile with the raw 8.6-point shared-basis shift on the \$764.7 billion benchmark, $\approx\$65.8$ billion, to within rounding of the 0.1-point recovery shares): inside the static \$20--\$47 billion bound above, and consistent with the window-mean Ginnie--Freddie differential of 2.1 points the series itself shows against the bound's 1.2--2.8-point snapshots. The full-universe series if anything overstates the correction for SOMA's seasoned, low-coupon Ginnie holdings, the same signed direction as the static bound; and structural Ginnie inclusion remains out of scope for the modeling reason above: the overlay treats the buyout-inclusive Ginnie speed as data, which is what runoff accounting requires of a passthrough holder. This overlay and the age-standardized floor read of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} cut the same way and have never been run against each other; I report them separately and give no composed point.

Two Danish counterfactual mechanisms exist in this framework's pipeline, and only one is live in production. The first is a \emph{rate-gap swap}: holding the baseline hazard, rate-gap coefficient, covariates, and random draw logic fixed, the Danish leg expresses the market-value buyback in rate-equivalent units, so that the buyback discount offsets the locked-in spread; $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}} + \Delta y_{\mathrm{buyback}} \approx 0$ whenever the loan is out of the money, and $g_{\mathrm{DK}} = g_{\mathrm{US}}$ when rates have fallen and the buyback price is capped at par (at the representative 3.0\% coupon / 6.8\% market state the U.S. gap is $-380$ bp while the Danish effective gap is zero, so the U.S. hazard is suppressed by the full locked-in spread while the Danish hazard resets toward its baseline seasoning curve). This swap defines the \emph{mechanism-substitution} variant (the specification behind the superseded 47.1\% Danish CPR of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}), and it is \emph{not} the production mechanism. The production Danish leg is the Berger-recalibrated specification adopted in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}: it replaces the rate-gap swap with the imported flat moving elasticity, so the production Danish hazard does not respond to the buyback discount at all. The swap machinery remains in the codebase and its PV-versus-par classification is still computed each month, but no production quantity consumes its output, which is why the discount-rate diagnostic of Section~\ref{sec:method-abm} moves the Danish leg by exactly \$0.00 billion. Table~\ref{tab:discount} reports that inertness: shifting the buyback discount rate down by 25 to 150 bp (through the primary--secondary spread range) flips thousands of monthly present-value-versus-par classifications while moving the Danish CPR path, roll-off path, and trapped-liquidity dollar by exactly zero on every scoring basis. Two limits apply to that result. I state both because together they retract the immunity I previously claimed from it. First, the diagnostic executed on the Danish-level bracketing leg, not on the production U.S.-intercept leg: its own artifact records a mean Danish CPR of 3.39\%, the bracketing anchor's speed. Under the production anchor the same independence does hold, but by construction rather than by measurement, because that branch replaces the rate gap with zeros before the hazard is evaluated. Second, and more important, the result is about the hazard, not about the accounting. The Danish leg credits every retired balance at par: its dynamic-balance loop books roll-off as balance times drain, with no price term anywhere in it. Netting the roughly \$199 billion scheduled component from the leg's \$669.3 billion of Danish roll-off leaves on the order of \$470 billion of buyback-triggered retirement credited at par, against a proxy discount of 32 to 34\% at the representative 3.0\% coupon / 6.8\% market state (36 to 38\% at the book's 2.49\% weighted-average coupon). A market-value credit would reduce the Danish leg's cash receipts by an amount larger than the $+\$61.2$ billion gap itself, and in the direction that shrinks it. The pipeline does not distinguish a cash haircut from a balance adjustment, and the incidence depends on which the buyback is, so a pre-committed bracket prices both readings from the committed pair rather than choosing one (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket}; gates and the interpretive rule fixed in the script header before execution): under the balance-adjustment reading face retirement is unchanged and the gap stands at $+\$61.2$ billion, while under the cash-haircut reading receipts fall by the proxy discount on the leg's \$470.7 billion of early face and the gap reverses, to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion across the committed 32--38\% discount grid. The gap's sign is therefore incidence-conditional, the channel is priced rather than un-priced, and the discount-rate diagnostic still should not be read as immunity to the pricing critique.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{Danish-leg sensitivity to the buyback discount rate. The diagnostic executed on the Danish-level Berger-recalibrated leg (mean Danish CPR 3.39\%), which is the bracketing anchor of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} rather than the production U.S.-intercept leg. Neither leg consumes the present-value classifications the discount rate enters, so the zero deltas hold on the standalone, shared, and printed bases alike; under the production anchor that independence is structural rather than run-verified. The baseline CPR path reproduces the frozen parquet for the leg that ran exactly. The zeros bound the hazard channel only: the leg's dollar conversion credits retired balance at par, an un-priced channel sized in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}.}
\label{tab:discount}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Discount-rate shift & PV classifications flipped & $\Delta$ CPR path (pp) & $\Delta$ trapped (\$B) \\
\midrule
0 bp (production) & -- & 0.00 & 0.00 \\
$-25$ bp & 2{,}470 of 1{,}683{,}082 & 0.00 & 0.00 \\
$-50$ bp & 7{,}805 of 1{,}683{,}082 & 0.00 & 0.00 \\

Simulated on the 40,234 window-start survivors of the 75,000-loan stratified sample drawn from the same Freddie Mac 2017--2021 origination universe (Appendix~\ref{app:params} reports the full attrition accounting), this specification recovers \$818.5 billion, or 107.0\%, of the empirical benchmark, with a raw CPR correlation against the empirical path of $r = +0.190$ at zero lag and a peak cross-correlation of $r = +0.404$ at lag $-3$. Throughout, the cross-correlation at lag $k$ pairs the simulated series at month $t$ with the empirical series at month $t+k$ (equivalently, the empirical series at month $t$ with the simulated series at month $t-k$; both series demeaned over the overlap, boundary months dropped), so at the peak $k=-3$ the simulated value at month $t$ is matched with the empirical value from three months earlier. That definition was inverted in print---the two series interchanged, which would have implied the opposite direction---but the direction claims themselves were always computed under the implemented convention (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}). A direction check with synthetic data confirms that under this implementation a peak at $k=-3$ means the simulated path \emph{trails} the empirical path by three months. At this peak, the empirical SOMA-derived CPR path moves approximately three months ahead of the simulated path (a peak selected across the seven examined lags, so its nominal significance is overstated; no inference rests on this single coefficient). The level and band reported here follow the rate-gap coefficient's unit convention documented in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline} (the superseded figure is catalogued, with provenance, in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}).

Importing Path B's elasticity unmodified, rather than fitting it to the benchmark, has two consequences. First, unlike Path A, whose rate-gap and burnout coefficients are estimated directly from the Freddie Mac sample, Path B's rate-gap elasticity is imported unmodified from \citet{liebersohn2024} and never touches the \$764.7 billion benchmark during calibration. The two are mutually reinforcing rather than redundant: Path A's fitted rate-gap coefficient is an in-sample estimate of the same suppression channel on the same loans ($+0.29$ per 100 bp; Section~\ref{sec:patha}), and the Fannie Mae replication reproduces its sign on a second agency's loan book at roughly $3.6\times$ the per-basis-point magnitude, so the two in-sample fits are directionally consistent with the imported band---but no more than that: a pre-committed test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ that respects the diagnosed bootstrap bias (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}: BCa with a 296-refit jackknife acceleration, $p = 0.093$ stratum scheme and $0.412$ temporal; a 200-draw month-permutation of the rate-gap series, $p = 0.241$) does not reject at any conventional level, so the in-sample estimates are not statistically distinguishable from zero under bias-respecting constructions and the elasticity's evidential basis is the external literature alone. A prepayment-hazard coefficient is also not unit-comparable to \citepos{liebersohn2024} quarterly-mobility decline, and Path A measures prepayment rather than pure mobility, so what remains is directional consistency, not triangulation of sign or direction, not magnitude. A second literature anchor corroborates from outside the band: propagating \citepos{fonseca2024} roughly 9\% moving-rate reduction per 100 bp through the same transform ($\beta_1 \approx 0.096$) and the same band machinery yields a marginal of $+\$87.9$ billion ($+11.5$ points) at the production floor, above the Liebersohn--Rothstein high edge ($+\$79.5$ billion, $+10.4$ points) yet inside the pre-committed floor-and-band box, so the adopted central calibration is the conservative choice among the available literature estimates; because that estimand is a moving-rate reduction rather than a quarterly-mobility decline, I read it as directional corroboration, not a competing point estimate. Path B is accordingly not a second attempt to fit my benchmark but a structural verification exercise: it asks whether an elasticity estimated in a different empirical setting, dropped into a PSA-baseline hazard with competing risks and no tuning against my target, reproduces the Federal Reserve's actual QT runoff shortfall. Because the aggregate level is floor-dominated, landing within 8 percentage points of the benchmark is not, by itself, evidence for the imported elasticity, since the residual over the $\beta_1 = 0$ null that the elasticity must explain is itself a function of the floor calibration (the marginal spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The defensible verification claims are the bounded interval the calibration box implies and the monotone response across that band reported below; the marginal is also positive at every floor, band, and floor-form point tested, but under this window's rate configuration that positivity verifies the specification rather than identifying the mechanism (Section~\ref{sec:identification}), and I do not claim the level validates the elasticity's magnitude. But the timing is not part of that evidence: as Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} reports, a rerun with the elasticity set to zero reproduces the same three-month lead, so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the mechanical model. Second, propagating the full Liebersohn--Rothstein 5.5\%--7.7\% sensitivity band through the simulation yields a recovery range of \$809.9 billion to \$827.7 billion (105.9\%--108.2\% of benchmark under the standalone scorer; 96.8\%--99.1\% on the shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}), with the peak cross-correlation lag stable at $-3$ across the entire band (peak $r$ between $+0.371$ and $+0.423$; given the moving-block intervals above and the seven-lag search, these differences carry no evidential weight). Stronger assumed mobility suppression therefore maps monotonically to more trapped liquidity, as expected, and the central 107.0\% estimate should be read as a point within this interval; Figure~\ref{fig:band} plots the band. That band is a calibration interval, not sampling error, and the sampling dimension is now quantified separately: a stratified loan-level bootstrap specified ex ante in its script header (200 replicates resampling the 75,000-loan draw with replacement within strata, central and null legs paired per replicate, engine seed held at production) puts the central recovery's 95\% interval at $[107.0, 107.1]\%$ under the standalone scorer (replicate standard deviation 0.03 points), the null's at $[97.8, 97.9]\%$, and the paired lock-in marginal's at $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points (standard deviation 0.016). Those intervals are narrow because the scheme holds the design fixed: it resamples loans within each stratum and preserves every stratum's count, so between-stratum composition variance is never drawn. Drawing the 130 strata themselves with replacement, at the off-window floor the paper headlines (run \texttt{bootstrap\_pathb\_cluster}, 200 replicates), widens the marginal's interval to $[+4.63, +6.92]$ points with a standard deviation of $0.60$ points---a width of $2.29$ points against the within-stratum scheme's $0.062$, so the committed scheme understated loan-sample uncertainty by roughly a factor of $37$. The committed point marginal falls inside that interval. Two limits on the wider figure: the strata are very unequal, so the effective cluster count is $25.8$ rather than $130$ (the largest single stratum is $9.77\%$ of sample balance) and a percentile interval on $26$ effective clusters is itself noisy; and the resampled loan count varies from roughly $54{,}000$ to $101{,}000$, which is harmless here only because the engine renormalises the pool to Fed holdings each month, so the level is set exogenously and only composition varies. Even at the wider figure the sampling layer is less than half the width of the floor-read interval of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, so the operative uncertainty statements for Path B's level and marginal remain the calibration box and floor-form results there rather than sampling intervals; the shared-basis equivalents follow by the common 9.1-point netting, which cancels from the marginal entirely.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.82\textwidth]{fig6_elasticity_band}
\caption{Path B trapped liquidity across the Liebersohn--Rothstein elasticity band. The quarterly mobility-decline parameter $\delta$ is swept across its published 5.5\%--7.7\% range (central 6.5\% marked), mapping monotonically to recovered trapped liquidity of \$809.9--\$827.7 billion on the standalone-scorer basis (shared-basis equivalents in Table~\ref{tab:bases}) against the \$764.7 billion benchmark line: stronger assumed mobility suppression yields more trapped liquidity, with the peak cross-correlation lag stable at $-3$ across the entire band. Table~\ref{tab:floorband} extends this band across the involuntary-turnover-floor grid.}
\label{fig:band}
\end{figure}

The peak alignment does \emph{not} show the simulated path leading SOMA cash flow. The empirical path moves first: a settlement-delay mechanism, in which loan-level prepayment decisions show up in SOMA cash two to three months later, predicts the \emph{opposite} direction of offset. I therefore attach no settlement interpretation to the timing alignment.

A direct input-timing diagnosis rules out the dating of the rate input and narrows, but does not isolate, the mechanism. In monthly \emph{changes}, the simulated CPR responds to the contemporaneous rate change with the correct sign and near-mechanical strength ($\Delta$-correlation $-0.94$ at lag 0), while the empirical CPR's month-to-month changes show no detectable rate response ($+0.25$, inside the $\pm 0.31$ zero band at $n = 41$): at monthly frequency the empirical path's variation is dominated by non-rate factors the simulation does not model, calendar seasonality above all. One construction asymmetry also belongs in this reading: the empirical CPR back-out retains the income-scaled curtailment component (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}), while the simulated CPR paths exclude it (curtailment enters only as a common dollar flow in the accounting layer), so the two series being correlated differ by a small, slowly-varying component as well as by seasonality.

\subsection{External Replication on Fannie Mae Loan-Level Data}\label{sec:pathb-fannie}

Beyond the Freddie Mac sample on which Path B is built, the same construction admits a direct external replication on a second agency's loan book. I re-ran the full Path B construction on Fannie Mae's single-family loan performance data (24 acquisition quarters, 2017Q1--2022Q4; 17.6 million loans and 826 million loan-months, staged into the identical origination/performance layout so that the panel builder, the estimator, and the stratified 75{,}000-loan sampling design run unchanged on the same 2017--2021 origination universe) under a spec fixed and committed before the run: zero-balance code 01 maps to voluntary prepayment as for Freddie; codes 06 and 16 (non-credit removals with no Freddie counterpart) are censored, with a worst-case bracket of 0.38\% of removals if every such removal were instead counted as a prepayment; and the pre-committed acceptance gate required the Fannie lock-in marginal to be positive and inside the Freddie floor/band envelope of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}. The result replicates: central \$821.0 billion (107.4\% of benchmark), $\beta_1 = 0$ null \$754.6 billion (98.7\%), lock-in marginal $+\$66.4$ billion ($+8.68$ points of benchmark) against Freddie's $+\$70.3$ billion ($+9.20$ points): inside the envelope, within half a point of the Freddie estimate. The envelope was the calibration box of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, which spans 2.11 to 13.17 points---an 11.06-point window around a 9.20-point Freddie estimate, admitting a replication 77\% below it or 43\% above. A gate that wide cannot fail against anything but a sign error, and I do not treat its passing as evidence. The agreement is a different matter: 17.6 million staged Fannie loans, run through the same pipeline end to end, land 0.52 points from the Freddie point, which is a good deal more than the gate demanded and is the part of this exercise that is reassuring. What that agreement certifies needs the same care: every behavioral parameter is fixed a priori and identical across the two runs, and the two stratified samples are compositionally indistinguishable (population-stability indices at or below 0.018, Appendix~\ref{app:composition}), so the 0.52-point agreement certifies pipeline determinism and cross-agency compositional stability; the external-validity content lives in the sign reproduction and the staging inputs, not in the closeness itself. The Path A fixed-effects refit on the Fannie cohort panel reproduces the rate-gap coefficient's sign (its per-basis-point magnitude is about $3.6\times$ the Freddie fit's, and its holdout diagnostics carry the same no-forward-precision character as Section~\ref{sec:patha}'s, so it retains aggregate-corroboration status only). Two scope notes are part of the claim, both fixed ex ante: whether Freddie's code 01 absorbs exits that Fannie books under 06/16 is not settled by public documentation, so the replication claim is sign and envelope-conditional marginal consistency rather than a hazard-level match; and Fannie's terms prohibit redistribution, so the replication package ships the Fannie ingestion and replication code only, with no Fannie data and, conservatively, no derived cells; the committed artifact carries headline statistics alone.

\subsection{Path A: Stratum-Month Discrete-Time Hazard Model}\label{sec:patha}

The second hazard path aggregates Freddie Mac loan-level performance data to the stratum-month level: strata $s$ are the 296 observed four-way cells of vintage $\times$ coupon $\times$ FICO bucket $\times$ LTV bucket, and the unit of observation is the stratum-month pair $(s,t)$. The bucket edges are the estimation configuration's: FICO below 680, 680--740, and 740 and above; LTV at or below versus above 80; coupons in 0.5-percentage-point steps; vintages 2017--2021. Geography is not a stratum axis, and no geographic term enters \eqref{eq:pathA}. The model is a discrete-time hazard with a log-exposure offset,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathA}
\log \mathbb{E}\!\left[y_{s,t} \mid x_{s,t}\right] = \log n_{s,t} + \alpha_{s} + f\!\left(a_{s,t}\right) + \beta_g\, g_{s,t} + \beta_b\, b_{s,t} + \beta_\phi\, \phi_t ,
\end{equation}
where $y_{s,t}$ is stratum $s$'s prepaid unpaid principal balance (UPB) in month $t$, in dollars, $n_{s,t}$ its UPB exposure, $x_{s,t}$ the right-hand-side covariates, $\alpha_{s}$ the stratum fixed effect (295 enter, one absorbed as reference), $f(\cdot)$ a loan-age seasoning spline in mean loan age $a_{s,t}$ (truncated quadratic power basis: a linear term plus $(a - k)_+^2$ at knots $k \in \{12, 24, 36, 60, 84, 120\}$ months, seven basis columns), $g_{s,t}$ the rate gap in 100 bp units, $b_{s,t}$ the stratum burnout state, and $\phi_t$ the dynamic friction index. The outcome is dollars, not a count: with the log-exposure offset the modeled object is the prepayment rate $y_{s,t}/n_{s,t}$, a nonnegative fraction, so the model is estimated by Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood, whose score equations remain consistent for the conditional mean without any distributional assumption on $y_{s,t}$; no dispersion statistic is meaningful for this pseudo-likelihood. The 295 stratum fixed effects raise no incidental-parameters problem for the coefficients of interest: because the model is Poisson, concentrating the fixed effects out of the log-likelihood returns exactly the conditional-likelihood score for the slope vector, so the unconditional dummy-variable fit computed here yields the same slope estimates as the conditional estimator and stays consistent as the number of strata grows with the 36 training months held fixed, unlike the fixed-effect logit or probit, whose slope estimates are inconsistent at fixed panel length \citep{wooldridge1999}. That consistency requires only the multiplicative conditional mean $\mathbb{E}[y_{s,t}\mid x_{s,t},\alpha_{s}] = n_{s,t}\exp(\alpha_{s} + x_{s,t}^{\top}\beta)$ with no restriction on the distribution, dispersion, or serial dependence of $y_{s,t}$. All uncertainty statements below come from the two bootstrap schemes of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}: a stratum-level cluster bootstrap, robust to arbitrary within-stratum dependence and overdispersion but structurally unable to measure uncertainty in the friction coefficient ($\phi_t$ varies only over time, so resampling strata reproduces the identical friction series in every replication), and a temporal moving-block bootstrap that resamples the 36 training months in blocks of six with each month's full cross-section intact, which carries that burden (and, partially, burnout's). Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel's accounting; both it and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}, because Path A is excluded from every headline range by pre-committed specification and neither table enters a headline quantity. The displayed form \eqref{eq:pathA} is the no-seasonal spec v3 design; the production specification (spec v4) adds eleven calendar-month dummies and is the design under which the 121.5\% recovery reported below is computed, though that level is not calendar-attributable (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), while every Path A inferential object stays on spec v3. Table~\ref{tab:hazard-notation} collects the notation shared by the two hazard paths.

The 295 stratum fixed effects absorb each cohort's baseline prepayment speed so that the burnout term captures within-pool adverse selection over time rather than cross-sectional differences in pool quality. The burnout regressor is a within-stratum demeaned measure of the surviving loan stock, orthogonalized against the loan-age spline so that it is not mechanically collinear with seasoning. This design is the estimation counterpart of \citepos{lesniewski2026} selection identity: the exposure-offset outcome is itself a pool-level, survival-weighted (here UPB-exposure-weighted) hazard, the 295 strata cut the cross-sectional dispersion that drives the identity's negative selection term down to within-cell variation, and the demeaned surviving-stock regressor proxies the residual composition drift that the time-invariant fixed effects cannot absorb. An earlier specification using four vintage-year dummies in place of the 295 stratum fixed effects returned a burnout coefficient of the wrong sign (+0.76); replacing the coarse vintage fixed effects with the full 295-stratum specification and demeaning burnout within stratum removes that wrong-signed artifact. The corrected estimate ($-0.13$) carries the theoretically expected sign (a more heavily burned-out pool should prepay more slowly, holding age and rate gap fixed), but the sign statement is a point-estimate statement only: the temporal bootstrap scheme of Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} centers this coefficient on the opposite sign (mean $+1.23$, median $+0.52$), and under both schemes it is imprecise and not statistically distinguishable from zero, so I treat it as a control rather than a finding. Because 295 fixed effects make the model ill-conditioned under plain iteratively reweighted least squares (20 strata record no training prepayment event at all, so the likelihood is monotone in their fixed effects and the maximum-likelihood estimates diverge to $-\infty$), the fit carries a nominal ridge penalty $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ on the full coefficient vector, with $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}}$ chosen over the grid $\{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$ on a temporally blocked holdout comprising all stratum-months from January 2024 onward, so that no within-stratum information crosses the train/holdout split. The penalty is not what stabilizes the fit: at these magnitudes it is a numerical no-op (the two grid points return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start, so the production coefficients are the IRLS solution and the grid selection is inconsequential), the 20 zero-event strata are held at the finite value the warm-started optimizer leaves them at (the penalty shrinks them only negligibly); and because the split is temporal over a fixed set of strata, that same fixed effect scores each stratum's post-January-2024 holdout months, so no fixed effect is estimated on, or transferred to, a stratum absent from the training window. The penalty's operative role is as the fallback for resampled refits in the bootstrap machinery. Nor is the production point an artifact of the device: dropping the 20 zero-event strata and fitting unpenalized Poisson PML reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units (rate gap $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$). The verification details, including a reference-stratum sensitivity of penalized cold-start refits that motivates the warm-started bootstrap below, are recorded in Appendix~\ref{app:ridge}, all under spec v3 (the device is unchanged under the spec v4 refit; the verification artifacts were not recomputed at adoption). Two caveats still attach: the same holdout later serves as the out-of-sample diagnostic, so the RMSE reported below is at least weakly favored by selection, and months never touched by any selection step do not exist in this design, one reason Path A is held to a robustness role.
Estimated on this panel, the production specification (spec v4, the calendar-month seasonal design adopted at the freeze) yields a rate-gap coefficient of $+0.65$ (standardized; the training standard deviation of the rate gap is 221 bp, so the equivalent per-100 bp coefficient is $+0.29$), a burnout coefficient of $-0.17$, and a friction coefficient of $-0.007$, each with the theoretically expected sign. The friction coefficient's shrinkage relative to the prior spec ($-0.037$) is expected under the seasonal design, whose eleven month dummies absorb most within-year variation in the time-only friction index. Point estimates alone overstate what is identified here, so Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} reports cluster-bootstrap uncertainty for all three (estimated under the prior spec v3; see the table note), including the bias diagnostics: the rate-gap point estimate sits near the 3rd percentile of its own bootstrap distribution (bootstrap mean $+1.49$, median $+0.76$, estimated bias $+0.82$), a signature of resampling-induced upward skew under which percentile and basic intervals disagree (the percentile interval excludes zero while the bias-reflecting basic interval does not). I therefore rest no claim on interval-based significance for any Path A coefficient. The defensible statements are about direction and imprecision: the rate-gap coefficient's point estimate is positive, and positive in 197 of 198 converged replications (99.5\%), but that share is a property of a right-skewed resampling distribution rather than a test, and the pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ does not reject under any construction (BCa $p = 0.093$ stratum / $0.412$ temporal; permutation $p = 0.241$; run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}), so the coefficient is directionally consistent with the imported elasticity without being statistically distinguishable from zero; its magnitude is identified only up to a wide, construction-dependent interval, and burnout and friction are not distinguishable from zero on any construction. All three enter the production simulation as fitted inputs, none as findings, and no result in this paper depends on their point values. Path A's aggregate recovery now carries the interval that pass produces: resimulating the QT window under each of the 198 converged replications (the three macro coefficients swapped in per draw; intercept, age spline, and fixed effects held at the production fit) yields a median of \$922.6 billion and an interquartile range of \$900.1--\$1{,}130.6 billion, but a 95\% percentile interval of $[-\$4{,}459.8, +\$1{,}190.1]$ billion. Fifteen replications with extreme burnout or friction draws (the two coefficients the bootstrap classifies as statistically zero) destabilize the 42-month forward compounding entirely, and 83.8\% of draws land above the benchmark. Those two facts pull in opposite directions, and the unfavorable one belongs in the text as much as the favorable one: the same 198 draws have a \emph{mean} of \$598.9 billion, or 78.3\% of the benchmark---below the mechanical $\beta_1 = 0$ null's 85.7\% and below Path~B's headline 91.3\%---against a median of 120.6\%, and on the share basis the percentile interval is $[-583.2\%, +155.6\%]$ with a resampling standard error of 201.9 points. I report the mean alongside the median for the same reason the coefficient table does: these distributions are severely right-skewed, so no single summary is sufficient, and quoting only the median and the fraction above the benchmark would flatter the estimator. Propagated coefficient uncertainty therefore leaves the forward simulation with no useful precision, an additional reason the aggregate dollar figure is held to the aggregate-level role assigned below and is not carried in the abstract. Every Path A inferential object (Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap}, this interval, and the train/holdout decomposition below) remains estimated under the prior spec v3, because bootstrap replications under the spec v4 seasonal design are branch-unstable (the resampled penalized likelihood has a second optimum that attracts replications away from the production branch); until that is stabilized, the production specification carries no valid uncertainty of its own, and Path A is excluded from the paper's headline figures for this reason, retained as an aggregate-level corroboration exhibit. Simulating forward (draining each cohort's balance deterministically each month by balance times hazard, with fractional prepayment settling directly to simulated SOMA roll-off and no settlement-lag convolution), this model recovers \$928.9 billion, or 121.5\%, of the \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark under the production calendar-month specification (spec v4), adopted at the pre-submission freeze under pre-committed parity gates that reproduce the committed seasonal robustness artifact exactly (the prior specification is preserved as a spec-v3 exhibit).

Three diagnostics temper this aggregate result because they bear directly on how much weight the \$928.9 billion figure can carry. First, the holdout fit (evaluated under spec v3; the frozen spec v4 fit, re-scored on the same January-2024-onward holdout without refitting, gives 37.9 unweighted and 3.0 exposure-weighted percentage points against spec v3's 37.8 and 2.5, so the seasonal terms do not improve the out-of-sample fit) is weak in both weightings. The units matter: the unweighted RMSE of approximately 38 percentage points of annualized stratum-month CPR is dominated by small-exposure strata with unstable realized rates, while the exposure-weighted RMSE (the weighting the fit itself uses) is 2.5 percentage points against an empirical mean CPR of 5.14\%, half the mean. Neither weighting supports out-of-sample predictive power at the stratum-month level (and the ridge grid's two candidate fits are numerically identical on this holdout, so no selection hinges on the weighting). A flexible-learner ceiling test bounds how much of this weakness is functional form: a gradient-boosted Poisson learner given the same covariate information on the identical pre-committed holdout improves the exposure-weighted RMSE materially, to 0.9 points against the frozen spec v4's 3.0, while leaving the unweighted RMSE essentially unmoved (37.6 against 37.9) and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($+0.003$); a penalized Poisson GLM on the exact v4 design does no better than the production fit (2.6 and 38.1 points). Read against the run's pre-committed thresholds, flexibility buys recalibration of the high-exposure cells but no cross-cell predictive power in the 2024-onward regime. The landmarking-with-recalibration strand receives the same test: an exposure-weighted isotonic map fit on the 2023 inner-validation year only, applied to the frozen spec v4 holdout predictions, improves the exposure-weighted RMSE to 2.53 points against the frozen 3.04 (short of the pre-committed 0.80$\times$ threshold) while leaving the unweighted RMSE at 37.8 and out-of-sample $R^2$ at zero ($-0.007$); the pre-committed verdict is no material change: a monotone level recalibration fit in the training regime does not transfer predictive power across the 2024 boundary. Second, the recovery is concentrated where the model is unsupervised (decomposition measured under spec v3; it was not recomputed at adoption): of the spec v3 fit's \$915.0 billion, \$331.0 billion accrues in the 19 QT months overlapping the training window (June 2022--December 2023), against \$337.5 billion empirical (98\%), while \$584.1 billion accrues in the 23 post-training simulation months from January 2024 onward (21 of which have performance data; the empirical comparison uses those), against \$427.3 billion empirical (137\%). Components are independently rounded, and the overshoot lives almost entirely out of sample. Third, the raw CPR correlation against the empirical path is $r = -0.444$ at zero lag (more negative even than the ABM's $-0.318$), and \eqref{eq:pathA} omits calendar-month seasonality, one of the four factors of \citepos{richard1989} canonical prepayment function and a natural first suspect for a monthly path fit this poor. Re-estimating with eleven calendar-month dummies confirms strong, correctly signed seasonality (spring and early-fall log-effects up to $+0.43$ against a January base) and improves the simulated path's contemporaneous correlation from $-0.444$ to $-0.378$, with the peak moving from lag $-3$ to $-2$. That timing improvement is calendar-attributable, and I test it rather than assert it. Permuting the assignment of the twelve estimated month log-effects to calendar months preserves their multiset exactly --- and therefore every level channel they drive --- while destroying only their alignment with calendar time; scored on the conjunctive criterion the claim actually states (a lag-0 correlation better than $-0.378$ \emph{and} a peak at lag $-2$), scrambled assignments win 1 of 64 draws, a one-sided $p$ of 0.031, with an independent sixteen-draw family at a different seed also returning a single win (\texttt{pathA\_seasonal\_permutation}). The two components scored separately, 11 and 8 of 64, are diagnostics only: each necessarily exceeds the joint count, so reading either alone understates a conjunctive claim. The recovery figure is a different matter, and I no longer credit it to the calendar terms. Across those same 64 scrambles the recovery spans 121.14--122.29\%, a range of 1.15 points that contains the adopted 121.5\%, so the level is calendar-invariant: permutation holds the multiset of month effects fixed, and it is the multiset, not its alignment, that sets the level. Nor is the spec-v4 total decomposable as production plus seasonality in the first place --- the seasonal refit with all twelve month multipliers zeroed moves the recovery by $+\$80.27$ billion on its own, and restoring the multipliers claws back $-\$66.44$ billion of that, against a net reported seasonal effect of $+\$13.83$ billion. The timing improvement is real but far from sufficient: the named first suspect accounts for only a modest share of the negative path fit. A model that recovers 121.5\% of the dollar benchmark while mismatching the monthly path this badly may be recovering the aggregate partly through offsetting errors: over-predicting prepayment in some cohort-months and under-predicting in others in a way that nets out near the benchmark without the underlying mechanism being correctly timed. I therefore treat Path A's recovery as evidence about the aggregate level only. No timing claim rests on Path B either: its apparent levels advantage does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and every claim this paper makes for Path B is a level-accuracy claim. Table~\ref{tab:pathadiag} collects Path A's diagnostics in one place.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{Path A diagnostic summary. An aggregate-level corroboration exhibit: Path A is excluded from the paper's headline figures by pre-committed specification, and no cell of this table enters a headline quantity. Values repeat committed figures derived in this section and Appendix~\ref{app:theil}.}
\label{tab:pathadiag}
\footnotesize
\begin{threeparttable}

\midrule
