Refit-and-resimulate distribution \emph{(spec v3)} & point \$915.1 billion; median \$922.6 billion; \emph{mean \$598.9 billion $=$ 78.3\% of benchmark}; IQR \$900.1--\$1{,}130.6 billion; 95\% percentile interval $[-\$4{,}459.8, +\$1{,}190.1]$ billion ($[-583.2\%, +155.6\%]$ on the share basis); 83.8\% of draws above benchmark \\

The spec v3 point (\$915.1 billion) is shown so the median is not read as centred on the spec v4 level printed above it. The same caution applies to the paths themselves. Path B's $+0.190$ lag-0 levels correlation is carried by the window's shared trend: in first differences it is $-0.23$ and after linear detrending $-0.20$. The same constructions for the other estimators give $-0.20$ and $-0.30$ for the ABM (from $-0.32$ in levels) and $-0.21$ and $-0.29$ for Path A (from $-0.44$ in levels): detrending collapses the three estimators' apparent differences into a cluster between $-0.20$ and $-0.30$. Against the i.i.d.\ $1.96/\sqrt{n}$ reference ($\pm 0.30$ at $n = 42$ for the detrended series; $\pm 0.31$ at $n = 41$ for first differences), Path A ($-0.29$) and Path B ($-0.20$) are inside the band under both empirical-series conventions, while the ABM's detrended value straddles the band's edge across them ($-0.301$ common-series, $-0.303$ against its own back-out). Indistinguishability verdicts therefore rest on the wider moving-block bands of Table~\ref{tab:estimators}, under which none of the three is distinguishable from zero. Once the shared trend is removed, no estimator's contemporaneous co-movement is positive; Path B's distinction is the accuracy of its aggregate level, not its timing. (In levels, which are trend-contaminated over the window and reported only for completeness, the simulated path co-moves most strongly with the rate from four months earlier while the empirical path is contemporaneous.) No single channel carries the offset: the $\beta_B = 0$ and $\beta_1 = 0$ ablations both leave the peak at $-3$, and re-running the simulation with the rate input shifted by one to three months in either direction never moves the peak to lag 0 (it reaches $-1$ under a two-to-three-month input lead) while moving recovered trapped liquidity by less than \$1.5 billion across the entire scan. The offset is therefore a property of the fitted path's smooth aggregate dynamics set against a seasonal empirical series (mechanism not isolated), and Path A's seasonal re-estimation moving its own peak from $-3$ to $-2$ (Section~\ref{sec:patha}) is consistent with seasonality carrying part of it. Figure~\ref{fig:ccf} plots the full cross-correlation functions for all three estimators. Extending this input-timing diagnosis to the cohort level confirms the residual is structural rather than compositional: across the six 30-year coupon cohorts spanning the SOMA low-coupon mass (2.0--4.5\%), every simulated cohort CPR tracks the contemporaneous rate change with near-mechanical strength (first-difference correlation between $-0.87$ and $-0.94$; five of six cohorts cross-correlation phase-locked at lag~0, the 4.5\% coupon peaking at lag $-6$ with a weaker $r=-0.29$, and reproducing the aggregate $-0.318$), whereas no realized cohort speed, measured independently in the Freddie 2017--2021 performance panel and in the Fannie replication cohorts, shows a rate response distinguishable from zero at any lag within $\pm 6$ months (Freddie lag-0 first-difference correlations of $-0.05$ to $-0.16$, Fannie $-0.03$ to $-0.14$, all inside the $\pm 2/\sqrt{n}$ band, and a maximum magnitude of $0.34$ over all cohorts and all lags, with no coherent sign or lag). The aggregate empirical non-response is not a composition artifact, and the simulated lead is not a datable phase shift at the cohort level. One honest scope limit travels with this exhibit: realized cohort-level monthly speeds exist only in the loan-level agency panels that anchor Path B, not in the SOMA holdings from which the aggregate empirical CPR is backed out, so the cohort split is run in the Path B universe rather than on the SOMA book itself, which is why the estimator-level timing falsification above stays at the SOMA aggregate. A cumulative counterpart completes this exhibit (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation}). Because monthly differences are seasonality-dominated, the cross-section is also read in cumulated levels: over the window, the age-matched Freddie panel's realized prepayment runs from 9.44\% annualized CPR (28.1\% of balance cumulatively) in the shallowest surviving gap bucket ($[-1,0)$ points, mean coupon 6.02\%) down to 5.24\% (16.4\%) at gaps of $-3$ points and deeper (mean coupon 3.37\%): a gradient of $+4.20$ CPR points, with a stratum-cluster 95\% interval of $[+3.59, +4.66]$ and a one-sided permutation $p$ of 0.005---the one realized-data exhibit in this paper that moves in the lock-in direction. It is not corroboration of the imported elasticity's magnitude: the production hazard's implied gradient across the same buckets is $+0.94$ points at the central $\beta_1$, so the realized gradient is roughly $4.5\times$ the model's, and the excess carries everything else that travels with coupon across cohorts---credit composition, within-window refinancing on the high-coupon shallow buckets, residual seasoning past the age cut. Detection power for the implied gradient is 0.68: the design could have resolved $\beta_1$'s implication, and what it found instead was larger and confounded. I therefore read the cross-section as a signed, significant, composition-confounded level gradient, consistent with lock-in operating but unusable as a measurement of its size, and the monthly-timing nulls above stand unchanged.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig2_ccf}
\caption{Cross-correlation functions between each estimator's simulated CPR path and the empirical SOMA-derived path, lags $-6$ to $+6$. Under the implemented convention (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), a peak at negative lag means the empirical series leads and the simulated path trails. Path B's $-3$/$-4$ peak and Path A's and the ABM's negative contemporaneous correlations summarize the same fact from Table~\ref{tab:estimators}: no estimator shows positive contemporaneous alignment, and once the window's shared trend is removed no estimator's co-movement is distinguishable from zero under the moving-block bands.}
\label{fig:ccf}
\end{figure}

A moving-block bootstrap (block length 6, 4,000 replications) puts the 95\% interval on the peak-lag correlation at $[-0.21, +0.49]$ and on the lag-0 correlation at $[-0.13, +0.44]$; with seven lags searched, individual band-point differences in peak $r$ carry no evidential weight. These intervals are not sensitive to the block-length choice in any verdict-relevant way: at block lengths 4 and 8 the peak-lag endpoints move to $[-0.27, +0.39]$ and $[-0.22, +0.51]$, and the lag-0 endpoints to $[-0.11, +0.45]$ and $[-0.17, +0.42]$, every interval still containing zero.

A Theil decomposition formalizes where each estimator's monthly error lives; it is a fit diagnostic, not a timing credential. On levels, the ABM's mean-squared error is 68\% bias share (the level failure already visible in its aggregate), while Path B's is 6.7\% bias and 86.6\% variance share (its residual monthly error is under-dispersion against a seasonal empirical path, not level bias), with Path A intermediate (26.5\% bias, 63.0\% covariance share); after linear detrending the variance share still dominates for Path B (93.7\%). On first differences, Theil's $U_2$ is 1.23 for the ABM, 1.01 for Path A, and 1.00 for Path B: no estimator beats a naive no-change forecast of month-over-month CPR changes, which restates the timing concession above in forecast-accuracy units. The full decomposition by transform, with cumulative dollar-error profiles whose terminal values reproduce Table~\ref{tab:estimators}'s aggregates by construction, is collected in Table~\ref{tab:theil}.

\subsection{Identification: the Marginal over the Null}\label{sec:identification}

The design does not identify the aggregate recovery level: the level is floor-dominated, and the $\beta_1 = 0$ null (the mechanical model with the lock-in elasticity switched off) already recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark on the shared accounting basis at the off-window floor this paper headlines, and 88.7\% at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}; standalone figures in Table~\ref{tab:bases}). What it identifies is the \emph{marginal} of the central Path B run over that null, writing $R$ for recovered trapped liquidity,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:marginal}
\text{lock-in marginal} \;=\; R_{\text{central}} - R_{\beta_1 = 0},
\end{equation}
which is basis-invariant because the shared-accounting netting is common to both legs (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). Its value moves with the involuntary-turnover floor, and because that floor is the design's dominant level-setter and is calibrated in-window on the very cohorts the marginal decomposes, the headline value is the one obtained with the floor measured off that population: $+\$42.6$ billion, $+5.6$ points of the benchmark, at the defensible off-window floor of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}, and $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across that floor's range. At the in-window production calibration the same difference is $+\$70.3$ billion, or $+9.2$ points. The recovery \emph{level} moves with the floor for the same reason the marginal does, and I now report it at the same two calibrations rather than quoting a level from one floor beside a marginal from another: the central leg recovers 91.3\% of the benchmark on the shared basis at the off-window floor (88.2--93.7\% across its range) against 97.9\% at the in-window calibration, and the null 85.7\% against 88.7\%. Only the central and null legs were re-run off window, so those are the two levels quoted at both calibrations; the concave-gap variant was also re-run there, at 99.8\% central against a 94.8\% null on the standalone scorer; every remaining estimator level in this paper---Path~A, the full-book variant, the composed estimator, the covariate-prior and elasticity bands, the Ginnie overlay and the Danish legs---was run only in window, and any level appearing without a floor label should be read at the in-window 4.0\% calibration. I call the in-window configuration the \emph{in-sample calibration point} throughout. Differencing does not, however, immunize the marginal against non-stationary baselines: because the baseline multiplies the very rate-gap channel the null switches off, a structural break in $h_0$ scales the marginal rather than cancelling from it. Re-running both legs under a $\pm 20\%$ multiplicative break from January 2023 onward, imposed identically on both legs and fitting nothing to the benchmark, carries the marginal from $+5.0$ to $+12.1$ points around the committed $+9.2$ (reproduced bit-exactly at the unit factor), while a $\pm 20\%$ break in the involuntary floor alone moves it the other way, to $+6.6$ through $+10.4$ points. Every perturbation leaves the marginal inside the calibration box and positive. What survives a baseline break is the box membership, not the point value. Three qualifications fix its evidential content. First, the identified content is the marginal's \emph{bounded range}, together with the monotone response across the Liebersohn--Rothstein band, and neither its point magnitude nor its sign: the box spans $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points, and across the empirically plausible floor range the marginal spans $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points over the full band, or $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points at the central elasticity, moving to $+11.25$ points under the competing-risks floor form. The share of the sample the floor censors moves with its level: at the 4.991\% off-window floor the paper headlines, the central leg's hazard is pinned to the involuntary floor in 68.8\% of the 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months, against 36.3\% at the in-sample calibration point and 60.0\% to 77.3\% across that floor's defensible range. In most evaluated loan-months at the headline calibration the elasticity therefore no longer sets the hazard, and its contribution to the marginal there is truncated at the floor rather than carried in full; these are unweighted loan-month counts, not balance-weighted shares, so they do not say what fraction of the dollar marginal the uncensored months carry. The $\beta_1 = 0$ leg is pinned less often---35.8\% of loan-months at the headline floor and 14.3\% at the in-sample calibration point---and wherever it is pinned, switching the elasticity off cannot raise the hazard at all. The marginal is thus floor-level sensitive under the production max form, while the form-robust statement is roughly $+9$ to $+11$ points at the production floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}); no form-robust point exists at the off-window anchors, where the two forms return $+5.6$ and $+11.2$, so the defensible statement there is the form-conditional range $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points. Its upper end comes from the additive form at the 4.695\% floor and the band's 7.7\% high edge, whose central leg recovers 61.2\% of the benchmark, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} states that it should not be read as an equally credentialed member of the range. That hull is conditional on each floor read being taken at its point estimate, and it is therefore not the paper's widest disclosed interval: propagating the floor reads' own cluster-bootstrap sampling error puts the max-form member at $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points after wild-cluster correction (the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) and leaves it open below $+4.3$ on an age-standardized read (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). The hull's lower edge is thus narrower than the layer the paper calls binding, and the two must be quoted together rather than the hull alone. The floor's provenance now has an out-of-agency read as well as an out-of-window one, and it does not narrow the range. Applying the committed off-window selection rule unchanged to the Fannie Mae cohort-month panel---the same fourteen columns, the same 2017 start, staged for the external replication of Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie}---returns 5.52\% on the headline cell against Freddie's 5.19\%, on 421 cohort-months against 438 (run \texttt{fannie\_floor\_read}; the Freddie panel replays every committed cell of that grid through the identical code path first). That is above the clean band's 5.334\% top, and it sits 0.01 points from the age-standardized read of 5.51\% that the same table already flags. Two independent lines---one an imputation-heavy age standardization on Freddie, the other a direct read on a second agency's book---therefore point to the same place, and both point above the band. Read against the committed floor-to-marginal grid, a 5.52\% floor brackets the marginal between the 5.334\% row's $+4.27$ points and the 6.0\% row's $+2.28$, that is, below the $+4.3$ lower edge this paper quotes. I do not restate the headline on the Fannie floor, because the production calibration is the Freddie one and the two books differ in composition as well as in this read; what the out-of-agency read establishes is that the band's \emph{lower} edge is the soft one, which is the direction the age-standardized caveat already pointed. Because that sensitivity is what makes the floor's provenance decisive, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} re-measures the floor outside the evaluation window and re-runs both legs there: the marginal lands at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points across the defensible off-window range, inside the box and well clear of either edge, and stays positive at all twenty-seven floor-by-band cells run. The covariate-prior swing of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} is also comparable in size to the marginal itself. That the marginal is positive in all twenty-seven cells needs a word of its own, because it reads as the robust identified content, and is not. Over the forty window months the cohort panel covers (June 2022 through September 2025; the panel ends two months before the window does) the exposure-weighted mean coupon on the surviving book is 3.134\%, with 97.40\% of exposure at or below 4.79\% and 99.53\% at or below 5.09\%, against a window-minimum 30-year rate of 5.2311\% (\texttt{sign\_forcing\_stats}). The two uncovered months cannot overturn this: the book is closed at 2017--2021 originations, so no higher-coupon loan can enter it, and the out-of-the-money share can only hold or rise as the low-coupon cohorts season. Essentially the entire book is out of the money for essentially the entire window, so the rate gap is negative in every cell of every sweep reported here, and a specification in which a more negative gap suppresses prepayment cannot return a negative marginal at any floor or band in the box. The sign is fixed by the rate configuration rather than by the estimated elasticity. The twenty-seven-cell check accordingly verifies the specification---it would catch a wiring error that flipped a coefficient or crossed the central and null legs, and that is worth having---rather than identifying the mechanism, and I retain it on that footing. This is why the reported range, and not the sign, carries the paper's identified content. Second, timing carries no credential: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null also peaks at lag $-3$ (Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}), so the lead cannot discriminate the elasticity from the mechanical model. Third, the marginal's sampling precision is not the operative uncertainty: the stratified loan-level bootstrap puts the paired marginal at $[+9.17, +9.23]$ points (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so the calibration box, not simulation noise, governs the interval.

A fourth qualification concerns composition. This subsection's decomposition exhibits are computed at the in-sample calibration point, so the total they partition is the $+\$70.3$ billion figure; the composition findings are shares and intensities, which the floor's level shifts far less than it shifts the total. A group-ablation decomposition of the marginal itself (zeroing $\beta_1$ cell by cell over a vintage $\times$ coupon grid, with everything else held at production) shows the $+\$70.3$ billion is broad-based rather than cohort-specific: all fifteen cells contribute positively, the cells sum to within 1.03\% of the paired-run total, no single cell contributes more than 21\%, and the 2020--2021 vintages carry 72\% of the marginal on 73\% of sample balance, with per-balance intensity ranging from $0.82\times$ to $1.75\times$. Extending the same group-ablation engine to the dimensions that grid does not cover, the marginal is broad-based on all three: every cell of the FICO ($<$680 / 680--740 / 740$+$), LTV ($\leq$80 / $>$80), and Census-region partitions contributes positively, the per-grid cell sums reproduce the $+\$70.3$ billion total within interaction residuals of 0.8, 0.4, and 0.5 percent, and every cell's per-balance intensity lies in 0.88--1.26, tighter than the vintage $\times$ coupon grid's span. That verdict does not, on its own, discriminate, and I say so rather than leave the reader to assume otherwise: running the identical engine and the identical pre-committed decision rule over twelve random three-way partitions of the same 75,000 loans returns \emph{broad-based} in all twelve, on per-balance intensities of 0.995--1.007 and additivity residuals of 0.49 percent (\texttt{subgroup\_marginals\_placebo}). A partition with no economic content earns the label, so the label alone carries no evidential weight. What the comparison does establish runs in the opposite direction. The random partitions' intensities concentrate on unity --- the widest of the twelve spans 0.011 --- while the economic groupings span 0.381, more than thirty times wider. The informative statement is therefore not that these cells are homogeneous, which chance alone delivers, but that they disperse considerably more than chance: the tilts below are structure, not sampling noise, and the generality claim rests on the sign homogeneity and on the bounded magnitude of that dispersion rather than on the verdict label. The mild tilts run toward below-740-FICO ($1.23\times$--$1.26\times$) and above-80-LTV ($1.23\times$) borrowers, with regional intensities spanning only 0.93--1.06. The marginal is a book-wide property of the rate-gap distribution interacting with seasoning and the floor; Figure~\ref{fig:marginalcells} maps the cells. The benchmark-basis question is likewise answered by direct computation: Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}'s expectations-based complement shows the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection already implied 75.6--88.5\% of the realized cap-shortfall. The 0.2-point distance between the 88.5\% upper end and the null's 88.7\% benchmark-consistent recovery is not corroboration: the coincidence is doubly conditional, holding only at the in-sample 4.0\% floor \emph{and} under the uniform-spread allocation, which Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} itself judges the less faithful of the two constructions. At the headline off-window floor the null recovers 85.7\%, 2.8 points \emph{below} the uniform-spread projection rather than 0.2 above it; against the settlement-aware allocation's 75.6\% the null sits 13.2 points above in-sample and 10.1 points above at the headline floor. Two quantities that move by several points under either condition cannot be reported as converging, so I report the projection range and the null separately and draw no agreement claim from their proximity. Every percentage-point magnitude quoted for this decomposition is a cap-basis quantity, with dollars and rankings basis-invariant.

The decomposition also carries its time axis. The monthly marginal path (the central-minus-null difference of the two committed simulated legs, whose sum reproduces the $+\$70.3$ billion bit-exactly) is positive in all 42 window months, splits 21.3\%/41.9\%/36.8\% across the window's thirds, and peaks at \$2.29 billion in November 2023, the window's eighteenth month. By coupon bucket the terminal marginal divides 38.0\%/43.7\%/18.3\% across the $<3.0\%$/3.0--4.0\%/$\geq 4.0\%$ groups on 40.3\%/40.4\%/19.3\% of sample balance; Figure~\ref{fig:marginaltiming} draws the stack. Because neither simulated leg carries calendar-month seasonality, seasonal structure enters these exhibits only through the empirical comparison, and per the pre-committed posture of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} they are fit diagnostics, not timing credentials.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig12_marginal_timing.png}
\caption{Monthly lock-in marginal by coupon bucket. Stacked areas are the per-bucket monthly marginals from the month-by-cell capture of the group-ablation engine; the dark line is the aggregate central-minus-null path, whose sum reproduces the $+\$70.3$ billion in-sample marginal bit-exactly. Dotted rules mark the window's thirds; the marker is the November 2023 peak. The stack differs from the aggregate by the decomposition's 1.03\% terminal additivity residual, asserted in the generator.}
\label{fig:marginaltiming}
\end{figure}

A fifth qualification states what kind of claim ``identifies'' makes. The marginal is a within-model counterfactual decomposition: the difference between two runs of a fully disclosed hazard structure that differ only in the elasticity term. It is not causal identification in the econometric sense: no quasi-experimental variation in rate gaps across otherwise-comparable loans is exploited anywhere in this paper, and none is claimed. The causal content of $\beta_1$ itself is inherited rather than asserted: the elasticity is imported unmodified from \citepos{liebersohn2024} estimated band, with \citet{fonseca2024} as independent corroboration, and no Path B parameter is estimated on the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). What the switch-off adds is accounting, at a stated price: the marginal inherits any misspecification of the held-fixed structure, which is why the channels I can name (floor level and functional form, the rate-gap transform, the covariate priors) are each swept directly (Sections~\ref{sec:pathb} and \ref{sec:robustness-floor}), and why Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} reports calibration, not sampling, as the operative uncertainty.

A sixth qualification concerns three readings I decline in three separate places. Path A's fitted rate-gap coefficient is $+0.29$ per 100 bp against the imported $\beta_1$'s $0.069$, though the two are on different scales and I compare them on sign rather than magnitude (Section~\ref{sec:patha}); the realized cross-cohort gap gradient runs at roughly $4.5\times$ the gradient the central $\beta_1$ implies (run \texttt{episode\_confrontation}); and the real-covariate cross-design variant recovers 59.3\% of the benchmark, 76.3\% once reweighted to the book's own composition, against the production ABM's 13.6\% seed mean (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}). I stand by each reason for declining: the first is not unit-comparable and does not survive a bias-respecting sign test, the second is composition-confounded, the third is a recovery level, which this design does not identify, and the same reweight moves the frozen cross-design variant down to 12.6\%. Stated together rather than three sections apart: none of the three points toward a smaller lock-in channel. I keep the imported value for the reason Section~\ref{sec:pathb} gives---Path B is a structural verification exercise whose elasticity is estimated in a different setting and never touches this benchmark, so re-fitting it to any in-sample reading would end that exercise---and among the literature estimates available the adopted band is already the conservative one, since \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor puts the marginal at $+11.5$ points against the central calibration's $+9.2$ at the production floor. Other disclosed variants move the marginal the other way, the Ginnie-speed overlay to $+4.4$ points and the age-standardized floor read to $+3.8$ (Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty}), so I do not read the three as a case for a larger headline.

A seventh qualification assembles those readings against the ones that run the other way, because this paper states each correction where it arises and never lines them up, and a reader who lines them up sees something the sections do not show one at a time. Holding the production hard-maximum form and the imported elasticity fixed, every correction to the floor or to the accounting basis moves the headline down. The in-sample calibration returns $+9.2$ points; re-anchoring the floor outside the window returns the headline $+5.6$; the Ginnie-speed overlay at that same floor returns $+4.4$; the age-standardized floor read of 5.51\% implies a marginal near $+3.8$; and the independent Fannie Mae read of the same off-window cell, 5.52\%, brackets the marginal below the $+4.3$ edge of the clean band (above). Carrying the overlay's $0.797$ scaling up to the age-standardized floor, a composition neither run establishes, would put the marginal near $+3.0$. I report no composed point, because the one joint cell this paper has run for two adjustments to the marginal returned an interaction of $-1.47$ points (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}): corrections here do not compose additively, and a composed figure would carry a precision nothing in the design supports. Nor are they independent of each other. The floor-level corrections and the censoring share are one mechanism read twice, since raising the floor is what crowds the elasticity out: at the 4.991\% headline floor the central leg is already pinned to the floor in 68.8\% of evaluated loan-months, against 36.3\% at the in-sample calibration point, so a further increase in the floor has progressively less of the hazard left to act on. That share is not a statement that the estimate rests on a third of the data, and it should not be read as one: a pinned month's contribution to the marginal is truncated at the floor rather than erased, the $\beta_1 = 0$ leg is pinned in only 35.8\% of loan-months at the same floor so the two legs still separate wherever the null clears it, and these are unweighted loan-month counts rather than balance-weighted shares, so nothing follows from them about what fraction of the dollar marginal the uncensored months carry. What the assembly settles is where in the interval the mass sits, not whether the finding holds. Within the production form the binding layer is the floor reads' own sampling error, $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points after wild-cluster correction of the percentile read's under-coverage (run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction}; the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$); the headline sits just below that interval's midpoint, while every correction listed above falls in its lower half and the uncomposed pair falls near its bottom. I therefore read $+5.6$ as a middle member of an interval whose measured corrections concentrate below it, and the range rather than the point as what the design delivers---with the form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points remaining the wider statement, since the form dimension is not a floor correction and is not disciplined by this list. Three things stop the list from becoming a retraction. The interval does not contain zero, and the sign is fixed by the window's rate configuration rather than by any of these calibrations, as the sign-forcing argument above establishes, so nothing here bears on the direction of the result. The two specification choices that are not floor corrections both run the other way: the additive form returns $+11.2$ points at the same off-window anchors, nearly floor-invariantly, and it is what sets the hull's upper end, while \citepos{fonseca2024} anchor would put the marginal at $+11.5$ points at the production floor against the adopted band's $+9.2$. And the three readings of the preceding paragraph point to a larger lock-in channel, not a smaller one. I decline those three for the reasons given there, and I decline to compose these for the reason given here: the identified content is the range, and the corrections concentrate in its lower half.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{fig11_marginal_decomposition}
\caption{Group-ablation decomposition of the lock-in marginal over the vintage $\times$ coupon grid (production configuration; each cell's $\beta_1$ zeroed with everything else held fixed). All fifteen cells contribute positively; the 2020--2021 rows carry 72\% of the cell sum on 73\% of sample balance, and no cell exceeds 21\%. Cell values and shares are read from the committed decomposition artifact, whose additivity gate bounds the cells' sum within 1.03\% of the paired-run \$70.3 billion in-sample total.}
\label{fig:marginalcells}
\end{figure}

Table~\ref{tab:uncertainty} consolidates the three uncertainty layers that attach to the headline level and marginal (sampling, simulation seed, and calibration) on both accounting bases. The hierarchy it displays is the one argued above: sampling error is negligible at the paper's reporting precision, the draw-seed contribution is smaller still (under \$0.01 billion across ten paired reruns), and the operative uncertainty on every headline quantity is calibration.

Percentile cluster bootstraps are known to under-cover at cluster counts in that range; run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction} reports the corrections for the floor read (CR2/CR3 $t(30)$ intervals and a Rademacher wild-cluster bootstrap-$t$), and the headline row quotes the wild-$t$ interval as the binding layer. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor} diagnoses the Jensen mechanism responsible. \begin{tablenotes}[flushleft]\scriptsize
\item A prior fact frames the whole table. The engine renormalises the simulated pool to realized Fed holdings every month, so a leg's dollar roll-off is a \emph{rate} applied to an exogenous level and its balance path cannot drift from the realized one. No recovery percentage here is a balance-path fit, and none can compound error across months: each is close to a restatement of that leg's window-mean CPR against the empirical 5.14\%. That is why the sampling intervals are as tight as they are, and it is a limit on what any of these levels can corroborate. The final column is each leg's terminal cumulative runoff error, simulated minus realized roll-off, and it is the sign-transparent statement of the same information: recovery is an affine transform of it, $\text{recovery} = 1 + \text{error}/764.7$, so a large additive constant sits inside every percentage in the first four columns and makes all of them look better. Two things the percentages hide. Path~B's in-sample 107.0\% is an \emph{over}-prediction of trapped liquidity of \$53.8 billion, which is an 8.2\% \emph{under}-prediction of the \$652.8 billion of runoff actually realized---the referent flips even though the map does not. And the headline off-window central leg, at 91.3\% shared, carries the smallest error of any leg here on the standalone scorer: $+\$2.8$ billion, 0.4\% of realized runoff. Errors as a share of realized runoff: $+8.2\%$, $-2.5\%$, $+0.4\%$, $-6.1\%$, $+25.1\%$, $+6.7\%$ down the rows. The lock-in marginal is unaffected by any of this, being a difference of two trapped aggregates from which the constant cancels; that is the same fact as its basis-invariance. Standalone and shared are related by the flat 9.1-percentage-point curtailment wedge of Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} (\$69.56 billion over the window), derived in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}. Path B's central elasticity band is 105.9--108.2\% standalone (96.8--99.1\% shared); the covariate-prior band is 92.2\%--99.2\% shared (approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% composed). The lock-in marginal is $+9.2$ points ($+\$70.3$ billion) on the U.S. leg at the in-sample calibration point, and $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion) at the off-window floor (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}); both are basis-invariant because the netting is common to the central and null legs. The off-window rows carry the same flat netting: the layer is a constant curtailment flow over a constant cap benchmark, identical across all five committed estimators spanning 29.6 points of standalone recovery, so it is path-invariant and therefore floor-invariant (\texttt{calibration\_reconciliation}). Full-book and composed columns were run only at the in-sample calibration and are not extrapolated to the off-window floor. Path A, the concave-gap variant, the elasticity band, and the covariate-prior band are all in-sample-calibration quantities. The Danish leg's counterfactual image, run at the production floor and so comparable to the in-sample point, is $+\$61.2$ billion, or 8.0 points, the difference reflecting the Danish leg's dynamic-balance compounding (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). The ABM rows of Table~\ref{tab:estimators} run through the accounting layer natively (Figure~\ref{fig:recovery}) and are not restated here.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The two hazard paths, despite sharing no household-level decision machinery with the ABM, together land closer to the empirical benchmark (97.9\% and 112.4\% of it on the benchmark-consistent shared accounting basis of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}, 107.0\% and 121.5\% under their standalone scorers, both at the in-sample calibration point; Path B is 91.3\% shared at the off-window floor the paper headlines, and Path A was not re-run there) than any synthetic-population ABM specification tested in this paper, including the falsified burnout attempt. Overshooting less, however, is not validation: Path A overshoots the benchmark by \$164.1 billion---its terminal cumulative runoff error, and a 25.1\% over-prediction of the \$652.8 billion of runoff actually realized---with a monthly path fit no better than the ABM's, and Path B's recovery, reported across its full elasticity sensitivity band (\$809.9--\$827.7 billion, 105.9\%--108.2\%; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), reflects an elasticity imported unmodified from \citet{liebersohn2024} rather than one fit to this paper's own benchmark, an import whose verification content Section~\ref{sec:pathb} limits to the marginal's bounded interval and the band's monotone response. The comparative claim I defend is therefore narrower than ``the hazard framework fits'', and narrower again than ``lands within 2.1\% of the benchmark'': that 2.1-point miss belongs to the in-sample calibration point, and quoting it beside a marginal measured at the off-window floor mixes two calibrations. At the calibration this paper headlines Path B (a loan-level survival structure with no household-utility content) recovers 91.3\% of the aggregate benchmark on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis, a miss of 8.7 points, and 88.2--93.7\% across the off-window floor's defensible range, a miss of 6.3 to 11.8 points; the 2.1-point miss (97.9\%, and within 0.05\% composed) is the in-sample figure, retained as the model-fit diagnostic it is, with the ex-ante covariate priors spanning 92.2\%--99.2\% and approximately 94.3--101.3\% composed at that same in-sample calibration and not re-run off-window. What survives the reconciliation is an order-of-magnitude comparative statement rather than a precision one. Path A, whose forward simulation carries no useful propagated precision and whose own rate-gap coefficient is not statistically distinguishable from zero under any bias-respecting construction (Section~\ref{sec:patha}), lands at 112.4\% under a specification that shares data but no estimated parameters. I read that coincidence as order-of-magnitude agreement and nothing stronger: the same resampling pass puts Path A's mean at 78.3\% of the benchmark with a percentile interval spanning zero, so it cannot corroborate a sign. No synthetic-population household-choice specification I tested lands within 45\% of it. I attach no timing credential to this: Path B's raw $+0.190$ lag-0 correlation is carried by the window's shared trend (in first differences and after detrending the co-movement is indistinguishable from zero; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), and the moving-block intervals of Table~\ref{tab:estimators} caution against weighing any single timing coefficient, including Path B's lag $-3$ peak, whose interval spans zero. Timing alone, however, does not identify the lock-in channel: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null also peaks at lag $-3$ while still recovering 88.7\% of the benchmark on the shared basis at the in-sample calibration point and 85.7\% at the off-window floor (Table~\ref{tab:bases}). The elasticity's identified contribution is its marginal recovery over that null, defined and bounded once in Section~\ref{sec:identification}; the design identifies the marginal and the band's monotone response, not the lag structure. At the in-sample calibration point, across 10 paired reruns with different draw seeds (central and null sharing each seed), the marginal is \$70.3 billion and varies by less than \$0.01 billion, because the production engine drains prepayments fractionally and the draw seed touches only the small delinquency channel; the uncertainty that matters for the marginal is the calibration band and the structural assumptions, not simulation noise. I restrict this claim to synthetic-population specifications: Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} reports a real-covariate cross-design variant of the ABM that does land within 45\% of the benchmark (59.3\%, and 76.3\% once that covariate population is reweighted to the book's own coupon $\times$ vintage composition, which is within 24\%), a result that complicates rather than confirms the paradigm interpretation this paragraph would otherwise support---and the reweighted reading makes the complication larger, not smaller.

Because the hazard framework's only behavioral content is loan-level covariates, a cohort-level burnout term, and literature-calibrated elasticities (with no analogue to mobility desire, loss aversion, or utility maximization), its comparative success might be read as evidence for the loan-level and institutional mechanisms (heterogeneity in FICO, LTV, and geography; servicer forbearance and pipeline delay; and cohort-level burnout) that the synthetic-population ABM of Section~\ref{sec:abm} does not represent. The paper's own measurements cap that reading: the named mechanisms ablate to a point or less each (burnout 0.86 points at the in-sample floor and 0.45 at the off-window floor, FICO/LTV 1.3 points, the delinquency pipeline \$0.39 billion, with no servicer channel beyond that pipeline modeled), the synthetic companion of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis} recovers a comparable aggregate level with no real loan-level structure at all, and the data-source asymmetry of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} remains unresolved---so what the level supports is the mechanical decomposition, with the named mechanisms bounded small and remaining candidates only for the residual. The scale-sensitivity replication in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} rules out the ABM/hazard sample-size difference as the driver of this gap; the data-source difference is addressed only partially by the cross-design test in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}, which finds that endowing the ABM with real structural covariates changes its explanatory share substantially: enough, under one calibration choice, to raise the question of whether ``cannot represent'' is the right description at all. The rate-gap coefficient's sign stability across both hazard specifications does not indicate that the underlying par-payoff penalty is doing real work, and I restate it. Only Path A estimates that coefficient; Path B imports it from \citet{liebersohn2024} as a signed elasticity band, so it cannot come out the other way, and an agreement between one estimate and one import that has no freedom to disagree is an orientation check on the two specifications' unit and sign conventions, not corroboration. It is worth running---it is what would catch a transposed sign between the paths, and it passes---but the evidence that the penalty has a real effect is the external literature; the estimated Path A coefficient is directionally consistent without being statistically distinguishable from zero under bias-respecting constructions (Section~\ref{sec:patha}). Household rationality is not irrelevant, but the aggregation structure of loan-level survival is what a model of this shortfall must get right at minimum, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} leaves open how much more it must get right. This sharpens the trade-off named in this paper's title, with the qualification Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} adds: the price of the TBA market's homogeneity appears to be paid at least in part through loan-level and institutional frictions that the synthetic-population household model does not represent and that a market-value repurchase design, as Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s counterfactual suggests, would substantially relieve.

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}

Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} presented the paper's central contrast: household simulation, rational or behaviorally extended, does not close the gap to the empirical benchmark, while a structurally distinct hazard framework (discarding household utility altogether, on a substantially larger and non-synthetic sample) recovers 91.3\% of it on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis in its microsimulation form at the off-window floor this paper headlines (88.2--93.7\% across that floor's range, a miss of 6.3 to 11.8 points), and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point, with the caveat that no estimator's monthly path co-movement, Path B's included, survives removal of the window's shared trend (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The contrast is easy to over-read. The $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% on the same basis at the headline floor (88.7\% in-sample), so the components common to any prepayment model (scheduled amortization, the seasoning baseline, and the involuntary-turnover floor) deliver the large majority of the level; the caps sat far above what any plausible prepayment environment would have produced. The named loan-level mechanisms are measured small wherever I can ablate them: switching burnout off moves Path B by \$6.58 billion ($0.86$ points) at the in-sample floor and \$3.48 billion ($0.45$ points) at the off-window floor this paragraph's other figures use, zeroing the FICO/LTV covariates moves it $1.3$ points, and the delinquency transition matrix is bounded at \$0.39 billion (Appendix~\ref{app:params}); no servicer channel beyond that pipeline is modeled at all. Three mechanisms therefore remain \emph{candidates} for the residual---modest at the in-sample calibration (2.1 points) but 8.7 points, roughly 1.6$\times$ the headline marginal itself, at the off-window calibration the paper headlines---and for the timing failure every estimator shares, not demonstrated drivers of the level; servicer behavior beyond the delinquency pipeline, updated-equity dynamics, and home-equity extraction carry no bound of any kind here, and each is plausibly rate-cycle-correlated. First, loan-level heterogeneity in credit score, geography, and life circumstance means that mobility decisions are not a smooth function of coupon and market rate alone; the hazard framework's 295 stratum fixed effects are one mechanism that lets Path A track pool-level heterogeneity the ABM's coupon-cohort buckets cannot represent. Second, servicer behavior (loss mitigation, forbearance, and loan modification practices that vary by servicer and were unusually active during the higher-rate, higher-delinquency-risk period of the QT window) operates on prepayment and default outcomes through channels outside a borrower-side decision model; Path B's explicit competing-risks structure and Markov delinquency pipeline are a direct, if partial, representation of this channel. Third, prepayment burnout is representable at the cohort level (as in both hazard paths) but not within a closed, per-agent household population, as Section~\ref{sec:abm-burnout}'s falsified survivor-selection experiment showed.

\subsection{The Dual Systemic Fallout}\label{sec:discussion-fallout}

While the macroeconomic literature frequently focuses on the housing mobility implications of the lock-in effect, its transmission into the fixed-income sector creates a two-pronged systemic risk.

The first channel runs through the central bank itself: the extension of MBS durations deprives the SOMA of its scheduled cash flows, breaking the predictability required for effective Quantitative Tightening; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-wal} expresses this extension in approximate weighted-average-life units. The \$764.7 billion shortfall-versus-cap is, per the decomposition above, mostly mechanical: a gap to a cap schedule that no plausible prepayment environment would have met. The duration extension itself (a 9.4-year portfolio WAL under the empirical path against 3.4 years at 2021 realized speeds, Section~\ref{sec:robustness-wal}, where the 3.4-year comparator is a refinancing-boom baseline) is the rate-driven object, and this extension, not the cap arithmetic, feeds the fragility channels below.

The second channel runs through private banks, which, unlike the Fed, must mark their portfolios to market. The structural duration extension of these low-coupon MBS assets generated hundreds of billions in unrecognized losses across the banking sector. \citet{jiang2023} show that duration extension alone was not sufficient to precipitate failure; rather, it was the interaction of mark-to-market losses with a high share of uninsured deposits that made specific regional institutions vulnerable to self-fulfilling runs, as uninsured depositors withdrew funds upon recognizing that reported asset values no longer covered liabilities. The duration extension that is a necessary precondition for this fragility channel is generated by the below-market coupon stock meeting rate normalization under any payoff rule---the rule-only counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} shortens the portfolio by only 0.6 of the roughly six extension-years (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-wal})---so the elasticity channel this paper identifies is a second-order contributor to that precondition, and neither it nor the extension is, on its own, a sufficient condition for bank failure.

Beyond its transmission into fixed-income markets, the lock-in effect imposes a real economic cost by preventing households from relocating toward higher-productivity labor markets. \citet{hsieh2019} estimate that housing supply constraints in high-productivity U.S. cities impose substantial aggregate output losses through this kind of spatial misallocation of labor; their headline magnitudes are cumulative level effects over 1964--2009 and were revised in a published correction, so I cite the mechanism rather than a point estimate. While their analysis concerns supply-side constraints on housing availability, the demand-side mobility constraint I quantify here operates through the same channel: households who cannot economically justify relocating cannot respond to geographic differences in labor demand, regardless of housing supply elsewhere. I do not translate the \$764.7 billion shortfall into welfare terms: it is an accounting gap against a cap schedule, mostly mechanical by the decomposition above, and neither a lower nor an upper bound on the welfare cost of the par-payoff rule. The welfare-relevant objects are the constrained moves themselves, whose fiscal image is the lock-in marginal ($+\$42.6$ billion of trapped roll-off at the headline off-window floor, $+\$70.3$ billion at the in-sample calibration point), and the forgone labor-reallocation gains this literature identifies, which my accounting framework does not measure.

\subsection{Evaluating Alternative Securitization Policies}\label{sec:discussion-policy}

In response to the mobility collapse, U.S. policymakers, including the Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), proposed expanding assumable and portable mortgages. Fixed-income analysis suggests these proposals address only part of the problem: while portability eases consumer mobility constraints, it explicitly prevents the loan from prepaying upon relocation. The same holds for assumability, with one added wrinkle: an assumed loan also fails to prepay (the below-market coupon transfers to the buyer and the extension stays on the investor's book), and the buyer must separately finance the gap between the purchase price and the assumed balance, typically at prevailing rates, which limits take-up. Both instruments therefore relocate rather than eliminate duration extension risk, solving the microeconomic mobility constraint while cementing the macroeconomic extension risk onto the investor's balance sheet.

A second lever, which I flag as speculative design space rather than a finding this paper's estimates support, operates on the policy instrument rather than the mortgage contract: the redemption cap itself. The shortfall I measure is defined against a fixed \$35 billion monthly cap that locked-in prepayment behavior predictably cannot reach, so a cap indexed to the observable state of the outstanding stock (for example, to the share of the book sitting deeply below market coupon), or a standing policy of substituting Treasury runoff or outright MBS sales when passive runoff undershoots, would convert extension risk from a silent shortfall into an explicit, priced policy choice. The decomposition supplies the ex-ante arithmetic such a design would need: both components of the $\beta_1 = 0$ null (scheduled amortization from the book's coupon, age, and term composition, and baseline involuntary turnover at any assumed floor) are computable in advance of the cycle, and the null's recovery of the realized shortfall (88.7\% at the in-sample calibration point, 85.7\% at the off-window floor) measures how far the fixed \$35 billion cap sat above that mechanical path, a state of the world the New York Fed's September 2022 projection of \$20--\$30 billion in monthly principal payments already implied at the caps' effective date \citep{nyfed2022}. A cap intended to bind would be set at or below the book's projected scheduled-plus-involuntary principal path, with the identified elasticity margin ($+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points of the shortfall across the calibration box) as the behavioral band around it. The New York Fed's May 2022 baseline path supplies an ex-ante test: it implied agency MBS runoff of \$740.6 billion over the window against the caps' \$1{,}417.5 billion, so the \$35 billion cap was set roughly 1.7 to 1.9 times as high as the Federal Reserve's own contemporaneous projection of achievable runoff, the multiple depending on the same disclosed intra-2022 allocation choice as the anticipated share (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}). Nothing in my estimates identifies the welfare or market effects of such designs; active sales in particular would realize the mark-to-market losses that passive runoff defers. I record the option to mark where the design space lies, not to recommend a point in it.

\subsection{The Danish Market-Value-Repurchase Alternative}\label{sec:abm-danish}

A third alternative acts on the payoff rule itself rather than on the mortgage contract's portability or the redemption cap. Sections~\ref{sec:abm-results} and~\ref{sec:abm-burnout} show that no synthetic-population household-choice specification tested here explains the majority of the trapped liquidity---a finding Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} confines to the synthetic population, its recalibrated real-covariate variant having crossed the pre-committed threshold past which the paradigm reading cannot be cleanly maintained---so the question is how much of that trap is a consequence of the par-payoff rule itself, rather than of household behavior under any institutional regime. The Danish market-value repurchase system, which severs the link between market rates and payoff cost, gives me a direct way to ask that question quantitatively. The operative specification calibrates each framework's Danish response against \citet{berger2026}'s separately estimated moving and refinance-in-place channels and transplants them onto the U.S.\ loan population so that only the payoff rule changes; it yields the paper's finding on this question, an institutional gap of $+\$61.2$ billion (Table~\ref{tab:danish}). The rest of this subsection develops that specification and its sensitivity.\footnote{\label{fn:manifest}CPR means below are aggregates over the 42 active QT months (June 2022--November 2025), and the institutional wedge is the mean over those months of the cohort-weighted Danish-minus-U.S.\ simulated CPR difference. This operative specification supersedes an initial mechanism-substitution that had implied a much larger gap; that superseded figure, and the frozen run manifests underlying every ABM headline, are catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}.}

The recalibration rests on two behaviorally distinct channels that \citet{berger2026} (my source for the Danish institutional mechanism itself) estimate separately, and that a single extrapolated mechanism would conflate. The first is a moving channel: does removing the par-payoff penalty change how often households relocate? The second is a refinance-in-place channel: does the option to buy back debt at a market discount induce discount-buying behavior independent of moving? Danish administrative data show the two channels behave very differently under a U.S. tax transplant. The moving channel is well-identified and close to flat in the coupon gap: Danish households move at an unconditional rate of 3.2\% per year regardless of how far their coupon sits below market \citep[\S4.6, structural parameters in tab.~3]{berger2026}, versus a U.S. moving-hazard slope of 0.57--1.20 percentage points per year per 100 bp of coupon gap \citep{fonseca2024}. The refinance-in-place channel, by contrast, is substantially a Danish tax artifact: discount buybacks are attractive there because mortgage interest is deductible at a flat 33\% rate and realized capital gains on the buyback are tax-exempt. Under U.S. tax treatment (a 22\% mortgage-interest deduction and a 15\% capital-gains tax on the discount, per Berger et al.'s own counterfactual), this channel loses its shield, and their general-equilibrium counterfactual \citep[\S4.9.1]{berger2026} finds it shifts the equilibrium mortgage rate by only about one basis point, economically negligible.

Recalibrating both frameworks against these two estimated channels, rather than against a single extrapolated mechanism, first requires a choice: what to do with the moving channel's \emph{level}. Berger et al.'s identified fact about the moving margin is one of slope: Danish moving is flat in the coupon gap under market-value payoff; the 3.2\% per year is Denmark's descriptive unconditional moving rate, not an estimate of how often U.S. households would move under a Danish payoff rule. Importing that level onto the U.S. loan population (the \emph{Danish-level} anchor) therefore bundles two changes, the payoff rule and Danish baseline mobility itself, and produces Danish legs whose mean CPR (3.39--3.40\%, Table~\ref{tab:danish}) sits \emph{below} the 4\% involuntary-turnover floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side (Sections~\ref{sec:method-frictions} and~\ref{sec:pathb}). Death, divorce, and forced relocation do not become rarer because the payoff rule changed, so a rule-only counterfactual on the same U.S. households cannot prepay below that floor. The construction that isolates the rule is the \emph{U.S.-intercept} anchor: keep the identified flatness, but anchor the level at the production U.S. hazard evaluated at zero rate gap, with the involuntary floor, seasoning baseline, burnout, and covariate terms all retained and only the rate-gap suppression switched off, plus the same U.S.-transplant refinance-in-place channel. I adopt the U.S.-intercept anchor as the production reading (it is the only one consistent with the floor's stated semantics) and retain the Danish-level anchor as a labeled bracketing case in which U.S. households are assumed to adopt Danish baseline mobility along with the Danish rule. Holding the floor fixed while importing only the Danish moving slope treats involuntary turnover and rate-elastic moving as separable channels; that separability is motivated by the floor's involuntary semantics rather than identified in the data available here, which is why the Danish-level anchor, importing the two jointly, is retained as the bracketing case.

The anchor choice determines the sign of the institutional gap. Under the shared-accounting hybrid pipeline (specified in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}: the ABM's macro-accounting layer with Path B's microsimulated CPR as the micro-foundation), the U.S.-intercept anchor yields a Danish mean CPR of 5.61\% against the locked-in U.S. leg's 4.76\%, and an institutional gap of $+\$61.2$ billion (8.0\% of the benchmark) at Berger et al.'s general-equilibrium best estimate of a negligible refinance-in-place contribution (the U.S. leg reproduces the committed production artifacts exactly). This is a return to a construction the pipeline already contained: the U.S.-intercept hybrid coincides numerically, leg for leg, with the NPV-identity hybrid the replication package's technical documentation records from before the Berger recalibration, because the buyback-at-market-value identity zeroes the effective rate gap, which is computationally the same object as the zero-gap U.S. hazard; what that earlier construction lacked, and Berger et al.'s moving-flatness estimate now supplies, is empirical evidence that Danish-rule moving is flat in the gap. The sign is invariant under this anchor, and it could not have come out otherwise. The specification defines the Danish moving hazard as the production U.S. hazard evaluated at a zero rate gap; the U.S. book carries a negative gap in essentially every cell over essentially the whole window (99.53\% of exposure below the window-minimum market rate, Section~\ref{sec:identification}); and the hazard is monotone in the gap. The zero-gap leg therefore must prepay faster, the Danish trapped balance must come in lower (\$687.8 billion against \$749.0 billion), and the gap must be positive. Sweeping the refinance-in-place input from 0\% to the rejected partial-equilibrium ceiling ($\approx$18\% CPR) only adds Danish prepayment on top of that, so the sweep's sign-robustness---positive at every point, rising to $+\$1{,}038.9$ billion at the ceiling (Figure~\ref{fig:gapsweep})---is forced by construction as well. I retain both as wiring checks and read neither as evidence. What is not forced is the magnitude: \$61.2 billion, 8.0\% of the benchmark, is the quantity the recalibration actually delivers, and the Danish-level anchor below shows that a different and defensible transplant choice reverses the sign outright. This positive gap is the lock-in marginal of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} seen from the counterfactual side: switching off the rate-gap suppression is what the $\beta_1 = 0$ null does, so the rule-only Danish counterfactual and the paper's lock-in contribution at the in-sample calibration point are one object measured on two accounting legs ($+\$61.2$ billion here, where the Danish leg compounds its own counterfactual balances; $+\$70.3$ billion as the U.S.-leg marginal). That near-equality is a statement about the demoted in-sample point and not about the paper's headline: against the $+\$42.6$ billion off-window marginal the Danish rule-only gap is about 44\% larger, not equal, and both legs were run at the production floor; re-run at the off-window floor itself (run \texttt{danish\_offwindow\_floor}; the U.S. leg reproduces the committed off-window central exactly), the rule-only gap is $+\$28.2$ billion, 3.7\% of the benchmark, 34\% below the off-window marginal, so the near-equality is specific to the in-sample calibration and no equality survives at matched floors. Under the Danish-level anchor, by contrast, the gap is $-\$99.9$ billion (U.S. 4.76\%, Danish 3.39\% mean CPR) and crosses zero at a refinance-in-place contribution of 1.4\% CPR; the fragility reflects the bundled mobility-culture change. The ABM's own recalibrated run under the Danish-level anchor shows $-\$728.4$ billion (crossing at 12.6\%), but its U.S.-side leg is over-predicted relative to the empirical benchmark (11.76\% simulated mean CPR under the Berger run's own calibration-stage freeze, versus 5.14\% empirical; cf.\ the production tag's 11.68\%, Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}), and under the U.S.-intercept anchor the ABM's direction follows a fortiori without a run: the ABM's lock-in penalty only ever suppresses moving, so its zero-gap intercept exceeds its locked-in U.S. leg month by month and its rule-only gap is positive by construction. Table~\ref{tab:danish} collects all four specifications.

That ceiling is rejected on partial-equilibrium grounds and is not reached by any calibration this paper adopts. The upper half of the sweep should not be read as a quantitative statement at all: at refinance contributions of 12\% and above the Danish leg's trapped balance turns negative ($-\$14.4$, $-\$158.0$ and $-\$290.0$ billion at 12, 15 and 18\%), which is not an economically meaningful quantity.
