The initial mechanism-substitution that this specification corrects had implied a far larger gap, \$925.5 billion, by feeding a U.S.-calibrated mobility function a Danish-style rate gap it was never estimated to describe; it is an extrapolation rather than a measurement, catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}. The case for the Danish alternative is modest and anchor-signed: roughly $+\$61$ billion, 8\% of the benchmark, at the in-sample calibration point ($+\$28$ billion, 3.7\%, at the off-window headline floor), the counterfactual image of the lock-in marginal. Its positivity throughout the refinance-in-place sweep is a property of the zero-gap anchor rather than a finding, so the magnitude is the informative part. The earlier ``sign we cannot identify'' reading survives only as a statement about the bracketing Danish-level anchor, which asks a different question (what if U.S. households also moved like Danes) and answers it with a Danish leg that violates the floor both frameworks impose on the U.S. side. This does not alter Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s central finding (that loan-level and institutional mechanics, not household choice, explain the U.S. empirical shortfall) since that comparison never depended on the Danish counterfactual. What it does revise is the payoff-rule question: the par-payoff rule's institutional cash-flow cost is real but an order of magnitude smaller than the original extrapolation implied, and of the same origin as the lock-in marginal the paper identifies directly---and of the same size as that marginal at the in-sample calibration point where both legs were run, though about 44\% larger than the off-window marginal the paper headlines.

\section{Robustness Checks}\label{sec:robustness}

This section collects the methodological checks referenced throughout the paper. Two of them changed reported results rather than confirming them. The recalibrated cross-design variant of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} exceeds my ex-ante threshold and qualifies the household-choice reading at the aggregate level; and the off-window floor re-measurement of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} is where the paper's headline marginal and level are actually derived ($+9.2 \to +5.6$ points; 97.9\% $\to$ 91.3\% shared), demoting the in-window calibration to an in-sample point. On the shared basis the paper quotes throughout, the hazard framework undershoots the benchmark at the headline calibration (91.3\%); it overshoots only on the standalone basis (107.0--121.5\%). The remaining checks confirm rather than change the central decomposition.

\subsection{Scale Sensitivity}\label{sec:robustness-scale}

The ABM's 10,000-household population and the hazard framework's 75,000-loan sample differ in both scale and data source. To test whether population size alone drives the gap between the two frameworks, I re-ran the ABM's core household mechanic (exponential mobility-desire heterogeneity---the distribution the production code draws, on a dollar scale---mapped through a rate-gap-driven CPR function) at N = 10,000 and N = 75,000 across 50 seeds each. The mean simulated CPR is 15.616\% at both population sizes (difference 0.0003 percentage points), while the 95\% confidence interval narrows by a factor of 2.4$\times$ at the larger $N$, of the same order as, though about 12\% short of, the 2.74$\times$ an idealized $\sqrt{N}$ law would predict ($\sqrt{7.5} \approx 2.74$), consistent with a non-i.i.d.\ component of seed noise; I do not lean on the exact ratio. Population size changes the precision of that mechanic's estimate, not its central tendency. Two limits on what the check shows. Both are large, so I state the conclusion conditionally. First, it exercised the household cost-benefit mechanic \emph{in isolation}: the production specification adds a 43\% front-end DTI wall, a discrete twenty-percent wait-and-see freeze trait, income-scaled curtailment, eleven-cohort multi-vintage weighting with a fifteen- and thirty-year term split, and a settlement-lag kernel, none of which are present here. The isolated mechanic's 15.616\% mean CPR accordingly runs 3.9 points---about a third---above the production ABM's 11.761\%, so the object whose scale-invariance is demonstrated is not the estimator whose recovery is at issue. Second, that check had no committed script, artifact or gate behind it; the figure survived from an earlier draft. Both limits are now addressed by a pre-committed rerun on the full production specification (run \texttt{production\_scale\_test}), fifty seeds at each population size, carrying the DTI wall, the freeze trait, curtailment, the eleven-cohort weighting with its term split and the settlement-lag kernel. It recovers 12.66\% of the benchmark at $N = 10{,}000$ against 12.18\% at $N = 75{,}000$---a difference of $0.48$ points against a fifty-seed standard deviation of $3.25$ points, so well inside one standard deviation---at mean simulated CPRs of 11.60\% and 11.64\%, close to the production run's 11.76\% rather than to the isolated mechanic's 15.6\%. Seed precision improves at the larger population, the standard deviation falling from $3.25$ to $1.05$ points (a ratio of 3.1 against the $\sqrt{7.5} \approx 2.74$ an idealized law would give), and again I do not lean on the exact ratio. Population size therefore does not drive the gap between this estimator and the hazard framework, and that statement now rests on the production specification rather than on a fragment of it. It does not follow, however, that the gap is attributable to modeling paradigm alone: that distinction cannot be closed by resampling, and Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} takes it up directly with the completed cross-design pair.

\subsection{Benchmark Sensitivity to Data Source and Cap-Schedule Assumptions}\label{sec:robustness-benchmark}

The \$764.7 billion empirical benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) is constructed from the New York Fed's SOMA current-face-value series under the actual phased redemption cap schedule, netting realized roll-off against the cap within the active QT window. I treat net accumulation, rather than one-sided accumulation of shortfall months only, as the correct construction because it is the only version comparable to the Danish counterfactual of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, where simulated roll-off routinely exceeds the cap; a one-sided construction would overstate the empirical benchmark relative to that counterfactual and bias the recovered percentages reported for both estimators. Researchers extending this work with alternative SOMA vintages or a different cap-schedule assumption should expect the benchmark level, and therefore the recovered percentages in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}, to shift; the qualitative ranking of the three estimators reported in Table~\ref{tab:estimators} is not sensitive to this choice, since the ABM's shortfall against any plausible benchmark construction remains an order of magnitude smaller than either hazard path's. Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} separately addresses the conceptual status of the cap-relative construction itself. One such alternative is now measured rather than anticipated. The benchmark nets cash arriving in month $t$ against the cap in force in month $t$, which is what the cap constrains: the Committee set a ceiling on principal not reinvested in a given month, and that is a statement about cash. Agency remittance, however, means the cash arriving in month $t$ was generated by prepayment decisions in months $t$, $t-1$ and $t-2$, on the settlement kernel this paper already applies to simulated roll-off (0.10, 0.60, 0.30 at lags zero through two, from standard 55-day agency remittance cycles). Convolving the cap schedule forward with that same kernel and re-netting the unchanged realized series gives a settlement-aligned benchmark of \$722.7 billion against the calendar \$764.7 billion, a shift of $-5.5$\% (run \texttt{settlement\_months\_benchmark}). The shift is mechanical, and its size is exactly accounted for: the QT window is half-open, so cap convolved past its final month is dropped---October 2025 loses its two-month tap and November 2025 its one- and two-month taps, \$42.0 billion of the \$1{,}417.5 billion schedule---and because the realized series is untouched and both legs sum over the same months, the benchmark moves by exactly that \$42.0 billion. Two alternate kernels bracketing the remittance cycle put the shift at $-\$28.0$ and $-\$52.5$ billion. I retain the calendar convention, for the reason Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark} gives for retaining the cap at all: it is the only policy-committed quantity available, and it is committed in cash-arrival months. What the variant establishes is that re-basing moves no dollar quantity in this paper---the identified marginal is \$42.6 billion on either denominator---and moves the percentages only by the common factor $1.058$: the central Path~B level reads 96.6\% rather than 91.3\%, the $\beta_1 = 0$ null 90.7\% rather than 85.7\%, and the marginal $+5.9$ rather than $+5.6$ points. The decomposition is unchanged in substance, and the null still recovers the large majority of the benchmark under either convention. A related window-construction pitfall merits a reproducibility note: accumulating from the QT start date without an upper bound admits eight post-QT months (December 2025 onward). Where the dollar aggregation already applies the bounded active-QT mask, as it does throughout this paper, only the path statistics drift---the headline CPR means by roughly 0.3--0.5 percentage points; where it does not, the benchmark level itself moves. That asymmetry (path statistics silently sensitive to a window filter the masked dollar aggregates are robust to) is why every headline figure in this paper cites a single tagged run manifest (footnote~\ref{fn:manifest}).

\subsection{Behavioral Extension Mechanics}\label{sec:robustness-extensions}

This subsection reports the mechanism behind each of the three behavioral extensions summarized in Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}. As Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions} describes, loss aversion steepens the penalty every household faces, which on its own would suppress simulated mobility below the historically observed 4--5\% involuntary-turnover floor; to preserve that floor, I re-calibrate the mobility-desire parameter upward, nearly tripling it. That recalibration mechanically inflates the model's overall responsiveness to rate gaps, so the jump to 54.9\% is an artifact of holding the turnover floor fixed, not a validation of loss aversion as a mechanism. Because that floor is anchored at maximum lock-in, the sign of the recalibration's effect on rate-sensitivity is not fixed: a specification change that eases turnover at the anchor lowers the recalibrated mobility scale (in the real-covariate cross-design of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} it falls to 36,086 from the synthetic 43,883) and deflates rather than inflates rate-sensitivity, so the headline recovery, itself resting on a floor-anchored calibration, inherits that indeterminacy, which is why the falsification is read only alongside the cross-design hedge and never on its own. The second extension, income-scaled curtailment, pulls the estimate down to 45.1\%; because curtailment is a strictly additive drag on prepayment, this decline is what I predicted ex ante from a diagnostic correlation between the ABM's over-prediction and disposable-income growth (Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). The third extension, multi-vintage coupon composition, pulls the estimate down further, to 33.7\%. A lower weighted-average coupon widens the gap to prevailing market rates, and my hypothesis was that this should increase predicted trapped liquidity, not reduce it. The inversion suggests the ABM's CPR mechanism is not translating a wider coupon gap into slower prepayment the way the household-choice framework predicts it should. I have not diagnosed this inversion, and its implication cuts in both directions: an aggregate response to the rate gap running backward is equally consistent with ``household choice explains little'' and with ``this specific ABM's gap response is mis-specified,'' which is why the ABM's recovery share is never quoted in this paper without the cross-design qualifier of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}, why the paradigm attribution inherits that section's hedge, and why the two-estimator contrast, not the ABM's point estimate, is what the paper asks the reader to carry. I cannot rule out that a differently specified household model would perform better, but that possibility does not bear on the results reported in Section~\ref{sec:abm}.

The wait-and-see freeze now carries a direct sensitivity run, scored on the frozen Danish-anchor production accounting (trapped \$84.5 billion at the production 150 bp trigger and 20\% share; parity gate $+\$0.02$ billion). Removing the freeze entirely drops trapped liquidity to \$44.6 billion (an isolated contribution of $+\$39.9$ billion, $+5.2$ points of benchmark), and moving the six-month trigger to 100 or 200 bp spans \$105.9 to \$70.5 billion, so the trigger's placement matters at the few-points scale and its author-calibrated status remains a disclosed limitation. Sweeping the freeze share with the floor anchor retained gives \$65.1 billion at a 10\% share, \$81.5 billion at the externally implied 18.3\%, and \$103.3 billion at 30\%: re-anchoring the share to the Liebersohn--Rothstein-implied value moves recovery by only $-0.4$ points of benchmark, and the calibrated mobility scale and floor CPR are bit-identical at every share, confirming that the freeze cannot compromise the involuntary-turnover floor. The front-end DTI gate receives the parallel treatment: moving the 43\% wall to 36\% or 50\% on the same frozen surface moves ABM trapped liquidity to \$153.3 and \$119.7 billion (from \$84.5 billion at the production 43\%), with the involuntary floor re-anchored into the empirical 4--5\% band at every threshold, so the gate is material to the ABM's own estimate while never breaching the floor. That sweep is non-monotone in the wall, and the production 43\% sits at its minimum, which invites the reading that the paper's own parameter choice is the one minimizing its own estimate. It does not survive decomposition (run \texttt{dti\_nonmonotonicity\_decomposition}). The floor-retention rule re-calibrates the mobility-desire scale only where the fixed-scale floor would leave the 4--5\% band, and across these three thresholds that happens exactly once: at 50\%, where loosening the wall lifts the fixed-scale floor to 5.09\% and the search cuts the scale from 43{,}883 to 41{,}934. Holding the scale at its production value instead, the sweep is monotone---\$153.4, \$84.6 and \$46.4 billion at 36, 43 and 50\%---and the 50\% leg's entire rebound is the re-calibration, worth $-\$73.4$ billion. The frozen and re-calibrated legs are bit-identical at 36 and 43\%, where the rule is a no-op, which is what identifies the channel. The wall is therefore monotone in the direction the mechanism implies, and the published non-monotonicity is the free parameter absorbing the floor constraint rather than a property of the DTI gate. Nothing here moves a headline: the ABM is a falsification device and the hazard marginal carries no DTI gate.

\subsection{Cross-Design Test: Real Covariates in the ABM}\label{sec:robustness-crossdesign}

Section~\ref{sec:method-estimators} flags an unresolved confound between this paper's two estimators: they differ in modeling paradigm (household choice versus loan-level survival) and in data source (synthetic population versus real Freddie Mac loan records) simultaneously, so a low ABM share and a high hazard-framework share are equally consistent with either explanation. To make partial progress on separating them, I re-ran the ABM's decision rule with structural covariates (coupon, loan age, and original LTV) drawn directly from the same 75,000-loan Freddie Mac sample used throughout Section~\ref{sec:hazard}, in place of the log-normal, FRED-median-calibrated draws used elsewhere in this paper. Behavioral draws (mobility desire, loss aversion, patience, transaction cost) remain synthetic throughout, since the Freddie Mac data contains no analogue for these constructs; this test therefore varies real structural heterogeneity---and, until the post-stratification below, book composition jointly---not a fully real population.

Because the ABM's mobility-scale parameter is calibrated to hold a real-world involuntary-turnover floor fixed (Section~\ref{sec:method-abm}), swapping in a population with different LTV and coupon moments raises a calibration question that a naive covariate swap does not resolve on its own: should the turnover-floor anchor be re-derived against the new population's moments, or held fixed at its original value? I report both variants. The recalibrated variant applies the recalibration rule of Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}, re-anchoring the mobility-scale parameter to reproduce the same 4--5\% turnover floor under the real population's moments; the frozen variant suspends that rule and holds the original synthetic-population calibration unchanged.

Before running either variant, I specified ex ante the following interpretive thresholds: a recovery above 50\% of the empirical benchmark would undercut the paradigm interpretation advanced in Sections~\ref{sec:abm}--\ref{sec:conclusion}; a recovery below 35\% would corroborate it, consistent with the synthetic-population ABM's own specifications; and a recovery between 35\% and 50\% would be ambiguous.

The recalibrated variant recovers \$453.5 billion, or 59.3\%, of the benchmark, above my ex-ante 50\% threshold. The frozen variant recovers \$159.5 billion, or 20.9\%, within the corroborating band. Both are single draws, so a pre-committed fifty-seed sweep varying both randomization layers (the behavioral draws and the covariate subsample; run \texttt{cross\_design\_seeds}) tests whether the threshold crossing is a property of the estimator or of the seed. It is the estimator: the recalibrated variant averages 60.2\% with a standard deviation of 3.3 points and a 95\% band of 55.1--66.0\%, the committed draw sits at the 48th percentile of its own distribution, and all fifty seeds classify as undercutting. The frozen variant averages 22.0\%. The band is wide enough to report---10.9 points, against the 7.7-point disclosure threshold fixed before the run---but not wide enough to reach the calibration swing, so the operative uncertainty here remains the calibration choice rather than seed noise, now measured rather than assumed. The same sweep records the deep-gap floor each variant implies, and the two variants separate cleanly on it. The recalibrated variant's CPR at an 8\% market rate averages 4.59\% and stays inside the observed 4--5\% involuntary band on all fifty seeds. The frozen variant averages 7.05\% and lies outside that band on all fifty (range 6.31--7.63\%). This is the same admissibility test I apply to the externally anchored variant earlier in this subsection, and applied consistently it counts against the frozen leg, not the recalibrated one: the leg the paper reports as falling within the corroborating band is the leg that misses the turnover moment. I therefore read the 20.9\% as the less admissible of the two calibrations rather than as corroboration, which strengthens the undercutting reading rather than qualifying it. One further caveat on the word ``frozen'': re-deriving that variant's mobility-scale anchor per seed gives a mean of 43{,}493 with a standard deviation of 1{,}181 and a minimum of 39{,}984, against the committed 43{,}883, which sits at the very top of its own range. The anchor is frozen by convention, not because the data pin it there. Table~\ref{tab:crossdesign} summarizes both against the synthetic-population control, and Figure~\ref{fig:crossdesign} plots the paired comparison.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig5_cross_design}
\caption{The cross-design test as paired panels. Panel (a): dollar recovery for the two real-covariate variants against the pre-committed thresholds (above 50\% undercuts the paradigm interpretation; below 35\% corroborates it) and the synthetic-population control band; the recalibrated variant's 59.3\% crosses the undercutting threshold while the frozen variant's 20.9\% stays within the corroborating band. Panel (b): lag-0 path correlations, all negative, so neither variant reproduces the empirical path's timing regardless of calibration choice, and after Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s detrending result no estimator in this paper does.}
\label{fig:crossdesign}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small

\label{tab:crossdesign}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.0cm}lll@{}}
\toprule

Trapped liquidity & \$453.5B (59.3\%; fifty-seed mean 60.2\%) & \$159.5B (20.9\%; 22.0\%) & \$91.0B (11.9\%) \\
Mean CPR & 8.14\% & 11.63\% & 11.68\% \\

Under the alternative most-positive-$r$ convention, the synthetic control's best alignment is $+0.195$ at lag $-3$, the ``$+0.19$ at the three-month lead'' of Section~\ref{sec:abm-results}; the two conventions summarize the same cross-correlation profile, and the substantive conclusion (no positive contemporaneous alignment, which, after Section~\ref{sec:pathb}'s detrending result, no estimator including Path B shows) holds under either.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Two results stand out. First, by my own ex-ante criterion, the primary recalibrated variant's 59.3\% recovery, 60.2\% averaged over fifty seeds, moves against the paradigm interpretation: with real structural covariates and a like-for-like turnover anchor, the household-choice ABM explains far more of the benchmark than the synthetic-population specifications throughout Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} suggested. Second, the \$294 billion gap between the recalibrated and frozen variants (produced solely by how the turnover-floor anchor is set, with the underlying covariates held fixed) indicates that the ABM's aggregate share is highly sensitive to a calibration choice that is orthogonal to data source. The present test cannot, on its own, cleanly attribute Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s contrast to modeling paradigm rather than to data source: recalibration choice is doing comparable work to whichever of paradigm or data source the test was designed to isolate.

A pre-committed post-stratification separates composition from heterogeneity (run \texttt{cross\_design\_reweight}). Reweighting the real-covariate population to the SOMA coupon-by-vintage-group composition---the book the benchmark is measured on---with the mobility scale re-derived on the reweighted population under the same floor rule moves the recalibrated variant from 59.3\% to 76.3\% (importance weights; effective sample size 1{,}332 of 10{,}000, disclosed; a seed-42 re-draw within SOMA-weighted cells gives 70.6\%) and the frozen variant from 20.9\% to 12.6\% (re-draw 36.0\%). The composition mismatch therefore understated rather than manufactured the recalibrated recovery: at book composition, real-covariate household choice with a like-for-like floor anchor explains roughly seventy to seventy-six percent of the benchmark, the ex-ante undercutting verdict survives composition adjustment, and the calibration-choice spread this subsection flags widens to roughly \$488 billion. The reweight is a level exercise; the path-level failure of Table~\ref{tab:crossdesign} is not revisited by it.

A third variant constrains the calibration question from the other side: it drops the turnover-floor anchor entirely and parameterizes the two author-calibrated behavioral elements from the same external source Path B's elasticity imports, pinning the mobility-desire scale to \citepos{liebersohn2024} zero-gap moving level (a 1.5\% quarterly ZIP-code moving hazard when the average rate gap was essentially zero, 5.9\% annualized) and the wait-and-see freeze share to the magnitude their 6.5\%-per-quarter-per-100 bp mobility decline implies at the production 150 bp trigger (18.3\% against the author-calibrated 20\%; the DTI wall and the loss-aversion coefficient are already externally sourced). Recovery does rise without the floor recalibration (to 150.6\% of benchmark, overshooting it by half), but it does so by predicting essentially zero prepayment at QT-window rate gaps: the variant's CPR at an 8\% market rate is 0.0\%, against the observed 4--5\% discount-cohort turnover and against the roughly 1.44--1.45\% quarterly moving hazard \citet{liebersohn2024} themselves observe at 2022--2023 gaps. The externally anchored gate structure can match the zero-gap moving level or the observed deep-gap turnover, but not both: the exponential mobility-desire distribution that fits the former (scale 4{,}472 against the recalibrated 36{,}086) shuts moving down completely once the par-payoff penalty binds. I read this as clarifying the floor anchor's role: it is what keeps the ABM on the observed turnover data, not an artifact that suppresses the model's rate response. The anchored dimension itself is never claimed as fit: matching the floor is tautological by construction (Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}), and what this variant measures is what that tautology purchases---empirical admissibility at deep gaps---one more instance of the deflate-as-well-as-inflate indeterminacy this subsection already carries (same harness as the two variants above). Scored against the pre-committed bands, as this variant's own specification required, its 150.6\% classifies as undercutting the paradigm reading, and the frozen artifact records that classification. I do not read it as informative about the paradigm question, and I label that reading post hoc rather than pre-committed, because this is the kind of distinction the paper's pre-commitment design is meant to surface. The pre-run specification committed only to reporting the deep-gap floor diagnostic ``alongside whatever the recovery number is, whichever direction it moves''; it did not commit to treating a failed diagnostic as grounds for removing the recovery from the band. The admissibility precondition---that a variant must reproduce an empirically admissible deep-gap turnover level before its recovery is interpretable---was formulated after the result was seen, and it is reported here as a labelled post-run qualification, not as a pre-registered exclusion. A reader who declines the qualification should score the variant at 150.6\% against the 50\% band. That strengthens the undercutting reading this subsection already concedes rather than weakening it, and no headline quantity turns on which way it is read.

To test whether a minimal simulated-minimum-distance calibration could reconcile the two external moments, I ran one: a joint two-parameter fit, the mobility-desire scale together with a forced-move point mass added to the structural family, with the moment set, admissibility bands, distance, and verdict vocabulary all fixed before execution and a parity gate replaying the committed 59.303\% first. The verdict is joint infeasibility, now with two degrees of freedom rather than one: at the distance-minimizing point (scale 1{,}000, forced-move mass 3.9\%) the zero-gap moving level overshoots its band just as the deep-gap turnover reaches its own (0.0644 against $[0.055, 0.063]$; 0.0394 against $[0.040, 0.050]$), because the forced mass lifts both moments together once the scale is at its floor. The near-fit, scored through the same harness, recovers 107.1\% of benchmark, far above the pre-committed cross-design bands, so external anchoring pushes this structural family past the benchmark, not toward it. I deliberately do not fit QT-window CPR moments: the recovery statistic is a functional of that path, and matching it would convert the test into a fitted quantity, the overfitting the pre-commitment architecture exists to prevent. The hazard-side headline quantities are not functions of ABM calibration and cannot move under any outcome of this run.

Both variants retain the ABM's central path-level failure: the monthly CPR path correlation stays negative at lag 0 ($-0.336$ and $-0.311$), as all estimators' are once the window's shared trend is removed (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); Path B's distinction is level accuracy, not timing. The timing critique of Section~\ref{sec:abm} therefore survives regardless of which calibration variant is preferred, even as the dollar-recovery critique does not straightforwardly survive under the recalibrated variant.

\subsection{Cross-Design Synthesis: The Symmetric Companion Test}\label{sec:robustness-synthesis}

The symmetric companion test (estimating the hazard framework's survival structure on a synthetic population, to check whether that structure alone recovers the benchmark independently of real data) materially updates the cross-design picture: Path B recovers 106.0\% of the benchmark on a fully synthetic population with no real Freddie Mac data at all (96.9\% on the shared accounting basis, whose common netting applies equally here), close to its 107.0\% real-data recovery at the same in-sample calibration point, though the raw levels correlation drops from $+0.190$ to $-0.06$ at lag 0 (a levels comparison; neither value survives detrending, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). That 106.0\% is a recovery level, however, and this paper demotes recovery levels everywhere else for a reason. Running the mechanism-removed leg on the same synthetic population --- $\beta_1 = 0$, nothing else changed --- recovers 95.6\%, so what the companion test identifies is a differential of 10.4 points, or \$79.3 billion, and not the 106.0\% level (\texttt{synthetic\_companion\_null}). That differential is of the same order as the 9.2-point real-book gap between the central leg's 107.0\% and the $\beta_1 = 0$ null's 97.8\%, which Section~\ref{sec:identification} already treats as too small to license a level-based reading; a superseded cross-basis figure put that gap at 18.3 points, which was an error of basis rather than of arithmetic --- it differenced a standalone-scorer central leg against a shared-basis null --- and on any single basis the real-book gap is 9.2 points at the in-sample calibration point and 5.6 points at the off-window floor, so the synthetic differential slightly exceeds its real-book counterpart rather than falling short of it; the synthetic recovery is floor-dominated in the same way the real one is. This decomposes the tension rather than resolving it uniformly: at the aggregate level, the household-choice paradigm required real covariates to reach 59.3\%, while the loan-level paradigm reaches a comparable aggregate recovery with none. At the path level, both paradigms still depend on real structure for correct timing. I treat the aggregate-level paradigm claim of Sections~\ref{sec:hazard} and~\ref{sec:conclusion} as corroborated by this result on its differential rather than substantially strengthened by its level, and the path-level claim as still open.

\subsection{Cross-Foundation Checks: Hybrid Pipeline and Full-Book Weighting}\label{sec:robustness-hybrid}

The hybrid pipeline referenced in Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} holds the paper's accounting fixed while swapping the micro-foundation for loan behavior. Concretely, it runs the ABM's macro-accounting layer unchanged (SOMA balance tracking, phased-cap netting, income-scaled curtailment, term-aware scheduled amortization, and the Danish dynamic-balance loop), but replaces the ABM's CPR surface with Path B's microsimulated CPR path; no settlement kernel applies, since Path B settles prepayments in the month they occur. Holding the accounting fixed, the U.S.-side recovery is 97.9\% (the shared layer adds the income-scaled curtailment flow that Path B's standalone scorer omits: \$69.56 billion over the window), and re-running the Danish leg under the Berger-recalibrated regime yields, under the production U.S.-intercept anchor, the U.S. 4.76\% / Danish 5.61\% mean-CPR pair and the $+\$61.2$ billion institutional gap that Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish} adopts as its production estimate (the Danish-level bracketing anchor gives 3.39\% and $-\$99.9$ billion). The construction removes accounting differences between frameworks as a confound of the institutional comparison: both regimes flow through one accounting layer with one empirically grounded micro-foundation.

The same layer now scores every estimator, which is what makes the recovery figures commensurable with the benchmark: the actual SOMA roll-off underlying the empirical shortfall contains borrowers' curtailment dollars, a flow no hazard simulation models, so the shared layer adds it to each simulated roll-off. The netting is identical across estimators (\$69.56 billion for every run), and identical \emph{by construction}, not by coincidence, with the conversion architecture stated per leg: each estimator's hazard dynamics evolve on its own internal balances (Path A drains cohort balances, Path B drains loan balances, and the Danish legs deliberately compound a dynamic counterfactual balance, Section~\ref{sec:method-abm}), but every \emph{U.S.-leg} dollar conversion is common (simulated roll-off is rescaled month-by-month to the actual holdings path (WSHOMCB) by the book-to-sample ratio), so the curtailment flow, computed on those same actual holdings, is a common flow rather than a per-run quantity. It is a flat 9.1-percentage-point wedge between the two bases; the invariance argument covers U.S. legs only. Danish legs compute curtailment on their own counterfactual balances inside the Danish loop; the slower Danish drain leaves higher balances and hence more curtailment dollars, a differential I quantify and print: Danish-leg curtailment is \$70.33 billion against the U.S. legs' common \$69.56 billion for the central hybrid (+\$0.77 billion), already embedded in every printed gap and immaterial to its sign or size.

Table~\ref{tab:bases} collects every hazard leg's recovery on all four accounting bases (the lock-in margin is basis-invariant at either floor because the netting is bit-identical across legs and cancels exactly, an invariance that holds at every cell of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}'s calibration box). The fully corrected figure comes from a joint run (the book-coupon-reweighted simulation scored through the shared layer in one pass), which recovers \$765.1 billion (unrounded: \$765.077 billion): 100.0\% of the \$764.7 billion benchmark at one decimal, and exactly 100.04\% as the ratio of the two unrounded figures, $765.077/764.748$, with Path A's joint run at \$905.1 billion, 118.4\%. The composed figure is conditional on the ex-ante covariate priors (the covariate band spans 92.2\%--99.2\% on the shared basis, approximately 94.3\%--101.3\% composed (Section~\ref{sec:pathb})), so its precision is a property of that calibration, not a tuned result. Because the netting is common, the joint results equal the one-at-a-time deltas composed ($107.0 + 2.1 - 9.1$) exactly; I report the identity as a cross-check on the joint run, not as its source. The abstract and Sections~\ref{sec:hazard-interp} and~\ref{sec:conclusion} state recoveries on this basis.

The curtailment layer invites one further stress, since its assumed rate is an external input rather than a fitted quantity. The monthly curtailment flow is the product of an income-driven fraction, which is independent of the assumed curtailment rate, and that rate, applied to the actual holdings path (WSHOMCB), so the netted total is exactly proportional to the assumed rate: it does not interact with any estimator's dynamics. A $\pm 25\%$ stress on the assumed rate (whose realized window average is approximately 0.84\% annualized, Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark}) therefore scales the \$69.56 billion netting to \$52.17 and \$86.95 billion and the 9.1-point wedge to 6.8 and 11.4 points, a uniform shift in every U.S. leg's shared-basis recovery that leaves the lock-in marginal and every cross-estimator gap unchanged to the decimal, because both legs of each comparison net the identical flow. The same argument holds month by month for any time-varying curtailment process: the netting is the month-by-month product of a shared curtailment rate and the shared actual-holdings path, and the trapped-liquidity metric is a linear signed sum against a fixed cap-target path, so a seasonal profile, or an income elasticity that differs across 2022--23 and 2024--25, subtracts out of the marginal term by term whatever its shape; only the common level wedge and the Danish leg's sub-\$1 billion differential can move. A demonstration run makes the identity concrete: a $\pm 25\%$-amplitude seasonal profile and a 2022--23 versus 2024--25 regime split move the netted total to \$70.06 and \$68.09 billion and the wedge to 9.16 and 8.90 points, while the lock-in marginal is unchanged to below $10^{-13}$ points on both legs and the simulated CPR paths are bit-identical. A fourth profile makes the servicer case explicit: a deterministic two-servicer mix (fixed 0.60/0.40 shares, front-loaded and flat group factors aggregating to one monthly rate) moves the wedge to 9.30 points (\$71.12 billion netted) with the marginal again unchanged, because any servicer-heterogeneous pattern that aggregates to a monthly rate is a special case of the same identity. The Danish legs, which compute curtailment on their own counterfactual balances, scale only to first order, and an exact rerun of the accounting layer across the same range (the simulated CPR paths are bit-identical across scales, since curtailment enters only the accounting layer) puts their differential at $+\$0.76$, $+\$0.77$, and $+\$0.66$ billion at the $0.75\times$, $1.0\times$, and $1.25\times$ points respectively. Those figures are measured on the \emph{Danish-level bracketing} leg, because the committed microsim cache the scaling run consumes was built under that anchor, and they do not carry over to the production leg. Re-scoring the same range on the U.S.-intercept cache (run \texttt{curtailment\_danish\_scaling} with the production anchor) reverses the sign and crosses the materiality threshold: the differential is $-\$1.68$ billion at the unit point and $-\$2.39$ billion at $1.25\times$. The mechanism is the one this paragraph already names, read the other way. Curtailment accrues on retained balance, so the sign follows which leg drains faster: the bracketing Danish leg is \emph{slower} than the U.S.\ leg (3.39\% against 4.76\% mean CPR) and so retains more balance and accrues more curtailment, while the production Danish leg is \emph{faster} (5.61\%) and accrues less. I therefore withdraw the claim that the curtailment differential is immaterial to the Danish comparison. On the production leg it is not: at $-\$1.68$ billion it is 2.7\% of the $+\$61.2$ billion in-sample gap and 6.0\% of the $+\$28.2$ billion off-window gap, small enough not to threaten either sign but large enough that it belongs in the figure rather than in a dismissal.

The second check reweights the sample to the book. The Path A and Path B headline recoveries are balance-weighted estimates on the Freddie sample's composition, whose weighted-average coupon (3.9\%) sits above the SOMA book's (2.49\%). Reweighting the simulated hazards to the book's actual coupon mix shifts Path B from 107.0\% to 109.1\% of the benchmark and Path A from 121.5\% to 127.5\%, a composition effect of roughly two percentage points for Path B that bounds the sample-to-book extrapolation error along the coupon dimension (the full sample-versus-book composition comparison is Appendix~\ref{app:composition}). The agency dimension (Ginnie Mae collateral) has no internal bound; it is externally bounded and now scored directly by a published-series overlay in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, whose Ginnie-specific contribution ($+\$38.5$ billion) lands inside the static bound.

\subsection{Duration Quantification: Approximate Portfolio Weighted-Average Life}\label{sec:robustness-wal}

The mechanism the paper quantifies is duration extension, so I express it in maturity units. Table~\ref{tab:wal} reports the SOMA book's approximate weighted-average life (WAL) under scheduled amortization only, under the empirical CPR path, and under each estimator's implied path, at the QT window's start and end.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small

\midrule

Empirical path (5.14\%) & 9.4 & 8.5 \\
Production ABM (11.68\%) & 6.0 & 5.6 \\
Hazard Path A (3.34\%) & 10.9 & 9.7 \\

\item Single-pool-per-cell approximation: vintage face shares from the SOMA CUSIP tabulation (2021: 43.9\%; 2020: 16.3\%; 2017--19: 6.0\%; 2022: 23.1\%; pre-2017: 10.6\%), a 90.7\%/9.1\% 30-year/15-year term split, coupon at the book's 2.49\% WAC, and each scenario's window-mean CPR held constant. Level-payment amortization with monthly SMM; November 2025 values age the same cells 42 months. Cell WAL formula: for remaining term $n$ months, $\mathrm{WAL} = \sum_{t=1}^{n} t\, p_t / (12 \sum_{t} p_t)$, with $p_t$ month-$t$ principal (scheduled level-payment principal plus $\mathrm{SMM} = 1-(1-\mathrm{CPR})^{1/12}$ of the surviving balance). Worked anchors for the scheduled-only row: a 2.49\% 30-year pool has origination WAL 16.9 years (the 2021-vintage cell, aged 12 months at June 2022, is at 16.3); the 9.1\% 15-year sleeve (origination WAL 8.0 years) and the older vintages (32.9\% of face is 2020 or earlier) pull the book blend to 14.7. Representative cell ages ($\{2022{:}\,2;\ 2021{:}\,12;\ 2020{:}\,32;\ 2017\text{--}19{:}\,60;\ \text{pre-}2017{:}\,120\}$ months at June 2022) are single-pool stand-ins recovered by matching the printed table (a reconstruction of the original computation's assumptions, disclosed as such), and anchored externally: ages derived from the SOMA CUSIP tabulation's own back-derived origination dates (maturity minus term, face-weighted within cells, computed with no reference to this table) run $\{2021{:}\,12;\ 2020{:}\,28;\ 2017\text{--}19{:}\,50\}$ months and give a scheduled-only blend of 14.9 years; naive vintage midpoints likewise give 14.9. The printed 14.7 sits within 0.3 years of both alternatives. Coverage reconciliation: the 90.7\%/9.1\% split (which sums to the 99.8\% covered) is the audit parse of the \$1{,}940.9 billion book, with 0.2\% of face in other terms never modeled; the 90.6\% of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline} is the production run's own as-of-date 30-year-only coverage share; the two parses differ by as-of date and their components round independently.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}
Read against the empirical column, each estimator's CPR error maps into effective-maturity error: the ABM's over-predicted prepayment implies a book roughly 3.4 years too short at window start, Path A's under-prediction a book 1.5 years too long, and Path B lands within 0.3 years. Relative to the no-shock world of the table's final row (prepayments continuing at the 22.81\% mean 2021 realized CPR, implying a 3.4-year WAL) the empirical 9.4-year WAL is a 6.0-year extension: that duration gap is what the paper's dollar benchmark prices month by month. The two Danish rows read the counterfactual in the same maturity units: feeding the Danish legs' mean CPRs through the identical calculator gives a rule-only WAL of 9.1 years at the window's start against the U.S. leg's 9.7 (a 0.6-year rule-only shortening, with the Danish-level bracketing case at 10.8), and the rule-only leg's extension is additionally rate-invariant on the rate-rise side by the identified moving-flatness (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). I deliberately price extension rather than convexity: option-adjusted duration and convexity require a term-structure and prepayment-option model outside this paper's scope, and ``convexity-neutral'' is used strictly in this rate-rise sense.

\subsection{Fifteen-Year MBS Fold-In and Path B Rate-Gap Units}\label{sec:robustness-pipeline}

Two features of the production pipeline set headline figures cited throughout this paper: the treatment of fifteen-year MBS, and the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient. Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} catalogues the figures each supersedes.

The first is the fifteen-year MBS fold-in. The SOMA cohort parser buckets 15-year holdings separately by coupon, never merging 15-year and 30-year buckets, and 15-year scheduled amortization enters both the empirical CPR back-out and the ABM's simulated roll-off. Restricting to 30-year MBS covers 90.6\% of face value, leaving the 15-year book (approximately 9.1\% of holdings) to contribute to total portfolio size but not to cohort-correct amortization or CPR weighting; folding it in brings coverage to 99.8\% of SOMA face value at a SOMA weighted-average coupon of 2.49\% (Table~\ref{tab:wal}'s note reconciles these coverage components with the audit parse's 90.7\%/9.1\% term split).

I tested, but did not adopt, a fully native treatment in which 15-year loans also draw voluntary CPR from a 15-year-specific surface. This produced out-of-bounds predicted values: the ABM's payment-delta gate (the change in \eqref{eq:annuity}'s monthly payment upon refinancing at the prevailing rate, which drives loss-aversion and mobility-desire responses elsewhere in this paper) breaks down for seasoned 15-year borrowers, whose same-term replacement payment is nearly flat relative to their current payment. Native 15-year surfaces predicted 32\%--61\% CPR against an empirical range of roughly 5\%--8\%, enough to flip the ABM's aggregate trapped-liquidity estimate to a large negative value, far outside the empirically observed range. Production therefore uses a structural-only fold-in: 15-year cohorts contribute real weights and real scheduled amortization, while voluntary CPR for those cohorts is drawn, in the frozen headline specification, from the same-coupon 30-year surface. This is a scope limitation for the production headline rather than for the framework: a 15-year-specific behavioral gate (a pure rate-lock penalty for short-amortization loans) is implemented and is the basis of the Berger run's calibration-stage freeze (Table~\ref{tab:danish}, note b); its adoption for the production headline is deferred to the pre-submission freeze.

The second is the units in which Path B applies its rate-gap coefficient $\beta_1$, calibrated from \citet{liebersohn2024} via the survival-function transform described in Section~\ref{sec:pathb} and expressed as a log-hazard-ratio per 100 bp increase in the rate gap. Applied to the gap in decimal rather than per-100 bp units the coefficient is numerically inert, yielding a multiplier of approximately $\times$1.0026 at a representative QT-era state (a 3.0\% coupon against a 6.8\% market rate) against the intended suppression of approximately $\times$0.77. Production applies it as $\exp(100\,\beta_1\,\tilde g_{i,t})$, with $\tilde g_{i,t}$ the decimal form of \eqref{eq:pathB}'s rate gap (equivalently $\exp(\beta_1 g_{i,t})$ in \eqref{eq:pathB}'s 100 bp units; the gap is negative when the borrower is locked in, so \eqref{eq:beta1}'s positive $\beta_1$ suppresses prepayment), consistent with Path A's convention of a positive coefficient applied to the gap in comparable units, and expresses the Danish counterfactual's rate-gap function in rate-equivalent units so that the buyback discount properly offsets the locked-in spread (live in the mechanism-substitution variant; the production Danish leg's prepay response, under either Berger-recalibrated anchor of Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}, does not consume that function's classification, which is nonetheless computed, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb} discloses). Section~\ref{sec:pathb} reports the results under this convention; the superseded \$747 billion (97.7\%) microsimulation is catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}.

Both are structural or mechanical rather than changes in modeling philosophy, and both are additive: disabling either (30-year-only filtering; the decimal-unit $\beta_1$ convention) exactly reproduces the corresponding superseded figures catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}, which I treat as evidence that each is isolated to its intended code paths.

\subsection{Floor and Elasticity-Band Sensitivity: The Calibration Box}\label{sec:robustness-floor}

Path B's dominant level-setter is the involuntary-turnover floor, and Section~\ref{sec:pathb} concedes that its 4\% calibration is measured inside the evaluation window. To quantify what rides on that choice, I swept the floor across seven values from 2\% to 6\% annual CPR jointly with the full Liebersohn--Rothstein elasticity band (5.5\%, 6.5\%, 7.7\%), rerunning the production central and $\beta_1 = 0$ null legs at every floor under the production convention: same committed 75,000-loan sample, same seed and regime tuple, one shared macro frame. Both sweeps were pre-committed: grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds entered the repository before any run executed (commit ordering recorded in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}); the fresh runs at the production point reproduce the committed artifacts exactly (central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion), and the instrumented floor-bind share reproduces the 36\% of 1,683,124 evaluated loan-months quoted in Section~\ref{sec:pathb}.

Table~\ref{tab:floorband} reports the lock-in marginal at every cell of the resulting calibration box. Three facts shape the reading. First, the pre-committed stability threshold ($\pm 2$ points across the empirically plausible 3--5\% floor range) fails: at the central elasticity the marginal spans $+10.8$ to $+5.5$ points across that range. The mechanism is structural rather than statistical: the hazard specification takes a maximum against the floor, so wherever the floor binds the elasticity is inert, and raising the floor mechanically crowds out the lock-in channel. Second, both pre-committed monotonicities hold, the marginal decreasing in the floor within every band row and increasing in the band at every floor, and the box maximum lands at the predicted corner: $+13.2$ points ($+\$100.7$ billion) at a 2\% floor on the 7.7\% band edge. Third, every cell is positive, with a minimum of $+2.1$ points at a 6\% floor on the 5.5\% band edge---a consistency check on the specification, not an identifying result, since the window's rate configuration fixes the sign before any floor or band is chosen (Section~\ref{sec:identification})---and every marginal in the box is basis-invariant because the shared-accounting netting is common to the central and null legs of each cell (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-hybrid}). The verification reading of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} accordingly weakens from a point to a bounded interval: the lock-in elasticity's identified contribution is $+9.2$ points at the production calibration, $+5.0$ to $+12.6$ points across the empirically plausible floor range, and $+2.1$ to $+13.2$ points across the full box, with the off-window re-measurement below placing the headline reading at $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points, while the aggregate level remains floor-dominated throughout.

The box sweeps the elasticity only over its published band, and the band's lower edge is a literature boundary rather than an evidential one: the in-sample estimates cannot reject zero for the elasticity (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), so a reader who weighs that non-rejection over the imported band needs the marginal at values the box never visits. A pre-committed extension (run \texttt{band\_low\_extension}; grid, parity gates against the committed production and off-window cells, and a monotonicity check fixed in the script header before any run) re-runs the paired legs at quarterly-decline values $\delta \in \{0, 1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 3.25, 4.0, 5.0\}\%$ below the band at both anchor floors, and Table~\ref{tab:lowband} reports the resulting curve beside the band's own cells. The curve is monotone at both floors, passes through zero at $\delta = 0$ by construction, and is concave at the off-window floor through the censoring mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:identification}: raising the response crowds the hazard into the floor (the bind share climbs from 35.8\% to 73.5\% along that curve), so each increment of elasticity buys less marginal. At half the adopted central, $\delta = 3.25\%$, the marginal is $+5.1$ points ($+\$39.1$ billion) at the in-sample floor and $+3.5$ points ($+\$26.5$ billion) at the headline off-window floor. This curve prices the dependence on the imported elasticity rather than identifying it: how small the marginal could be, if the moving response is weaker than the literature band, is read off the curve, and the identified content remains the bounded range stated in Section~\ref{sec:identification}.

The box sweeps the floor's level but never its functional form---the choice that censors the elasticity. A form test specified ex ante (spec and interpretive threshold fixed in the script's header before its runs executed, the ex-ante convention Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} records, with the script and its artifact entering the repository in one revision; the max-form pair at the production floor reproduces the committed artifacts exactly, as its parity gate) reran the central and null legs with the hard maximum of \eqref{eq:pathB} replaced by a competing-risks combination on the survival scale, $h = 1 - (1 - \underline{h})(1 - h^{\mathrm{vol}})$, under which involuntary and voluntary hazards add and the elasticity is never inert. The result changes the reading in two ways. The marginal moves to $+11.25$ points at the production floor, a $+2.05$-point shift that exceeds the $\pm 1$-point immateriality threshold fixed ex ante, so the form-dependence is real. But under the additive form the marginal is nearly floor-invariant, spanning only $+11.29$ to $+11.21$ points across the 3--5\% floors whose max-form span ($+10.8$ to $+5.5$) failed the stability threshold above: the failed gate was measuring the censoring mechanics of the max form, not instability in the elasticity's contribution. The aggregate level, by contrast, is strongly form-dependent (the additive central leg recovers 67.2\% at the production floor, versus 107.0\% under the max form, because adding a 4\% involuntary hazard to moderate voluntary hazards raises book-wide prepayment), and the max form remains production because its floor semantics, a minimum total turnover matching the observed 4--5\% on deep-discount cohorts where the voluntary hazard is near zero, is the reading the calibration anchor supports; at that anchor the two forms nearly coincide. A pre-committed completion of this design (run \texttt{floor\_form\_offwindow}) runs the additive form at the off-window anchors themselves: the additive marginal is $+11.22$/$+11.21$/$+11.19$ points at the 4.695/4.991/5.334\% floors, within 0.06 points of its production-floor value, so the entire in-window-to-off-window demotion of the max-form marginal operates through the censoring channel this paragraph diagnoses, and the near-coincidence defense of the max form at the deep-discount anchor does not extend to the shallow-gap off-window anchors. The level cost of that form-robustness carries over to the off-window anchors too, and it is large enough to report beside the hull rather than only at the production floor: at the 4.991\% headline anchor the additive central leg recovers 55.9\% of the benchmark against a 44.7\% null, and at the 4.695\% and 5.334\% anchors 59.3\% and 52.0\% at the central band point, so the additive form undershoots the max form's aggregate level by 43.5 to 45.3 points at every off-window anchor. The hull's upper endpoint is produced by the least accurate of these cells: $+13.10$ points comes from the additive form at the 4.695\% floor and the band's 7.7\% high edge, whose central leg recovers 61.2\% of the benchmark it is scored against. The form-conditional range is therefore a range over specifications that differ sharply in aggregate fit, and its upper end should not be read as an equally credentialed member. That is why the max form remains production. One further run measures the cell the hull leaves implicit. The form and transform dimensions were reported side by side without being crossed, which presumes they add. A pre-committed run of the joint cell (\texttt{concave\_additive\_marginal}) shows they do not. The concave transform under the additive form puts the marginal at $+9.25$ points at the production floor and $+9.22$ at the 4.991\% anchor---inside the hull at both, so the range itself stands---but the interaction is $-1.47$ points at the headline anchor, past the same $\pm 1$-point convention used throughout. The reason is the censoring this paragraph describes: the concave transform costs only $-0.52$ points under the max form, which absorbs most of it into the floor, against $-1.99$ under the additive form, which never censors and so passes the whole transform effect through. Two consequences. A successor run closes that grid (run \texttt{concave\_hull\_band\_ends}): the concave transform's remaining sixteen off-window cells put the form $\times$ transform product at thirty-six of thirty-six, and the hull does not survive intact. Seventeen of the eighteen concave cells fall inside it, but concave under the max form at the highest defensible floor and the band's low edge returns $+3.53$ points, below the $+3.89$ lower edge the log-linear cells set, so the form-conditional hull widens at the bottom to $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points. Its upper edge is unchanged, because the concave transform is signed downward and its highest cell reaches only $+10.83$. Any reading that composes a form adjustment with a transform adjustment additively is unsupported at the calibration this paper headlines. The form-robust statement of the paper's identified quantity is a lock-in marginal of roughly $+9$ to $+11$ points at the production floor, positive under every level, band, and form combination tested.

Those two checks vary the floor's level and its functional form; a third pre-committed check interrogates the level's provenance---whether the 4\% figure is an artifact of being measured in-window on the locked-in cohorts the marginal later decomposes (runs \texttt{out\_of\_window\_floor} and \texttt{oos\_identification}; spec, anchor definitions, acceptance thresholds, and parity gate fixed in each script header before its runs, the ex-ante convention Appendix~\ref{app:ledger} records). Run in-window on the deeply out-of-the-money 2023--24 discount cohorts, the identical exposure-weighted-CPR procedure reproduces the production floor at 3.8--3.9\% annual CPR, flat across seasoning cuts and validating the measurement where it is used. Applied out-of-window to pooled 2017--2019 discount-cohort turnover, before both the QT window and the 2020--21 refinancing wave, it recovers a materially higher 6.1\%. That pooled reading, however, does not survive its own diagnostics, and I do not take it as the operative off-window anchor.

Two contaminations are separable, and separating them is what changes the reading. The first is seasoning. A floor meant to capture involuntary turnover---death, divorce, relocation---should be approximately flat in loan age, but the pooled 2017--2019 out-of-the-money turnover \emph{rises} monotonically with it: 3.68\% below twelve months, 5.78\% at twelve to twenty-four, and 10.99\% at twenty-four to thirty-six. The second is rate regime. Splitting the pooled window at the 2018/2019 turn separates a rising-rate leg (the 30-year rate moved 4.03\% to 4.64\% over 2018, suppressing refinancing incentive) from a falling-rate leg (4.46\% to 3.72\% over 2019, a refinancing wave). The legs read very differently: the 2018 leg returns 4.70--5.33\% across the out-of-the-money gap grid at the age-$\geq$-12 cut, while the 2019 leg returns 6.91\% and carries the entire 10.99\% age-$\geq$-24 cell. The pooled 6.1\% is therefore a refinancing-inflated composite, not an involuntary floor, and the 2019 leg is disqualified on both diagnostics at once. The rate-regime half of that disqualification, as stated, compares the two legs at different discount depths; the matched-depth reconciliation below repairs the comparison and reports what survives it, which is less than the sentence just written implies.

What remains is the 2018 rising-rate leg, and its status must be stated precisely because the paper's off-window anchor now rests on it. Its mature-age seasoning test is \emph{not computable}: the leg contains no out-of-the-money cohort-months at all past the twenty-four-month age cut, because a loan that is out of the money while rates are rising is necessarily a recent, low-coupon origination and therefore young. The 2018 leg is thus clean by rate-regime construction rather than clean by test, and its one computable age contrast (3.78\% to 5.33\% across the twelve-month cut) is confounded by the PSA ramp, on which young loans genuinely turn over less. A second limitation concerns depth rather than age. The gap-$\leq$-two-point cell at which the in-window anchor is measured does exist off-window: the 2018 leg's gap runs to $-2.87$ points, and it holds 35 cohort-months reading 3.440\% annual CPR. Grids that stop at half a point do so because their thresholds were fixed at zero, a quarter point, and half a point in the script header, not because the rate history forbade anything deeper. The true limitation is exposure collapse, and a matched-depth run (\texttt{matched\_depth\_reconciliation}) measures it: down the 2018 leg's depth ladder the share of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure that survives runs 100\%, 83.7\%, 72.9\%, 35.9\%, 11.3\%, 3.9\%, and 0.02\%, so the two-point cell carries \$0.282 billion of \$1{,}264.5 billion. That cell is present and worthless, and an ex-ante support rule---a depth is admissible for a leg only if the leg retains at least 10\% of its gap-$\leq$-0 exposure there---excludes it before any reading is taken. These are the reasons the off-window floor resolves to a \emph{range} rather than a point, and I report it as one.

Re-running the central and $\beta_1 = 0$ legs with the floor set to each defensible off-window value, under the production convention in every other respect (the fresh runs reproduce the committed production artifacts exactly at the 4\% floor: central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion, floor-bind share 36\% of 1,683,124 loan-months), gives Table~\ref{tab:oosfloor}. Across the 2018 leg's defensible band the marginal runs $+6.8$ to $+4.3$ points ($+\$51.8$ to $+\$32.6$ billion), centered at $+5.6$ points ($+\$42.6$ billion) at that leg's mid-grid 4.99\% reading, and $+3.9$ to $+7.5$ points once the full Liebersohn--Rothstein band is swept jointly with it. The contaminated anchors are what produce the small numbers: the pooled 6.07\% read (evaluated at the box's adjacent 6.0\% row) gives $+2.3$ points, and the 2019 leg's 6.91\% read gives $+0.7$. The direction of the in-window concession stands---an off-window floor is higher than the in-window 4\%, and because the marginal decreases in the floor, a higher anchor shrinks the identified contribution rather than reversing it---but the conclusion that the plausible contribution therefore sits at the calibration box's lower edge does not. That lower edge was an artifact of the refinancing-contaminated pooled read; the defensible off-window floor places the contribution in the box's interior, at roughly $+5.6$ points, not at $+2.1$ to $+2.4$.

The move from the in-window anchor to the off-window one changes two things at once, and until now the paper charged both to the window. The in-window anchor is measured at gap $\leq -2$ points and reads 3.972\%; the off-window anchor is measured at gap $\leq -0.25$ points and reads 4.991\%. Mapping both through the committed floor-to-marginal grid (PCHIP on the frozen sweep, not re-derived, and reproducing the committed grid to within \$0.69 billion at every read inside it), that is a move of $-\$28.312$ billion, or $-3.702$ points. Decomposed at matched depth, the move splits: the window component, measured with depth held at gap $\leq -0.25$ points on both sides, is $-\$20.874$ billion ($-2.729$ points, 73.7\% of the move); the depth component, measured within the in-window panel by moving from gap $\leq -2$ points to gap $\leq -0.25$ points, is $-\$7.438$ billion ($-0.973$ points, 26.3\%). Re-anchoring the same decomposition at the deepest depth both panels support (gap $\leq -1$ point) gives 66.9\% window and 33.1\% depth. The window dominates either way, so the demotion is not primarily a depth artifact. But depth owns a real and previously unattributed \$7.4 billion, and the consequence is a correction to how the demotion should be described: at matched depth the in-sample comparison point is $+\$63.5$ billion, not the $+\$70.3$ billion this paper narrates the demotion against. Roughly \$20.9 billion of the \$27.7 billion demotion is the window; the remainder is a change in the discount depth at which the anchor is defined, and has nothing to do with out-of-sampleness. The reverse ordering---window first at matched deep depth, then depth---is not computable: it would have to read the off-window leg at gap $\leq -2$ points, where that leg retains 0.02\% of its exposure, so no average of the two orderings is formed and the depth-first path is reported alone. Extending the 2018 depth ladder from three well-supported depths to five leaves the committed clean band exactly [4.695\%, 5.334\%], because the two additional reads (4.722\% at gap $\leq -0.75$ points, 4.869\% at gap $\leq -1$ point) fall strictly inside it, and the marginal band therefore stays \$33.31 to \$51.75 billion around the \$42.58 billion point (the \$33.31 billion grid-read endpoint and the \$32.6 billion direct-run endpoint quoted earlier differ by the PCHIP grid-read wedge, whose committed maximum is \$0.69 billion). Two further pre-committed measurements discipline this band (run \texttt{floor\_uncertainty}). First, the floor reads themselves carry sampling error the depth cuts do not express: a 1000-replicate stratum-cluster bootstrap puts the clean-band reads' standard errors at 0.39--0.46 points of CPR (31 clusters carry the 2018 leg), and propagating the point anchor's replicates through the committed floor-to-marginal grid gives a percentile 95\% interval of $+3.0$ to $+8.0$ points on the off-window marginal---double the depth-cut band's width, so the binding uncertainty on the headline marginal is the floor's sampling error, not the depth convention (the in-window reads, on 226 clusters, carry standard errors of 0.16--0.19 points). A percentile interval on 31 clusters is itself a lower bound, and a pre-committed correction (run \texttt{floor\_inference\_correction}; CR2/CR3 $t$-intervals and a Rademacher wild-cluster bootstrap-$t$, with the percentile layer reproduced bit-exactly as its parity gate) widens it mostly at the top: the wild-$t$ interval is $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points (CR2 $+3.0$ to $+8.6$, CR3 $+2.8$ to $+8.8$), and it is the wild-$t$ interval this paper quotes as the binding layer, with the lower edge above zero on every corrected read. Second, an age-standardization of the off-window floor---reweighting the 2018 leg's age-bucket reads to the in-window deep-discount population's age mix, with the 2018 leg's empty mature buckets imputed from the in-window age gradient (84.0\% of weight imputed, and labeled as such)---reads 5.51\%, above the clean band's top, implying a marginal near $+3.8$ points: an indicative bound rather than a measurement, but signed one way, so the clean band is open below $+4.3$ and that endpoint is not a conservative floor. This read holds the conventional accounting fixed, and the Ginnie overlay of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} holds the floor at 4.991\%: both correct the same $+5.6$-point cell, both cut the same way, and I have not run them jointly. They do not look like the same adjustment counted twice---one moves the floor's level, the other confines the marginal to the conventional face share, and the overlay's share scaling is identical across the three speed series it was tested on---so carrying that $0.797$ scaling up to the higher floor, which the overlay run does not itself establish, would put the marginal near $+3.0$ points, at the bottom of that sampling interval and below the form-conditional hull's $+3.5$ lower end. I do not report that composition as an estimate. No joint cell exists, and the one joint cell this paper ran for two adjustments to the marginal---the concave transform under the additive form, above---returned an interaction of $-1.47$ points at the headline anchor, which is why a composed point here would not be credible.
The same matched-depth machinery undercuts the argument by which the 2019 leg was rejected above, though not the rejection itself. The rejection compares the 2019 leg's gap-$\leq$-0 read of 6.910\% against the retained 2018 leg's gap-$\leq$-0.25-point read of 4.991\%: a 1.919-point spread taken across two different depths, which is not a valid comparison as written. At matched depth the two legs do not converge either. Over the four depths at which both legs clear the 10\% support rule, the 2018-versus-2019 spread runs 1.576, 0.491, 2.112, and 0.857 points---non-monotone in depth, and at half a point the matched-depth spread exceeds the mismatched 1.919 points the earlier argument rested on. So neither ``the legs disagree by 1.9 points'' nor ``the legs agree once depth is matched'' is a defensible summary; the second would be a cherry-pick of one depth in four. The pooled 2017--2019 read is not a third independent leg, since it contains both 2018 and 2019 (and 2017), so any appeal to three legs agreeing double-counts; there are two. And the 2019 leg's own exposure collapses from \$1{,}085 billion at gap $\leq$ 0 to \$199 billion by half a point and \$0.29 billion by one and a half, so its deep reads are noise on the same grounds that disqualify the deep off-window cell. The honest verdict is therefore that the refinancing-contamination story is unadjudicable below gap $\leq$ 0 rather than refuted there: the only depth carrying solid exposure for both legs is gap $\leq$ 0, where 2019 sits 1.576 points above 2018, directionally consistent with refinancing contamination and consistent with the seasoning diagnostic that independently disqualifies the 2019 leg. The conclusion stands; the argument as earlier stated does not, and I state the weaker one.

A second instrument addresses the months dimension directly rather than the population dimension, and it is a floor-stability check rather than an outcome holdout: no realized prepayment from the held-out months enters either simulated leg, so the construction can falsify only the floor calibration's temporal stability, never the marginal against realized data. Calibrating the floor on deeply out-of-the-money turnover confined to the window's first nineteen months (June 2022 through December 2023) returns 3.97\%, and the central-minus-null marginal can then be summed over the remaining twenty-three months (January 2024 through November 2025) alone, which enter the floor's calibration nowhere. That held-out marginal is $+\$45.1$ billion, against $+\$44.8$ billion at the production floor over the identical months: very nearly the magnitude, and with it the sign, survives evaluation months that the calibration never touches. The decomposition is exact rather than approximate because trapped liquidity is a monthly sum against the redemption target, so the target cancels from the central-minus-null difference and the monthly marginals add back to the aggregate. Across every floor and band combination run here---twenty-seven cells spanning floors from 3.97\% to 6.91\%---the marginal is positive without exception, a consistency check the window's rate configuration cannot let fail (Section~\ref{sec:identification}) rather than a second identifying result. The identified content of the lock-in channel is therefore a magnitude that survives both instruments as a bounded interval: $+4.3$ to $+6.8$ points at the defensible off-window floor under the production max form ($+3.9$ to $+7.5$ jointly with the elasticity band), $+9.6$ to $+13.1$ under the additive form at the same anchors, a form-conditional hull of $+3.5$ to $+13.1$ points, against the $+9.2$ points the in-window production calibration returns, which this paper now reports as its in-sample calibration point rather than its headline.

\caption{Out-of-sample floor anchors and the lock-in marginal each implies, in percentage points of the \$764.7 billion benchmark. Floors are exposure-weighted annualized turnover CPR on out-of-the-money cohort-months measured \emph{outside} the June 2022--November 2025 evaluation window (2017--2019 performance), except the final row, whose floor is calibrated on the window's own first nineteen months. Every row, that one included, reports its floor's \emph{full-window} marginal on a common basis; the held-out-months evaluation is given in the note. Contaminated anchors are reported for completeness and are not this paper's off-window anchor; the pooled anchor's 6.07\% measurement is evaluated at the box's adjacent 6.0\% grid row. Every marginal is basis-invariant and reproduces the committed production artifacts exactly at the 4\% row. Pre-committed run \texttt{oos\_identification}; artifact catalogued in Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}.}
