\subsection{Seasonalizing the Involuntary Floor: Floor Dispersion, and Why No Timing Claim Follows}\label{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}

The three checks above vary the floor's level, its functional form, and its provenance, holding it constant within the window in each case. Two runs in this paper let the floor vary within the window instead: the cyclical variant of Section~\ref{sec:pathb}, which indexes it to the rate path, and the pre-committed seasonal check taken up here, which indexes it to the calendar. Both move the marginal, and this check is the one built to isolate why. It was designed to answer two questions at once: whether letting the involuntary floor vary by calendar month moves the lock-in marginal, and whether the resulting monthly path tracks the empirical benchmark well enough to license a timing claim (run \texttt{seasonal\_floor\_timing}; spec, leg definitions, the placebo families, and a frozen timing rule fixed in the script header before any run executed). The answers are asymmetric, and the second is the more consequential: the marginal does move, but not for the reason the design was built to test, and the timing rule is met without being informative.

The calendar profile is a twelve-cell exposure-weighted annualized turnover CPR measured on deeply out-of-the-money cohort-months. It is estimated on the full cohort-month panel (7{,}756 cohort-months, \$74.2 trillion of exposure, pooled annual CPR 4.187\%), not on the QT window; the window-restricted variant differs in four calendar cells and drives nothing here, and I state the basis because an earlier module docstring described the profile as in-window and was wrong. The profile peaks in June with a peak-to-trough ratio of 2.13. Four legs are run, each as a paired central and $\beta_1 = 0$ null: the production constant 4.0\% floor, a constant 4.5\% floor, a \emph{shape-only} leg carrying the calendar profile with its exposure-weighted mean pinned at 4.0\%, and the pre-committed 4.5\%-mean seasonal leg. The shape-only leg is the one that isolates the calendar, because the pre-committed leg confounds shape with the level move the committed sweep already prices. Every leg re-runs the production microsimulation with only \texttt{INVOLUNTARY\_CPR\_ANNUAL} varied by calendar month; the constant-4.0\% control reproduces the committed production artifacts bitwise (central \$818.530 billion, null \$748.185 billion, marginal $+\$70.345$ billion, $+9.20$ points).

Giving the floor a calendar profile at a held-constant 4.0\% exposure-weighted mean moves the marginal from $+\$70.345$ billion to $+\$65.448$ billion, $+9.20$ to $+8.56$ points: a shape effect of $-\$4.897$ billion. A permutation family disposes of the natural reading of that number. Assigning the \emph{same} twelve floor values to scrambled calendar months, so that the multiset of values is preserved exactly and only the month labels change, retains on average 89.2\% of the shape effect across fourteen draws (69.9\% to 102.8\%), with permuted marginals of \$65.311 to \$66.924 billion against the true \$65.448 billion. Calendar order is very nearly irrelevant to the movement, so the movement is not a calendar effect.

What it is instead is a Jensen effect operating through the floor's combination rule. Production combines the involuntary and voluntary hazards by a hard maximum (\texttt{FLOOR\_MODE = 'max'}, the semantics \eqref{eq:pathB} and the form test above discuss), and at the production floor the floor binds in 36.3\% of evaluated loan-months. Under a maximum, dispersion in the floor is one-sided: in months where the voluntary hazard already exceeds the floor, the low floor cells are truncated away and contribute nothing, while the high cells bind and lift the effective hazard. Any dispersion in the floor therefore raises book-wide prepayment regardless of which month carries which value, which is exactly what the permutation family measures. A convexity-matched control separates the small remaining piece: because the annual-CPR-to-monthly-hazard transform is itself convex, the twelve-cell profile is equivalent in level to a flat 4.0090\% floor rather than a flat 4.0000\% one, and running that flat leg gives $+\$70.156$ billion, a $-\$0.189$ billion move. Level leakage is thus 3.9\% of the shape effect and floor dispersion under the maximum rule is the remaining 96.1\%. I therefore report this as a floor-dispersion and functional-form result. It is not evidence that seasonality moves the marginal.

Level and shape do not separate cleanly, and I do not present an additive split as though they did. Measured against the constant-4.0\% control, the level move contributes $-\$12.704$ billion ($-1.66$ points), the shape move $-\$4.897$ billion ($-0.64$ points), and the two together $-\$16.040$ billion ($-2.10$ points), leaving an interaction residual of $+\$1.560$ billion ($+0.20$ points). On the simulated monthly paths the residual is larger relative to what it qualifies: mean absolute interaction residual 0.164 points of monthly CPR against mean absolute total movement 0.533 points, so the interaction is 30.9\% of the total movement. Any level-plus-shape decomposition of this floor must carry that residual; quoting either component alone misstates both.

What the experiment does buy is a narrow bound. Holding the involuntary floor flat within the window is a modeling convenience, and this run bounds how much of the constant-floor marginal that convenience was responsible for: at most $\$4.897$ billion of the $+\$70.345$ billion in-sample marginal, 7.0\% of it, which is 11.5\% of the $+\$42.6$ billion headline marginal the same absolute bound is measured against, and 96.1\% of even that is the maximum rule's response to dispersion rather than anything about calendars. The identified lock-in marginal is not an artifact of a flat floor. That is the whole of what the seasonal design establishes, and it establishes it about levels only.

\paragraph{The timing concession.} The design's second purpose was to test whether a seasonalized floor earns a monthly-timing claim, under a rule frozen before the runs: Theil $U_2$ on first differences below one \emph{and} a positive detrended lag-0 correlation against the empirical benchmark. That rule is met as written by both seasonal legs (shape-only $U_2 = 0.995$, $r = +0.016$; pre-committed leg $U_2 = 0.990$, $r = +0.070$), and I report it as met, because it was frozen ex ante and I will not reopen a rule after seeing which way it fell. I nonetheless claim nothing from the pass, because five diagnostics computed afterwards show the rule cannot discriminate. First, the benchmark carries it. The SOMA back-out has four exact-zero months (June 2022, February 2023, April 2024, September 2025), three of them immediately followed by a spike of up to 14.01\% annualized; the June 2022 zero opens the window, is followed by an ordinary month, and so contributes one first difference where the others contribute two. Those pairs touch 7 of 41 first differences in total but carry 0.813 of the empirical differenced signal and 0.796 to 0.811 of every leg's $U_2$ numerator. Repairing them by reallocating each spike evenly across its pair, conserving the pair total, flips both seasonal legs from pass to concede and turns the shape-only leg's detrended correlation from $+0.016$ to $-0.134$. Second, a calendar-rotation placebo moves the true profile to the wrong months while holding its amplitude and dispersion exactly; the rule is cleared by 1 of 11 wrong-month rotations for each seasonal leg, and the five-month rotation \emph{beats} the true profile on $U_2$ (0.9934 against 0.9947). Third, 3 of the 14 scrambled calendar orders above clear the rule, and the best of them attains $U_2 = 0.975$, better than either real seasonal leg. Fourth, and decisively, the $\beta_1 = 0$ null clears the rule on both constant-floor legs, the 4.0\% null doing so with $U_2 = 0.9997$ and a detrended correlation of $+0.115$, stronger than either seasonal central leg's. A criterion satisfied by a specification with the lock-in elasticity removed cannot be evidence for the lock-in mechanism. Fifth, the correlations are not distinguishable from zero on 42 months in any case ($p = 0.921$ and $p = 0.661$), and the leg that isolates the mechanism is the fragile one: the shape-only leg's verdict flips on 5 of 42 single-month deletions while the pre-committed leg's flips on none.

Two further diagnostics point the same way without being needed for the verdict. The seasonal legs concentrate 79.7\% (shape-only) and 89.4\% (pre-committed) of their simulated detrended variance in the twelve-month calendar harmonic, while the empirical benchmark carries 0.8\% of its own detrended variance there: the model acquires calendar structure the benchmark can neither confirm nor refute. And on the committed cross-correlation metric the seasonal legs peak at lag $+6$ with a negative correlation, on the boundary of a 42-month window, which I mark non-interpretable rather than read.

The right reading of this is a finding about the criterion, not a failure of the experiment. The frozen rule was a reasonable ex-ante test and it turns out to have no power against the alternatives that matter, because a lumpy benchmark, wrong-month profiles, scrambled calendars, and an elasticity-free null all satisfy it. I therefore restrict every claim in this paper to levels: no estimator here earns a monthly-timing claim, which extends to the seasonalized floor the levels-only concession the production microsimulation's monthly path already carries, where the raw lag-0 correlation does not survive detrending (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). This is a restriction on what the design identifies, not a retraction of any level result, and no aggregate, marginal, or bound reported elsewhere in this paper depends on timing.

\paragraph{Carried caveats.} Four qualifications travel with the seasonal floor wherever it is quoted, and none of them is repaired by the runs above. First, the profile's identification is circular in the same way the in-window floor's is: the deep-out-of-the-money filter that defines it captures 98.2\% of QT exposure, so it is close to the identity map on the panel and the profile is not measured off a lock-in-free population. Second, the clean off-window leg that rescues the floor's \emph{level} cannot rescue its \emph{shape}: the 2017--2019 out-of-the-money cells number 335 cohort-months on \$86.2 billion of exposure and record zero prepaid balance in February and April, so no twelve-cell profile is estimable there at all. Third, the seasonal share is not stable across windows, the calendar terms explaining $R^2 = 0.011$ of the full-panel variation against 0.755 in the QT window, and the profile inherits that instability. Fourth, this involuntary profile peaks in June while the committed \emph{voluntary} seasonality of the estimated cohort-hazard form peaks in March and October. They are different objects and need not agree, but the tension is real and I state it rather than let the two profiles sit unreconciled in the same paper.

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}

The shortfall of the Federal Reserve's mortgage runoff beneath its Quantitative Tightening caps was not solely a product of high interest rates, but a consequence of the structural design of the U.S. mortgage contract interacting with a prepayment environment mostly mechanical relative to the caps: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% of the benchmark at the headline calibration under any household response to rates, and how much of the remainder sits outside household-level decision-making is a question the cross-design test of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} leaves open. This paper's two central empirical findings, established in Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} under the synthetic household population specified in Section~\ref{sec:method}, are complementary and neither is derived from the other, though they are not independent in calibration: both are anchored to an involuntary-turnover floor read off discount-cohort turnover, and at the in-sample calibration point to the same 2023--24 read (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). The ABM (rational, behaviorally extended, or subjected to a direct burnout test) cannot explain the majority of the Federal Reserve's trapped liquidity, while a structurally distinct hazard framework built entirely from loan-level survival dynamics, with no household utility function at all, recovers 91.3\% of the full empirical benchmark on the benchmark-consistent accounting basis in its microsimulation form at the off-window floor this paper headlines, 88.2--93.7\% across that floor's defensible range, and 97.9\% at the in-sample calibration point, so the miss is 8.7 points on the headline calibration and 2.1 points on the in-sample one (a level-accuracy distinction, not a timing one: no estimator's monthly co-movement survives detrending, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; the estimated cohort-hazard form lands at a comparable aggregate level but does not corroborate it---its own coefficient's sign is not statistically distinguishable from zero---carries no valid production-spec uncertainty, and is excluded from headline figures, Section~\ref{sec:patha}). The composition of that recovery, however, disciplines what the contrast can mean: the $\beta_1 = 0$ null recovers 85.7\% on the same basis at the headline floor and 88.7\% in-sample, and the paper's own ablations bound the named loan-level mechanisms (burnout $0.45$ points at the headline floor and $0.86$ in-sample, FICO/LTV covariates $1.3$ points, the delinquency pipeline \$0.39 billion; no servicer channel beyond that pipeline is modeled at all). What delivers the level is scheduled amortization, the seasoning baseline, and the involuntary-turnover floor: the shortfall against the phased caps is mostly mechanical, because the caps sat far above what any plausible prepayment environment would have delivered---though against the Federal Reserve's own ex-ante projection rather than the non-binding cap, the lock-in channel accounts for roughly half the genuine surprise under the central allocation (23--49\% at the off-window floor and 38--80\% at the in-sample calibration point across the two disclosed intra-2022 allocations; Section~\ref{sec:method-benchmark})---and the lock-in elasticity's measured contribution is a bounded margin over that mechanical base rather than a pinned one: $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points once the involuntary floor is measured off the locked-in population it is meant to bound and that floor's own sampling error is propagated with a wild-cluster correction (the percentile read is $+3.0$ to $+8.0$), with $+5.6$ points the value at the mid-grid anchor inside it and every measurable correction to the floor or the accounting basis moving that value down within the interval, and $+9.2$ points at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:identification}; $+11.2$ under the competing-risks floor form). A separate question (whether the estimator contrast reflects the household-choice paradigm itself or the synthetic population it was tested on) receives an asymmetric answer in Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}: the hazard framework's aggregate recovery survives a fully synthetic population, while the ABM's share moves with calibration choice and data source, its recalibrated real-covariate variant reaching 59.3\% (76.3\% at the book's composition), above the pre-committed threshold at which Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} concludes the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm; and the path-level timing question remains open for every estimator.

Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s Danish counterfactual retires a superseded figure. The mechanism-extrapolated specification implied a \$925.5 billion institutional gap; recalibrating against \citepos{berger2026} separately-estimated Danish elasticities collapses it by an order of magnitude, and anchoring the transplant so that it changes only the payoff rule (the anchor consistent with the involuntary-turnover floor's semantics) signs it: $+\$61.2$ billion, 8\% of the benchmark, equal in origin to the lock-in marginal the paper identifies directly, and in size to that marginal at the in-sample calibration point on which both legs were run---though about 44\% larger than the off-window marginal the paper headlines; at the off-window floor itself the rule-only gap is $+\$28.2$ billion, 34\% below that marginal (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). The sign is anchored rather than estimated: pinning the Danish leg at the U.S. zero-gap hazard guarantees it against a book that is out of the money almost everywhere, and the refinance-in-place sweep can only reinforce it, while the bracketing anchor that also imports Danish baseline mobility reverses it to $-\$99.9$ billion---on a Danish leg whose 3.39\% mean prepayment sits below the involuntary floor the U.S. legs impose, which is the inconsistency that demoted it to a bracket. This does not disturb Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard}'s central contrast, which never depended on the Danish comparison, but it leaves the payoff-rule question at a modest magnitude and a stated anchor rather than an unidentifiable sign: a Danish-style payoff rule would have relieved a real but small share of the Federal Reserve's cash-flow problem, not the \$925.5 billion an extrapolated mechanism once implied. One qualification travels with that magnitude, and it is now priced as a bracket rather than left open. The Danish leg credits retired balance at par, so the cash a market-value buyback would \emph{not} return to the Federal Reserve is absent from it; on the paper's own pricing proxy that channel exceeds the gap and moves it toward zero (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Because the pipeline cannot distinguish a cash haircut from a balance adjustment, the pre-committed bracket of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket}) prices both: the gap stands at $+\$61.2$ billion under the balance-adjustment reading and reverses to $-\$89.4$ to $-\$117.7$ billion under the cash-haircut reading at the paper's own proxy discounts, so the honest reading is that the cash-flow relief is real under face accounting only and its sign is set by the incidence, which this design does not establish. The mobility relief such a rule would provide remains the better-supported and larger effect.

A plausible reason the U.S. does not simply adopt the Danish structure is the third leg of the trade-off: the Danish market-value repurchase system is difficult to reconcile with the U.S. fixed-income market as presently structured. The Danish system relies on strict one-to-one match funding (the Balance Principle); the United States relies on the To-Be-Announced (TBA) forward market, which requires highly homogenized, pooled mortgages to generate secondary-market liquidity. \citet{vickery2013} show that this homogeneity produces a measurable liquidity premium: TBA-eligible mortgage pools trade at significantly lower cost than otherwise comparable specified pools ineligible for TBA delivery. \citet{gao2017} measure the same differential directly in transaction data, and \citet{campbell2013} frames the U.S.--Danish contrast as a mortgage-design problem in exactly these terms. A market-value repurchase feature of the kind used in Denmark would introduce path-dependent, borrower-specific payoff values into each mortgage, straining the fungibility on which this premium depends. Adopting the Danish structure without a broader redesign of agency securitization rules would therefore risk sacrificing a documented, and currently uncompensated, source of secondary-market liquidity. A hedging-side literature sharpens what the two payoff rules allocate. In the U.S. structure, prepayment risk is borne and priced by specialized MBS investors rather than by diversified households \citep{gabaix2007}, and the aggregate hedging of the duration swings that prepayment creates feeds back into the level and volatility of long-term rates \citep{malkhozov2016}; the behavioral component of prepayment is the part that cannot be hedged away, since behavioral uncertainty renders the market for the embedded option incomplete and even robust replication strategies can only bound the resulting exposure \citep{perotti2024}. The Danish market-value rule does not remove that behavioral residual (the borrower retains the par call on the rate-decline side), but it removes the rate-rise extension leg on which this cycle's exposure accrued, so the choice between payoff rules is also a choice about which side of the convexity profile the imperfectly hedgeable behavioral risk sits on, and at what feedback cost to the yield curve.

Ultimately, U.S. policymakers weigh three considerations, and this paper's evidence is that they do not form the strict trilemma a three-way ``trade-off'' would imply: (1) deep, homogenized secondary market liquidity (the TBA market); (2) continuous household mobility (avoiding the lock-in effect); and (3) convexity-neutral duration profiles (predictable institutional cash flows). The evidence of Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} is that the U.S. has prioritized (1) at real cost to (2): household mobility is genuinely constrained, while the cash-flow shortfall against the caps is mostly mechanical, with the lock-in channel contributing an identified margin of $+2.8$ to $+8.7$ points at the off-window floor, $+5.6$ at the mid-grid anchor inside it ($+9.2$ at the in-sample calibration point). What I no longer find support for is the assumption that (2) and (3) trade off sharply under face accounting, in which fixing mobility would cost face-value cash flow: under the rule-only transplant, Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}'s recalibrated Danish counterfactual finds a market-value repurchase rule would have relieved (2), while its effect on (3) is incidence-conditional: an $\approx\$61$ billion improvement under face accounting (the figure that images the lock-in marginal at the production floor on which both were run), a $-\$89.4$-to-$-\$117.7$ billion cost under cash accounting at the committed proxy discounts (run \texttt{buyback\_credit\_bracket})---so this leg-(2)/(3) reading inherits the marginal's floor-and-band conditionality (Section~\ref{sec:identification}): its magnitude ranges with the $+2.1$-to-$+13.2$-point calibration sweep, it is stated at the in-sample calibration point rather than the off-window floor the headline marginal now uses, and its (3) sign is robust to the floor but not to the payoff credit's incidence, nor to bundling Danish baseline mobility into the transplant (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}). What this dissolution rests on is thinner than the framing suggests, so I state it plainly. The rule-only Danish gap is the lock-in marginal viewed from the counterfactual side, so ``(2) and (3) do not trade off sharply'' reduces to ``the marginal is small''---and under the production floor form the marginal is bounded small by construction. Because \eqref{eq:pathB} combines the involuntary floor with the voluntary hazard by a hard maximum, switching the elasticity off can raise the hazard by no more than the excess of the $\beta_1 = 0$ hazard over the floor, and by nothing at all where the floor binds on the $\beta_1 = 0$ leg itself, 14.3\% of loan-months at the in-sample calibration point (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The dissolution is therefore a statement about a calibrated ceiling, not an independent finding, and it is form-conditional: under the disclosed additive form, which never censors the elasticity, the same counterfactual roughly doubles to about $+11$ points (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}). What survives across forms is that the trade-off is modest, not that it is absent. The binding constraint against adopting such a rule is therefore not a mobility--cash-flow trade-off but the TBA liquidity premium described above. This is the two-sided exchange the paper opened with: (1) purchased in the currency of (2), with a small side payment in (3) whose sign depends on the buyback's incidence. As long as the U.S. maintains the par-payoff MBS structure within the TBA framework, severe duration extension should be expected to recur in future tightening cycles that begin, as this one did, with a large outstanding stock of deeply in-the-money, below-market-coupon mortgages (the state dependence documented by \citet{berger2021} and \citet{eichenbaum2022} makes the extension conditional on that stock, not on tightening per se). The duration extension itself, however, is mostly the mechanical arithmetic of scheduled amortization and baseline turnover against caps set above any plausible prepayment path; an alternative payoff rule would have trimmed it by the lock-in margin, not eliminated it, and would carry the liquidity cost above.

\subsection{Limitations and Avenues for Future Research}\label{sec:limitations}

Several limitations remain on both sides of the framework.

\textbf{Sample scale and comparability}: The ABM's 10,000-household synthetic population and the hazard framework's 75,000-loan real Freddie Mac sample differ in both size and data source. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale} settles the size question on the production specification (population size changes precision, not central tendency), and the completed cross-design pair of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign} addresses the data-source question asymmetrically: the hazard framework's aggregate recovery is largely data-source-independent, while the ABM's is not.

\textbf{Data:} Freddie Mac 2017--2021 loan-level data (20 origination quarters), used for both hazard paths, is integrated via a dedicated ingest pipeline; where real data files are unavailable, results are validated against a synthetic fixture before real-data estimation. Fifteen-year MBS, approximately 9\% of SOMA face value, remain excluded from cohort modeling on the hazard side only; the ABM side folds them in (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}).

\textbf{Model:} On the ABM side, the monthly CPR path fit remains weak (raw $R^2$ of $-6.984$) despite a directionally sensible level response to rates, and the production Danish counterfactual is a rule-only transplant onto U.S. households (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}): it does not estimate how Danish institutional frictions would themselves migrate, and the Danish-level bracketing anchor shows the gap's sign is not robust to bundling baseline-mobility differences into the transplant. The debt-to-income check remains front-end only, without a measure of total household debt. On the hazard side, Path A's Poisson pseudo-maximum-likelihood estimator requires a mild ridge penalty to remain well-conditioned at 295 stratum fixed effects (a stabilization device: Section~\ref{sec:patha} shows the prior-spec (v3) coefficients equal the unpenalized fit on the estimable strata), and its holdout RMSE (spec v3: approximately 38 percentage points unweighted, 2.5 points exposure-weighted; the frozen spec v4 fit re-scored on the same holdout: 37.9 and 3.0) together with a lag-0 CPR correlation of $-0.378$, more negative even than the ABM's $-0.318$, confines its evidential contribution to the aggregate dollar level, as Section~\ref{sec:patha} details; Path B's literature-calibrated hazard magnitudes, while directionally and quantitatively closer to the empirical benchmark than any ABM specification, are simulated on the 75,000-loan real Freddie Mac sample with elasticities calibrated from secondary literature rather than estimated jointly with the empirical benchmark itself, and its recovery percentage is reported across the calibration band (105.9\%--108.2\%) and now also with loan-level sampling uncertainty from an ex-ante-specified stratified bootstrap over the 75,000-loan draw (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). An external second-agency replication (Fannie Mae loan-level data, same pipeline end-to-end) reproduces the lock-in marginal at $+8.68$ points against Freddie's $+9.20$, inside its pre-committed envelope: a sign-and-envelope replication, not a hazard-level match, and one whose envelope gate is 11.06 points wide, so the 0.52-point agreement, rather than the gate, is what should be read from it (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). One global fact about the design, each of whose components is disclosed only locally: no outcome-holdout months exist anywhere in this paper. The nearest construction is the marginal's temporal holdout (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}), in which the involuntary floor is calibrated on the window's first nineteen months and the central-minus-null difference is then summed over the remaining twenty-three alone, months that enter that calibration nowhere; it returns $+\$45.1$ billion. That construction holds for the marginal, not for the level, and it is an input-stability check rather than an outcome validation: realized prepayment from the held-out months enters neither simulated leg, so it cannot falsify the marginal against data, and the statement above stands without exception. The production involuntary-turnover floor is still calibrated on 2023--24 turnover inside the evaluation window (the pre-committed off-window re-measurement of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} finds the defensible off-window level higher than 4\%, so the direction of any anchoring error shrinks rather than inflates the identified marginal, and the headline marginal is now reported at that off-window level rather than the in-window one); Path A trains on 19 of the 42 QT months and its ridge-selection holdout doubles as its out-of-sample diagnostic; and Path B's \emph{level}, as distinct from its marginal, is still set by a floor measured in the window on which it is evaluated. Every recovery percentage in the paper is accordingly an in-sample or calibrated quantity in the months dimension, disciplined by ex-ante-specified sweeps, nulls, parity gates, and bootstraps rather than by clean holdout months. All headline figures are frozen in tagged run manifests, with reproduction details recorded in the run ledger (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}). The marginal itself, at either floor, is a model-based decomposition conditional on the disclosed hazard structure (Section~\ref{sec:identification}): causal identification in the econometric sense would require exogenous variation in rate gaps across otherwise-comparable loans, which this design does not exploit.

\textbf{Status:} The Danish-regime recalibration (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}) calibrates both frameworks' Danish CPR response against \citepos{berger2026} separately-estimated moving and refinancing elasticities rather than an extrapolated U.S. mechanism, and anchors the rule-only transplant at the U.S. zero-gap intercept, collapsing the institutional gap by an order of magnitude to a signed $+\$61.2$ billion. Path B's full 5.5\%--7.7\% elasticity band is propagated through the microsimulation (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}), an exercise that also surfaced and corrected a units error in the rate-gap coefficient's application (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}). The symmetric companion cross-design test is complete (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-synthesis}): the hazard framework's survival structure recovers 106.0\% of the benchmark on a fully synthetic population, closely matching its real-data recovery, while its path-level timing does not transfer to synthetic data. Four items are bound to the pre-submission freeze as a hard gate: (i) executed: the calendar-month specification of Section~\ref{sec:patha} is production Path A as of this freeze, with Table~\ref{tab:estimators} and the WAL table restated under it, while the coefficient-uncertainty table and the refit-and-resimulate interval remain labeled under the prior spec v3 because bootstrap replications under the seasonal design are branch-unstable (Section~\ref{sec:patha}); (ii) executed for the LaTeX master: it carries no hardcoded cross-references (machine-checked by the replication package's liveness gates), builds clean with zero unresolved references or citations, and the build log ships with the submission bundle; the Word REF-field conversion applies when a Word submission master is cut from this source; (iii) executed: a claims-versus-code liveness audit ran at the freeze (77 production-mechanism claims traced adversarially to their executing code paths; the nine that did not trace as written are corrected in this draft or scoped to their frozen specifications, and the audit artifact ships in the replication package)---the defect class earlier caught in the Danish counterfactual's description; and (iv) the timing-sweep generator's golden-fixture test must pass at the freeze, three matcher generations having each produced a distinct silent defect. What remains open beyond that gate: adding a seasonal multiplier to Path B's baseline hazard (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}); a total-debt measure, or threshold sensitivity, for the ABM's front-end DTI gate (Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}); and the monthly-frequency question the timing diagnosis leaves (the empirical path's variation at that frequency is seasonal, not rate-driven, and no estimator here models it). The second-agency replication is complete (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}; the envelope caveat is in the Model paragraph above). The cyclical-floor variant is likewise complete, with a correction to how its output should be read: a floor co-varying within the run with the window-standardized mortgage rate, nesting the production specification at $\kappa = 0$, moves the marginal between $+7.1$ and $+9.4$ points across $\kappa \in [-0.5, +0.5]$, every cell inside the constant-floor sweep of Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}; but 85.0\% of that span survives scrambling the floor path's time order and 98.0\% of it vanishes under the additive combination rule, so the span measures floor dispersion under the hard maximum rather than cyclicality (Sections~\ref{sec:pathb} and~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}). The co-movement component that survives is real, monotone in $|\kappa|$, and at most \$5.29 billion. The Ginnie composition bound has been upgraded from two published snapshots to a time-varying overlay built from the published monthly CPR series, which leaves the conventional-share marginal at $+7.3$ points, positive under every variant, with the Ginnie-specific level contribution inside the static bound (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Replication alone could not retire the vintage residual: the 2022 and pre-2017 vintages (23.1\% and 10.6\% of book face) lie outside the 2017--2021 universe of both agencies' samples used here. The observed-speed bound of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} now disciplines that extrapolation directly (\$11.7 billion, 1.5\% of benchmark, signed toward overstating trapped liquidity), so what remains open on this dimension is no longer the absence of a bound but the bound's own proxy assumption: observed Fannie segment speeds standing in for the SOMA book's, the same single-agency posture the production calibration itself carries. Appendix~\ref{app:composition} collects the formal composition comparison behind these bounds: sample versus book by agency, vintage, coupon, and term, with a cross-agency stability check on the dimensions the book's CUSIP-level disclosure cannot supply.

Sections~\ref{sec:abm} and~\ref{sec:hazard} establish that contrast under the synthetic household population this paper specifies. Section~\ref{sec:robustness}'s cross-design pair leaves the attribution asymmetric and inherits Section~\ref{sec:robustness-crossdesign}'s own hedge: the hazard framework's recovery survives a fully synthetic population (106.0\% versus 107.0\%), evidence that its survival structure, not its data source, carries the aggregate level, but the recalibrated real-covariate ABM's 59.3\%, and its 60.2\% fifty-seed mean, cross the pre-committed threshold above which the contrast cannot, on that test alone, be cleanly attributed to modeling paradigm rather than to calibration and data source; the path-level timing question remains open for every estimator. That result cuts both ways: recovering 106\% on a fully synthetic population shows the calibration constants, not real loan-level structure, deliver the level, which is consistent with the mechanical decomposition above rather than with a loan-level-mechanisms reading.

\newpage
\appendix

\section{Superseded Figures and Run Ledger}\label{app:ledger}

\paragraph{Data and code availability.} The replication package is the public repository at \url{https://github.com/eugeneoCMU/Lock-in-Effect}. It ships all estimation, simulation, and figure code; the frozen 75{,}000-loan Freddie sample and the cohort-month panel that every production run consumes; every committed results artifact this paper cites (the results JSONs, the production coefficient artifact, the exposure-weighted delinquency-transition matrix); the tagged run manifests with SHA-256 pins on their artifact files; and the machine-checked liveness gates and the claims-versus-code audit artifact. Hazards are estimated on the Freddie Mac Single-Family Loan-Level Dataset, which Freddie Mac makes publicly available at no cost after registration; the raw origination and performance files are not redistributed, and the committed code rebuilds every derived object from them under fixed seeds (engine seed 42 with per-regime offsets; every post-freeze run's specification is fixed ex ante in its script header, the convention this ledger records). Macro and portfolio inputs are the public FRED and New York Fed SOMA interfaces; a user-supplied FRED key is the only credential involved. Fannie Mae inputs are code-only, as Section~\ref{sec:pathb-fannie} states: no Fannie data and no derived cells ship, and the committed replication artifact carries headline statistics alone.

Headline figures in this paper changed across drafts as specification errors were found and corrected. Because some superseded values appear in earlier internal drafts and in the accompanying technical documentation, I catalogue them here rather than in scattered footnotes, so that a reader consulting both always knows which figure superseded which, and why.

\begingroup\sloppy
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{U.S. CPR 12.3\%, empirical CPR 5.45\%, wedge 35.1pp}: produced by a window filter that inadvertently included eight post-QT months; superseded by the bounded active-QT window (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-benchmark}).
\item \textbf{17.6pp wedge; 21--27\% Danish CPR}: a pre-multi-cohort, rational-only calibration; superseded by the multi-vintage production specification.
\item \textbf{\$101.2 billion / 13.2\% ABM estimate} (\texttt{run-2026-07-04}): the pre-fold-in, 30-year-only tag, raw path $R^2 = -6.443$; superseded by the 15-year fold-in production tag \texttt{run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin} (\$91.0 billion / 11.9\%, $R^2 = -6.984$; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}).
\item \textbf{Danish CPR 47.1\%; wedge $\approx$35.5pp; institutional gap \$925.5 billion}: the mechanism-extrapolated Danish specification; the 35.5pp wedge is the difference between its 47.1\% Danish and 11.68\% U.S. mean CPR. Superseded by the Berger-recalibrated \texttt{run-2026-07-05-berger} (Danish CPR $\approx$3.4\%; institutional gap $-\$728.4$ billion against that run's own \$84.5 billion U.S. leg; Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
\item \textbf{``recovers 97.9\%, within 2.1\% of the benchmark'' as an unqualified headline}: the recovery level and the lock-in marginal were quoted at different involuntary-floor calibrations after the marginal was demoted to the off-window floor. Both are retained, each labelled with its floor: 91.3\% recovery and an 8.7-point miss at the headline off-window floor (88.2--93.7\% across its range), 97.9\% and 2.1 points at the in-sample calibration point (\texttt{calibration\_reconciliation}). The related 0.2-point agreement between the null's 88.7\% and the Fed's 88.5\% ex-ante projection share is retracted as a corroborating coincidence: it requires both the in-sample floor and the uniform-spread allocation.

\item \textbf{18.3-point real-book central-minus-null gap}: differenced a standalone-scorer central leg against a shared-basis null. The single-basis gap is 9.2 points in-sample and 5.6 points at the off-window floor.

\item \textbf{Path B \$747 billion / 97.7\%}: the pre-unit-correction microsimulation, in which the calibrated lock-in elasticity was numerically inert; superseded by \$818.5 billion / 107.0\% (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-pipeline}). Its corrected analogue, the deliberate $\beta_1 = 0$ null of Section~\ref{sec:hazard-interp}, recovers 97.8\%.
\item \textbf{Path A \$915.0 billion / 119.7\%} (spec v3; $r = -0.444$ at lag 0, peak $-3$): the prior production specification, which omitted calendar-month seasonality; superseded by the calendar-month spec v4 adopted at the pre-submission freeze on its timing behaviour, the peak moving to $-2$; the recovery difference between the two specifications is not calendar-attributable and no part of the adoption case rests on it (\$928.9 billion / 121.5\%; \texttt{run-2026-07-14-pathA-seasonal}; Section~\ref{sec:patha}).
\item \textbf{Hybrid Danish gap $-\$99.9$ billion, ``sign not robustly identified''} (Danish-level anchor; Danish mean CPR 3.39\%): superseded as the production reading by the U.S.-intercept transplant (\texttt{danish\_us\_intercept}: gap $+\$61.2$ billion, Danish mean CPR 5.61\%, positive at every refinance-in-place sweep point, that positivity being forced by the zero-gap anchor; the U.S. leg reproduces the committed central artifacts exactly). The Danish-level rows are retained in Table~\ref{tab:danish} as a labeled rule-plus-country bracketing case; their Danish mean CPRs sit below the 4\% involuntary floor, the inconsistency that motivated the restatement (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
\item \textbf{Post-freeze robustness artifacts}: the floor functional-form test (\texttt{hazard/floor\_form\_results.json}: additive-form marginal $+11.25$pp at the production floor, $+11.29$ to $+11.21$ across 3--5\% floors; max-form parity pair reproduces the committed artifacts exactly; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor}) and the Path B stratified loan-level bootstrap (\texttt{hazard/bootstrap\_pathb\_results.json}; Section~\ref{sec:pathb}). Neither changes a production headline; both are specified ex ante in their script headers, written before the runs executed. The seasonal-floor run follows the same convention (\texttt{hazard/seasonal\_floor\_timing\_results.json} and the placebo scoring in \texttt{hazard/data/b3\_timing\_scores.json}; Section~\ref{sec:robustness-seasonalfloor}), and carries one correction to its own provenance: an earlier module docstring described the twelve-cell calendar profile as estimated in-window, when it reproduces on the full cohort-month panel. Both bases are recorded in the artifact, the full-panel basis drives every run, and no figure elsewhere in this paper consumed the profile.
\item \textbf{``log-normal mobility-desire heterogeneity'' (scale-sensitivity description)}: the production ABM draws mobility desire from an exponential distribution on a dollar scale; the log-normal description was an error of description, not of code, caught by an adversarial claims-versus-code check and corrected in place (Section~\ref{sec:robustness-scale}).
\end{itemize}
\endgroup

Every current headline figure cites a frozen, tagged run manifest; two freezes ran on uncommitted working trees, so manifest git hashes alone do not name an exact reproduction checkout, and the repository's run ledger records the exact reproduction commits. The one that carries a headline quantity should be named here rather than left to the ledger: the fold-in Monte Carlo behind the ABM's seed distribution (\texttt{run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin}) was frozen about two minutes before its own code was committed, so its manifest's commit field points at the \emph{previous} state of the tree. Its reproduction checkout is recorded in the ledger, and on that run specifications must be identified by cohort-bucket count---seven for the thirty-year-only specification, eleven for the fold-in---rather than by the manifest commit, which is unreliable for exactly this reason. Anyone reproducing the ABM's 13.6\% seed mean or its 11.9\% frozen draw needs that rule, and it is the reason the two figures were once mistakenly attributed to the native-fifteen-year specification.

\paragraph{Reproducibility conventions.} Provenance details referenced from the main text are collected here. The ABM headline run's manifest lives at \path{abm/data/runs/run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin/manifest.json} and is reproduced via \texttt{freeze\_run.py -{}-tag run-2026-07-04-15yr-foldin} against the fold-in specification's code; a manifest's recorded \texttt{git\_commit} is the pre-run parent of the freezing commit (the convention the replication package's technical documentation records). Post-freeze runs follow the spec-before-run convention: grid, parity gates, and interpretive thresholds are fixed in the run script's header and committed before execution (for the floor sweeps, spec commits \texttt{ad52db6} and \texttt{651d1a0} precede result commits \texttt{d0ef130} and \texttt{57181b3}); where script and artifact enter the repository in one revision (the form test \texttt{hazard/floor\_form\_test.py}, the bootstrap), the header is the ex-ante record. Referenced artifact files not named in the main text: \texttt{wal\_anchor\_results.json} (WAL anchor), \texttt{floor\_sweep\_results.json} and \texttt{floor\_band\_cross\_results.json} (the calibration box), \texttt{theil\_data.json} (dynamic-fit decomposition), \texttt{vintage\_residual\_bound\_results.json} (vintage bound), and \texttt{ml\_comparator\_holdout\_results.json} (flexible-learner comparator).

\section{ABM Decision Rule and Parameter Provenance}\label{app:abmspec}
% Sources: abm/abm_lockin_simulation.py ("S:"), abm/fed_mbs_extension_risk.py ("F:"),
% abm/cross_design_test.py, abm/abm_external_gates.py. Line pins refer to the committed code.

This appendix specifies the ABM's move decision and every behavioral parameter at the level needed to reimplement the model without consulting the code; each equation and value is pinned to its source line in the replication package.

\paragraph{The move decision.}
Let $A(P,r,n) = P\,(r/12)\big/\big(1-(1+r/12)^{-n}\big)$ be the monthly payment on principal $P$ at annual rate $r$ over $n$ months. % S:130-136
Household $i$ carries gross income $y_i$ (\$/year), home value $H_i$ (\$), original principal $P_i = 0.8\,H_i$ (a fixed 80\% origination LTV in the synthetic population), note rate $c_i$, original term $n_i$ months, and outstanding balance $K_i$ (closed form at the cohort's seasoning; the U.S. par payoff). % S:141-155, S:314
The replacement-loan payment is $M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} = A(K_i, m_t, n_i)$: the par payoff financed at the market rate $m_t$ over a fresh \emph{full original term}, not the remaining term (term-preference persistence). % S:222-225, S:366-381
With $\Delta_{i,t} = M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(P_i, c_i, n_i)$, whose second term is the current payment (by the amortization identity, equal to the payment on $K_i$ at $c_i$ over the remaining term), the loss-aversion-scaled penalty, in \$/month, is term-dependent:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmpenalty}
\pi_{i,t} =
\begin{cases}
\lambda\,[\Delta_{i,t}]_{+} + [\Delta_{i,t}]_{-} & n_i > 240 \text{ (30-year book)},\\
\lambda\,\big[M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t} - A(K_i, c_i, n_i)\big]_{+} & n_i \le 240 \text{ (native short-term gate)},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
% S:383-409; the 240-month switch is S:408
with $\lambda = 2.25$, $[x]_{+} = \max(x,0)$, $[x]_{-} = \min(x,0)$: payment increases are scaled by $\lambda$; on the 30-year book decreases pass through unscaled as gains, while the short-term gate floors them at zero to isolate the pure rate-lock cost. The frozen production headline draws 15-year voluntary CPR from the same-coupon 30-year surface, so the short-term branch is operative only in the Berger-run calibration stage (Section~\ref{sec:abm-danish}).
The effective transaction rate is $\tau_{i,t} = \mathrm{clip}\big(\phi_t + (\tau_i - 0.07),\,0.02,\,0.12\big)$: the agent's idiosyncratic draw enters as an offset around the 7\% base mean and is re-clipped after the macro friction $\phi_t$ shifts it. % S:241-243, S:459-461
Household $i$ moves in month $t$ iff
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abmmove}
\underbrace{\frac{M^{\mathrm{new}}_{i,t}}{y_i/12} \le 0.43}_{\text{DTI veto}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{D_i > S\,\pi_{i,t} + \tau_{i,t} H_i}_{\text{cost--benefit}}
\;\wedge\;
\underbrace{\neg\big(v_t > 0.015 \,\wedge\, u_i < 0.20\big)}_{\text{wait-and-see freeze}},
\end{equation}
% Gates: DTI S:455-457, cost-benefit S:458-463, freeze S:464-467, conjunction S:468
where $D_i \sim \mathrm{Exponential}(\theta)$ is the mobility-desire draw in dollars, $S = 60$ months is the expected-stay horizon converting the monthly penalty into a dollar stock, $v_t$ is the trailing six-month mortgage-rate change (decimal), and $u_i \sim U[0,1]$ is a fixed per-agent trait (the code's ``patience'' draw, consumed only by the freeze gate; it is not a time-preference parameter). % S:48, S:192, S:302-309
All inequalities are as coded: the DTI gate passes at exactly 43\%, the cost--benefit gate requires strict exceedance, and the freeze binds only strictly above 150 bp. The share of a pool passing \eqref{eq:abmmove} at an evaluation point is read as that point's annualized CPR (the calibration anchor is an annual floor) and consumed by the macro accounting layer at $\mathrm{CPR}/12$ per month. % F:957

\paragraph{Parameter provenance.}
Table~\ref{tab:abmparams} collects every behavioral parameter. None is tuned to the \$764.7 billion benchmark; the mobility-desire scale $\theta$ is the one parameter re-derived under the floor rule, and only ever that one.

\paragraph{Order of operations.}
Within a simulated month the three gates of \eqref{eq:abmmove} are evaluated on the surviving population and combined conjunctively; they are side-effect-free, so their order cannot change the result, and the code evaluates them vectorized in the order DTI, cost--benefit, freeze. % S:449-468
In cohort-path (burnout) mode, movers are removed permanently before the next month's evaluation, so the involuntary tail sets a natural floor on the depleted pool. % S:470-518
Two named components are not in-month gates. The 4--5\% floor enters once, before simulation, through the desire-scale search of Table~\ref{tab:abmparams}, re-run, for that parameter only, whenever a specification change alters the penalty or covariate structure facing every household; because the anchor is evaluated at zero velocity, no freeze parameterization can move it (Section~\ref{sec:method-frictions}). % S:649-685, S:520-524
Curtailment never touches the household decision: it is an additive term in the macro layer's simulated roll-off. In production the rule is also not re-solved monthly: the CPR surface is precomputed on the rate $\times$ friction $\times$ velocity grid with all gates applied at each node and interpolated month-by-month, with the freeze's step-versus-ramp semantics disclosed in Section~\ref{sec:method-abm}. % F:873-895, S:526-548

The correction of this paper's earlier ``log-normal'' description of the mobility-desire distribution---it is exponential, on a dollar scale---is recorded in the run ledger (Appendix~\ref{app:ledger}).

\section{Path B Parameterization and Delinquency Transitions}\label{app:params}

Table~\ref{tab:params} collects every Path B parameter. All values are fixed a priori in the configuration; none is estimated on, or tuned to, the \$764.7 billion benchmark. One parameter is nonetheless measured inside the evaluation window, and benchmark-independence should not be read as window-independence: the 4\% involuntary-turnover floor is anchored to turnover observed on discount cohorts during 2023--2024 (Sections~\ref{sec:method-frictions} and~\ref{sec:pathb}), so the floor (which sets much of the aggregate level, binding in 36\% of loan-months) is in-sample with respect to the QT window even though it never touches the benchmark dollar figure. Section~\ref{sec:robustness-floor} sweeps the floor jointly with the elasticity band: the aggregate level and the lock-in marginal both move materially with the floor, within the bounded interval that section reports; the marginal's sign does not move, but under this window's rate configuration it could not (Section~\ref{sec:identification}). The covariate and default-hazard coefficients are round calibration priors. The two prepayment covariates' signs run opposite the canonical credit-risk direction (higher FICO $\Rightarrow$ better refinancing access $\Rightarrow$ faster prepayment; higher LTV $\Rightarrow$ less equity $\Rightarrow$ slower prepayment): in the deeply out-of-the-money 2022--2025 window the refinancing channel is shut and prepayment is turnover-dominated, so credit covariates proxy mobility rather than refinancing access; high-LTV loans are disproportionately recent purchases that churn. A Path-A-style estimation on the same stratum-month panel (Poisson PML with vintage-by-coupon fixed effects, so the credit buckets are identified from cross-bucket variation; bucket-mean covariates z-scored to the microsim pool's own moments) yields point estimates consistent with the priors' direction (and, taken at face value, roughly double their magnitudes), but with intervals that reject neither zero nor the opposite signs: $\hat{\beta}_F = -0.39$ (stratum-cluster bootstrap 95\% CI $[-1.51, +0.65]$) and $\hat{\beta}_L = +0.21$ ($[-0.59, +1.00]$). The estimation does not settle the sign question; it fails to contradict the priors while quantifying their sensitivity. The sensitivity brackets are correspondingly modest: a $\beta_F = \beta_L = 0$ rerun moves the recovery from 107.0\% to 108.3\%, and the panel-estimated pair moves it to 101.3\%; benchmark-independence is unaffected, since the estimation touches only the Freddie panel. The $\beta_B = 0$ rerun of Section~\ref{sec:pathb} quantifies the burnout channel the same way.

Delinquent loans transition monthly through the matrix of Table~\ref{tab:delinq}, estimated from consecutive stratum-month \emph{modal} servicer states in the Freddie cohort panel, exposure-UPB weighted (committed as an artifact in the replication package). The division of labor with the hazard draws: the default-hazard draw moves loans \emph{into} the pipeline (Current $\to$ D30) and terminates already-delinquent loans it hits, while the matrix governs month-to-month movement \emph{within} the pipeline; the matrix runs after the draw each month, so a loan entering D30 via the draw can also take a matrix transition in its entry month, an overlap bounded with everything else by the \$0.39 billion extreme-matrix test below. Only the delinquency rows are used in simulation, terminal states are absorbing by construction, and the forbearance state is unpopulated in the estimation panel and never entered in production runs.
I publish the full crosstab. D30 row (21 transitions, \$58.3M row exposure): 13 cures carrying 95.6\% of the exposure weight, two D30 continuations (0.8\%), five D60 progressions (3.1\%), and one Defaulted transition (0.4\%); the printed 0.004 is the exposure share of that single event, whose count share would be $1/21 = 0.048$. D60 row (9 transitions, \$3.1M): six cures carrying 56.8\% and three D90+ progressions carrying 43.2\%; the count shares are $6/9$ and $3/9$; the divergence between count and exposure shares means a few larger-balance stratum-months drive the matrix, which is what an exposure-weighted estimator is designed to do. D90+ row (38 transitions, \$76.2M): seven cures (30.3\%), one D30 (0.8\%), thirty self-continuations (68.9\%), and zero Defaulted events; the ``--'' cells are observed zeros in a thin panel, not structural zeros, which is why serious delinquency shows no direct default exit while D30 shows one. Probabilities are printed to three decimals from these exposure shares and rows may not sum to exactly one. The matrix is accordingly reported for completeness, not precision: with cells this thin it describes the estimation panel rather than estimating a transition law, and the paper's estimates rely on the materiality bound below, not on the matrix's point values.

The thinness is immaterial for the paper's estimates: replacing the entire matrix with either extreme (all delinquent loans cure next month; all default next month) moves trapped liquidity by \$0.39 billion (0.05\% of the estimate). The 482 loans delinquent at the window start are routed by the matrix from the first month (mostly cured, given the cure-heavy rows); the month-end delinquency stock thereafter never exceeds 154 of the 40,234 active loans, and delinquent UPB never exceeds 1.1\% of the pool. Both extreme-matrix runs include the initial 482, so the bound covers them.

Attrition accounting: of the 75,000 sampled loans, 34,734 prepaid and 32 defaulted before the QT window opens, so 40,234 are active at the June 2022 start (39,752 current, 482 delinquent). The production U.S. run therefore evaluates 1,683,124 loan-months over the 42-month window, a mean of 40,074 active loans per month. The involuntary-turnover floor $\underline{h}$ binds in 36\% of those loan-months (Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).

The $\delta$-to-$\beta_1$ conversion of \eqref{eq:beta1} is insensitive to the turnover proxy behind it: in the small-$P_q$ limit the transform reduces to $\beta_1 = -\ln(1-\delta) = 0.0672$ at the central $\delta$, and over $P_q \in (0, 0.12]$ (from arbitrarily low turnover to double the adopted proxy) the central $\beta_1$ ranges only from $0.0672$ to $0.0701$, so even a large error in $P_q$ moves the conversion by a few percent.

\section{Path A Estimation Panel, Coefficient Uncertainty, and Ridge Device}\label{app:ridge}

Three facts about the ridge device of Section~\ref{sec:patha} are verified in the replication package, all under spec v3 (the device is unchanged under the spec v4 refit; these verification artifacts were not recomputed at adoption). First, at production magnitudes the penalty is a numerical no-op on the full panel: the two grid points ($\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} \in \{10^{-5}, 10^{-4}\}$) return coefficient vectors identical to machine precision from the IRLS warm start (maximum parameter difference $4.5\times10^{-17}$ in the replication artifact), so the grid selection is inconsequential and the production coefficients are the IRLS solution to machine precision; ``mildly shrunk'' would overstate the penalty's effect, which is nil. Second, ridge on the full coefficient vector with one stratum absorbed as reference is not invariant to that choice: in cold-start refits on resampled panels, swapping the reference stratum can move the standardized rate-gap coefficient by more than its own magnitude (an optimizer-path artifact of penalized refits with the 20 inestimable fixed effects), which is why the bootstrap machinery warm-starts every replication and discards non-converged draws. Third, the production point itself is not an artifact of these choices: dropping the 20 zero-event strata (423 of 10,176 cells) and fitting unpenalized Poisson PML converges, is exactly invariant to the reference choice, and reproduces the spec v3 macro coefficients to within $0.002$ in standardized units (rate gap $+0.6727$ against the spec v3 $+0.6734$ and friction $-0.0373$ against $-0.0371$, third-decimal agreement; burnout $-0.1317$ against $-0.1301$, the larger burnout gap reflecting that dropping the 423 cells changes the burnout regressor's within-stratum demeaning and age-orthogonalization sample).

Table~\ref{tab:panel} reports the estimation panel this fit is built on, and Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} the coefficient uncertainty the two bootstrap schemes of Section~\ref{sec:patha} return. Both sit here rather than in the body because Path A is an aggregate-level corroboration exhibit excluded from every headline range by pre-committed specification, and no cell of either table enters a headline quantity.

\begin{tablenotes}[flushleft]\footnotesize
\item Stratum-cluster scheme: the observed vintage--coupon--FICO--LTV strata are resampled with replacement, each resampled stratum receives a fresh fixed-effect label so duplicated strata contribute independent fixed effects, and per-replication standardization (including the burnout orthogonalization, which is refit inside every replication) is rescaled to production standardized units. This scheme is structurally silent about the friction coefficient: the friction index varies only over time, so every replication sees the identical series, and the stratum-scheme friction row understates that coefficient's uncertainty by construction. Temporal scheme: the 36 training months are resampled in moving blocks of six with each month's cross-section kept intact, same refit and rescaling; it is the informative scheme for friction and, partially, burnout; its friction SE (2.80) is roughly $5.5\times$ the stratum scheme's (0.51). The ridge penalty is held at the production $\lambda_{\mathrm{ridge}} = 10^{-4}$ rather than re-selected per replication (re-selection would bootstrap a different, coefficient-plus-model-selection estimator). Replications returning non-finite or implausible ($|\beta| > 20$) coefficients are counted as failures, not draws; the point refit reproduces the production coefficients to four decimals on the same 10,176 training cells. Coefficients are in production standardized units (training SD of the rate gap: 221 bp). The stratum-scheme burnout median coincides with the point estimate at reported precision only (unrounded: median $-0.1297$, point $-0.1301$). Mean, median, and the implied bias (mean $-$ point) are reported because both schemes' rate-gap distributions are heavily right-skewed; under that skew the percentile and basic 95\% intervals disagree about zero, so interval-based significance is construction-dependent and the text relies on sign stability only; but the bootstrap sign-share is itself percentile logic, and the pre-committed bias-respecting test of $H_0\!: \beta_g \le 0$ (run \texttt{patha\_sign\_test}; BCa with the 296-refit jackknife acceleration this note previously deferred, plus a month-permutation leg) does not reject under any construction ($p = 0.093$/$0.412$/$0.241$), so the 99.5\% positive share is a description of a right-skewed resampling distribution, not a sign test, and no sign claim for this coefficient survives.
\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

\section{Dynamic-Fit Decomposition}\label{app:theil}

The $\beta_1 = 0$ null's cumulative-error profile follows from the committed legs by arithmetic: its terminal cumulative error is $-\$16.6$ billion against the empirical path, versus Path B's $+\$53.8$ billion; the null under-runs the empirical roll-off by approximately the lock-in marginal, by construction, and the full monthly profile is committed with the marginal-timing data of Section~\ref{sec:identification}.

Grouped calibration localizes the same verdict (run \texttt{grouped\_calibration}). Binning the frozen Path A cell predictions into five exposure-weighted prediction-CPR quintiles (edges fit on the training window only) crossed with calendar half-years, the training halves calibrate at a pooled predicted-to-realized ratio of 1.000 (exact by construction of the exposure-offset fit), with per-half ratios spanning 0.80--1.17, while the post-2024 holdout drifts into monotonically worsening under-prediction: 0.64, 0.57, 0.44, 0.43 across 2024H1--2025H2, pooled predicted 2.24 against realized 4.21 points. The departure is temporal drift rather than a reliability-slope failure: pooled across periods the four upper prediction bins calibrate at ratios 0.94--1.11, and the shortfall concentrates in the lowest-prediction bin (43.5\% of exposure), where the 2024--25 cells accumulate: the finer-resolution form of the $U_2 > 1$ diagnosis above. This is a cell-panel CPR object, distinct from the cumulative dollar-runoff split of Section~\ref{sec:patha}, and like every Path A diagnostic it moves no headline figure.

\section{Composition Shift: Estimation Sample versus SOMA Book}\label{app:composition}

Table~\ref{tab:composition} reports a formal covariate-shift analysis between the Freddie Mac data behind the hazard paths and the SOMA book the benchmark spans, on the dimensions both sides carry (run \texttt{composition\_shift}). The book side is the New York Fed's CUSIP-level SOMA holdings parsed by the same production code that builds the ABM cohorts, anchor-gated to the committed marginals (book WAC 2.49\%, Ginnie share 20.4\%, term split, vintage-group shares); the API also served the historical June 29, 2022 as-of, whose composition is consistent with those anchors and is reported in the artifact as a non-gated secondary block. The shifts on coupon, vintage, and agency are large (the expected result, disclosed in prose throughout the paper), and each carries a committed, signed bound as its consequence, listed in the table's final column. Geography, loan size, FICO, and LTV do not exist at the CUSIP level of the Fed's published holdings (par value and security description only); panel (b) supplies the deliverable substitute (the Freddie and Fannie loan-level samples agree to negligible shift on exactly those dimensions), and the hazard specification's exposure to the loan-level covariates is bracketed directly by the printed covariate-block endpoints (99.2\%/92.2\% shared-basis, Section~\ref{sec:pathb}).
